for every closed subset $Y$ containing $x$ and every $F\in\ob\D_c^b(X)$, with $F'=\R\Hom(F,(\mu_n)_X^{\otimes d})$.
\end{statement}

To finish, let us give a strictly local variant of \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:4.6.1]{(4.6.1)}.

\subsection*{4.7}\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:subsection:4.7}
Let $X$ be a noetherian strictly local scheme (SGAA~VIII~4.2), with closed point $x$. Let $Y$ be a non-empty closed subset of $X$, and consider the canonical immersions
\[
U=X-Y\xrightarrow{i}V=X-\{x\}\xrightarrow{j}X.
\]
Finally, let $K_X$ be a dualizing complex normalized at $x$ \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:4.1]{(4.1)}, giving dualizing complexes ``corresponding'' on $U,V,Y,x$ (and by hypothesis $\underline{D}_x=\R\Hom(\ ,A)$):
\[
K_V=j^*K_X,
\qquad K_U=i^*K_V,
\qquad K_Y=\R\Gamma_Y(K_X).
\]
Let $G\in\ob\D_c^b(U)$, and put $G'=\underline{D}_U(G)$. We propose to define a natural pairing
\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:equation:4.7.1}\[
(4.7.1)\qquad \R\Gamma(U,G)\times \R\Gamma(V,i_!G')[-1]\longrightarrow A,
\]
i.e. a functorial homomorphism
\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:equation:4.7.1:2}\[
(4.7.1)'\qquad \R\Gamma(U,G)\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_A\R\Gamma(V,i_!G')[-1]\longrightarrow A.
\]
(Since the ring $A=\Z/\ell\Z$ has infinite cohomological dimension for $\ell\geqslant2$, one knows how to define only the tensor products
\[
\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}:\D^-(A)\times\D^-(A)\longrightarrow\D^-(A)
\]
and
\[
\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}:\D(A)\times\D^b(A)_{\mathrm{torf}}\longrightarrow\D(A)\quad(*).
\]
We therefore leave it to the reader to make suitable hypotheses ensuring that the two factors of $\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}$ appearing in \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:4.7.1:2]{(4.7.1)'} are in $\D^b(A)$, or that one of them has finite tor-dimension.)

First note that, by the complementary duality formula (\hyperref[sga5:I:proposition:1.12]{1.12 a)} (ii), one has a canonical isomorphism
\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:equation:4.7.2}\[
(4.7.2)\qquad i_!(G')\xrightarrow{\sim}\underline{D}_V(\R i_*G).
\]
On the other hand, one has the ordinary duality isomorphism
\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:equation:4.7.3}\[
(4.7.3)\qquad \R\Hom(A_U,G)\xrightarrow{\sim}\R\Hom(A_V,\R i_*G).
\]
By the definition of $\underline{D}_V$ (and independently of the fact that $K_V$ is dualizing), one has a canonical arrow
\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:equation:4.7.4}\[
(4.7.4)\qquad \R i_*G\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}\underline{D}_V(\R i_*G)\longrightarrow K_V.
\]
From \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:4.7.2]{(4.7.2)}, \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:4.7.3]{(4.7.3)}, and \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:4.7.4]{(4.7.4)} one obtains a (canonical) arrow
\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:equation:4.7.5}\[
(4.7.5)\qquad \R\Gamma(U,G)\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_A\R\Gamma(V,i_!G')\longrightarrow \R\Hom(A_V,K_V).
\]
But $K_V=j^!K_X$, and one has the duality isomorphism (SGAA~3.1.10):
\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:equation:4.7.6}\[
(4.7.6)\qquad \R\Hom(A_V,K_V)\xrightarrow{\sim}\R\Hom(A_{V,X},K_X).
\]
Finally, the exact sequence
\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:equation:4.7.7}\[
(4.7.7)\qquad 0\longrightarrow A_{V,X}\longrightarrow A_X\longrightarrow A_x\longrightarrow 0
\]
gives a homomorphism of degree $+1$:
\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:equation:4.7.8}\[
(4.7.8)\qquad \R\Hom(A_{V,X},K_X)\longrightarrow \R\Hom(A_x,K_X)\xrightarrow{\sim}A.
\]
Composing \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:4.7.5]{(4.7.5)}, \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:4.7.6]{(4.7.6)}, and \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:4.7.8]{(4.7.8)}, one obtains the announced arrow \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:4.7.1:2]{(4.7.1)'}.

\emph{(*) (added in 1977) Inexact: in fact it is easy to extend $\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}:\D^-(A)\times\D^-(A)\to\D^-(A)$ to $\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}:\D^-(A)\times\D(A)\to\D(A)$.}

We shall now see that the pairing \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:4.7.1]{(4.7.1)} is ``perfect'', i.e. that the arrow \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:4.7.1:2]{(4.7.1)'} identifies $\R\Gamma(V,i_!G')[-1]$ with $\underline{D}_x\R\Gamma(U,G)$. Indeed suppose, as is allowed, that
\[
G=(ji)^*F,
\]
with $F\in\ob\D_c^b(X)$. Put $F'=\underline{D}_X(F)$ and $F'_{U,X}=(ji)_!G'$. One has the exact sequence
\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:equation:4.7.9}\[
(4.7.9)\qquad 0\longrightarrow F'_{U,X}\longrightarrow F'\longrightarrow F'|Y\longrightarrow0,
\]
which gives rise to a distinguished triangle
\[
\begin{tikzcd}
& \R\Gamma_x(F'_{U,X}) \arrow[dr] \\
\R\Gamma_x(F'|Y) \arrow[ur,"{+1}"] && \R\Gamma_x(F') \arrow[ll]
\end{tikzcd}
\qquad (b)
\]
By definition of $\R\Gamma_x$, one has
\[
\R\Gamma_x(F'_{U,X})=\R\Hom(A_x,F'_{U,X});
\]
hence, using \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:4.7.7]{(4.7.7)} and the fact that $(F'_{U,X})_x=\R\Gamma(X,F'_{U,X})=0$, one has a canonical isomorphism
\[
\R\Hom(A_{V,X},F'_{U,X})[1]\xrightarrow{\sim}\R\Gamma_x(F'_{U,X}).
\]
But one has
\[
\begin{aligned}
\R\Hom(A_{V,X},F'_{U,X})&=\R\Hom(j_!A_V,j_!i_!G')\\
&\xrightarrow{\sim}\R\Hom(A_V,i_!G')\qquad\text{by duality (SGAA~3.1.10)},
\end{aligned}
\]
and hence finally a canonical isomorphism
\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:equation:4.7.10}\[
(4.7.10)\qquad \R\Gamma(V,i_!G')[-1]\xrightarrow{\sim}\R\Gamma_x(F'_{U,X}).
\]
On the other hand one has the exact sequence
\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:equation:4.7.11}\[
(4.7.11)\qquad 0\longrightarrow A_{U,X}\longrightarrow A_X\longrightarrow A_Y\longrightarrow0,
\]
which gives rise to a distinguished triangle
\[
\begin{tikzcd}
& \R\Gamma(U,F|U) \arrow[dl,"{+1}"'] \\
\R\Gamma_Y(F) \arrow[rr] && \R\Gamma_X(F) \arrow[ul]
\end{tikzcd}
\qquad (a)
\]
By virtue of \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:4.7.1]{(4.7.1)} and \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:4.7.10]{(4.7.10)}, one has a natural pairing
\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:equation:4.7.12}\[
(4.7.12)\qquad \R\Gamma(U,F|U)\times\R\Gamma_x(F'_{U,X})\longrightarrow A.
\]
By virtue of \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:4.6.1]{(4.6.1)}, one has a perfect pairing
\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:equation:4.7.13}\[
(4.7.13)\qquad \R\Gamma_Y(F)\times\R\Gamma_x(F'|Y)\longrightarrow A.
\]
Finally, by the ordinary induction formula (\hyperref[sga5:I:proposition:1.12]{1.12 b)} (i)), one has a perfect pairing
\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:equation:4.7.14}\[
(4.7.14)\qquad \R\Gamma(X,F)\times\R\Gamma_x(F')\longrightarrow A.
\]
The reader who has the patience to orient himself in this maze of ``canonical arrows'' will check that the pairings \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:4.7.12]{(4.7.12)}, \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:4.7.13]{(4.7.13)}, and \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:4.7.14]{(4.7.14)} are ``compatible'' with the arrows of the triangles (a) and (b), and consequently that the pairings \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:4.7.12]{(4.7.12)} and \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:4.7.1]{(4.7.1)} are perfect.

The pairing of the triangles (a) and (b) gives rise to two exact sequences ``transpose to one another'':
\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:equation:4.7.15}\[
(4.7.15)\qquad \cdots\longrightarrow \H_Y^i(X,F)\longrightarrow\H^i(X,F)\longrightarrow\H^i(U,F)\longrightarrow\cdots
\]
\[
\cdots\longleftarrow \H_x^{-i}(Y,F'|Y)\longleftarrow\H_x^{-i}(X,F')\longleftarrow\H^{-i-1}(V,F'_{U,V})\longleftarrow\cdots.
\]
In summary, we may state:

\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:proposition:4.7.16}\begin{statement}{Proposition 4.7.16}
Let $X$ be a noetherian strictly local scheme, with closed point $x$. Let $Y$ be a non-empty closed subset of $X$, let $U=X-Y$, $V=X-x$, and let $i:U\to V$ and $j:V\to X$ be the canonical inclusions. Let $K_X$ be a dualizing complex on $X$ normalized at $x$ (hence giving associated dualizing complexes on $V,U,Y$). For $G\in\ob\D_c^b(U)$, one has a perfect pairing:
\[
(*)\qquad \R\Gamma(U,G)\times\R\Gamma(V,i_!G')[-1]\longrightarrow A
\]
(where $G'=\underline{D}_U(G)$). If $G=F|U$, where $F\in\ob\D_c^b(X)$, one has a canonical isomorphism
\[
(**)\qquad \R\Gamma(V,i_!G')[-1]\xrightarrow{\sim}\R\Gamma_x(F'_{U,X})
\]
(where $F'=\underline{D}_X(F)$ and $F'_{U,X}=(ji)_!(F'|U)=(ji)_!(G')$), and a perfect pairing between the distinguished triangles
\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=latex,scale=0.92,baseline=(current bounding box.center)]
\node (aT) at (0,1.2) {$\R\Gamma(U,F|U)$};
\node (aL) at (-1.75,0) {$\R\Gamma_Y(F)$};
\node (aR) at (1.75,0) {$\R\Gamma(X,F)$};
\draw[->] (aL) -- (aR);
\draw[->] (aR) -- (aT);
\draw[->] (aT) -- node[above left] {$+1$} (aL);
\node at (3.2,0.55) {and};
\node (bT) at (5.9,1.2) {$\R\Gamma_x(F'_{U,X})$};
\node (bL) at (4.25,0) {$\R\Gamma_x(F'|Y)$};
\node (bR) at (7.55,0) {$\R\Gamma_x(F')$};
\draw[->] (bT) -- (bR);
\draw[->] (bR) -- (bL);
\draw[->] (bL) -- node[above left] {$+1$} (bT);
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}
compatible with the pairing $(*)$ and the isomorphism $(**)$, and giving rise to two exact sequences transpose to one another \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:4.7.15]{(4.7.15)}.
\end{statement}

\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:remark:4.7.17}\begin{statement}{Remark 4.7.17}
In the particular case where $Y=\{x\}$, the pairing \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:4.7.1]{(4.7.1)} is defined under the sole hypothesis that $K_X$ is normalized at $x$ (and not necessarily dualizing), i.e. such that $\R\Gamma_x(K_X)\simeq A$ (the isomorphism being given): the isomorphism \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:4.7.2]{(4.7.2)} (which used biduality) then reduces to the identity. Since the pairing \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:4.7.14]{(4.7.14)} is in any case perfect, one sees therefore that local duality on $X$ (in the form \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:4.2.2:2]{(4.2.2)'}) is equivalent to the ``global'' duality on $U=X-x$, i.e. to the assertion that the pairing \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:4.7.1]{(4.7.1)}
\[
\R\Gamma(U,G)\times\R\Gamma(U,G')[-1]\longrightarrow A
\]
is perfect, or equivalently that the pairings
\[
\H^i(U,G)\times\H^{-i-1}(U,G')\longrightarrow A
\]
are perfect.

Suppose that one has on $X$ a dualizing complex $K_X$ normalized at $x$. If $X$ is excellent and $U$ is regular of dimension $d$ ($x$ may therefore be an isolated singularity), one should be able to check that $K_U=K_X|U$ is canonically isomorphic to $(\mu_n)_U^{\otimes d}[2d]$ (this is easily checked, at any rate, if $X$ is the strict localization of a prescheme locally of finite type over a field of characteristic~$0$). Then local duality on $X$ implies that, for every locally constant constructible $A_U$-module $G$, one has perfect pairings:
\[
\H^i(U,G)\times\H^{2d-1-i}(U,G^\circ)\longrightarrow A,
\]
where $G^\circ=\uHom(G,(\mu_n)_U^{\otimes d})$, corresponding formally to Poincaré duality on a scheme of dimension $2d-1$.
\end{statement}

\section*{5. Local duality on curves}\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:section:5}

\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:theorem:5.1}\begin{statement}{Theorem 5.1}
Let $X$ be a noetherian regular prescheme of dimension $1$. Suppose that $n$ is prime to the residual characteristics of $X$. Then the purity theorem is true on $X$ \hyperref[sga5:I:statement:3.1.4]{(3.1.4)}, and the complex $A_X$ is dualizing \hyperref[sga5:I:definition:1.7]{(1.7)}.
\end{statement}

\begin{prooftext}[Proof]
Suppose purity has been proved. Since $X$ plainly satisfies condition (reg) \hyperref[sga5:I:statement:3.1.1]{(3.1.1)}, and since condition $(\mathrm{cdloc})_n$ is verified by (SGAA~X~2.2), it follows from \hyperref[sga5:I:proposition:3.2.1]{(3.2.1)} that $\dim.q.inj(A_X)=2$. On the other hand, condition (ii) of \hyperref[sga5:I:definition:1.7]{(1.7)} is verified by (\hyperref[sga5:I:proposition:3.3.1]{3.3.1 b)} (i)) (which was proved assuming purity). Hence, by \hyperref[sga5:I:lemma:4.4]{(4.4)}, we are reduced to proving that $A_X$ is dualizing when $X$ is strictly local. But, to prove purity, which is a local question in a neighborhood of a closed point, one may equally well suppose $X$ strictly local. Finally, we are reduced to proving the theorem when $X$ is strictly local.

Thus suppose that $X$ is strictly local. Let $x$ be its closed point, let $(\pi=\car(k(x)))$, let $\eta$ be its generic point, and let $U=X-x=\Spec(k(\eta))$. Let $G=\Gal(k(\ol{\eta})/k(\eta))$ be the fundamental group of $U$. It is known [CL, Chap.~IV \S~2 Exer.~2] that one has a canonical exact sequence
\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:equation:5.1.1}\[
(5.1.1)\qquad 1\longrightarrow P\longrightarrow G\longrightarrow H\longrightarrow1,
\]
where $H=\prod_{\ell\ne\pi}\Z_\ell(1)$, $\Z_\ell(1)=\varprojlim \mu_{\ell^\nu}$, and $P$ is a pro-$\pi$-group. (Of course, $H$ is isomorphic, but not canonically, to $\prod_{\ell\ne\pi}\Z_\ell$.)

It is also known (SGAA~VIII~2) that the étale cohomology of $U$ is interpreted as the Galois cohomology of $G$, sheaves (respectively constructible sheaves) of $A_U$-modules corresponding to $A$-modules (respectively finite type $A$-modules) on which $G$ acts continuously.

This being so, the $\uH_x^i(A_X)$ are determined by the exact sequence
\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:equation:5.1.2}\[
(5.1.2)\qquad 0\longrightarrow \uH_x^0(A_X)\longrightarrow \H^0(X,A_X)\longrightarrow \H^0(U,A_U)\longrightarrow \uH_x^1(A_X)\longrightarrow0,
\]
and the isomorphisms
\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:equation:5.1.3}\[
(5.1.3)\qquad \uH_x^i(A_X)\xrightarrow{\sim}\H^{i-1}(U,A_U)\qquad\text{for }i\geqslant2.
\]
But trivially $\H^0(X,A_X)=\H^0(U,A_U)=A$, so $\uH_x^0(A_X)=\uH_x^1(A_X)=0$.

On the other hand, for a Galois $G$-$A$-module $M$, one has the Hochschild--Serre spectral sequence
\[
E_2^{pq}=\H^p(H,\H^q(P,M))\Longrightarrow \H^{*}(G,M).
\]
Since $n$ is prime to $\pi$ and $P$ is a pro-$\pi$-group, one has
\[
\H^q(P,M)=0\quad\text{for }q\ne0,
\]
hence $E_2^{pq}=0$ for $q\ne0$, giving a canonical isomorphism
\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:equation:5.1.4}\[
(5.1.4)\qquad \H^p(G,M)\xrightarrow{\sim}\H^p(H,M^P).
\]
By an analogous argument, one sees moreover that $\H^p(H,N)$ is identified with $\H^p(\widehat{\Z}(1),N)$ when the factor $\widehat{\Z}(1)$ is made to act trivially on $N$. Thus, finally, with the preceding convention, one has a canonical isomorphism
\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:equation:5.1.5}\[
(5.1.5)\qquad \H^p(G,M)\xrightarrow{\sim}\H^p(\widehat{\Z}(1),M^P).
\]
Now the Galois cohomology of $\widehat{\Z}$ is well known (CG chap.~I p.~31): for a $G$-$A$-module $N$, one has
\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:equation:5.1.6}\[
(5.1.6)\qquad
\H^i(\widehat{\Z},N)=0\quad\text{for }i\geqslant2,
\qquad
\H^0(\widehat{\Z},N)=N^{\widehat{\Z}},
\qquad
\H^1(\widehat{\Z},N)=N_{\widehat{\Z}}.
\]
From \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:5.1.3]{(5.1.3)}, \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:5.1.5]{(5.1.5)}, and \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:5.1.6]{(5.1.6)} one immediately deduces that
\[
\H^i(U,A_U)=0\quad\text{for }i\geqslant2,
\]
and
\[
\H^1(U,A_U)\xrightarrow{\sim}A.
\]
This last isomorphism is not canonical, since it depends on the choice of an identification of $\widehat{\Z}(1)$ with $\widehat{\Z}$. But, using the Kummer exact sequence, one finds (SGAA~XIX~1.3) a canonical isomorphism (given by the ``local fundamental class'')
\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:equation:5.1.7}\[
(5.1.7)\qquad \H^1(U,\mu_n)\xrightarrow{\sim}A.
\]
Purity is therefore proved.

Let us now prove that $A_X$ is dualizing. By \hyperref[sga5:I:remark:4.7.17]{(4.7.17)}, it is a matter of showing that, for every locally constant constructible $A_U$-module $M$, the pairing
\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:equation:5.1.8}\[
(5.1.8)\qquad \H^i(U,M)\times\H^{1-i}(U,\uHom(M,A_U))\longrightarrow \H^1(U,A_U)\xrightarrow{\sim}(\mu_n)_X^{\otimes -1}
\]
is a perfect duality. We shall interpret this pairing in terms of Galois cohomology. We shall use the following elementary lemma.
\end{prooftext}

\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:lemma:5.1.9}\begin{statement}{Lemma 5.1.9}
Let $D=\Hom(\ ,A)$ denote the Pontrjagin dualizing functor on the category of finite type $A$-modules. Let $M$ be a finite type $A$-module on which a group $G$ acts $A$-linearly.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item One has a canonical isomorphism
\[
D(M^G)\xrightarrow{\sim}(DM)_G.
\]
\item If $G$ is a profinite group acting continuously on $M$, and if $G$ has pro-order prime to $n$, then the canonical arrow
\[
M^G\longrightarrow M_G
\]
is an isomorphism.
\end{enumerate}
\end{statement}

\begin{prooftext}[Proof]
By definition one has
\[
M^G=\varprojlim_{s\in G}\Ker(M\xrightarrow{s-1}M),
\]
hence
\[
D(M^G)\xrightarrow{\sim}\varinjlim_{s\in G}\Coker(DM\xrightarrow{s-1}DM)\xrightarrow{\sim}(DM)_G,
\]
which proves (i).

Let us prove (ii). Let $M'$ be the $A$-module defined by the exact sequence
\[
0\longrightarrow M'\longrightarrow M\longrightarrow M_G\longrightarrow0.
\]
One has $\H^1(G,M')=0$ because $G$ has pro-order prime to $n$. The exact cohomology sequence therefore implies that the arrow $M^G\to M_G$ is an epimorphism. But, applying the functor $D$ and taking (i) into account, one deduces that it is a monomorphism, which completes the proof.
\end{prooftext}

Applying \hyperref[sga5:I:lemma:5.1.9]{(5.1.9)} to $M$ and $P$, one finds that $\uHom(M,A)^P$ is canonically identified with $\uHom(M^P,A)$. If one chooses an isomorphism from $\widehat{\Z}(1)$ to $\widehat{\Z}$, the pairing \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:5.1.8]{(5.1.8)} is written, taking \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:5.1.5]{(5.1.5)} into account, as
\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:equation:5.1.8:2}\[
(5.1.8)'\qquad \H^i(\widehat{\Z},M^P)\times\H^{1-i}(\widehat{\Z},D(M^P))\longrightarrow\H^1(\widehat{\Z},A)\xrightarrow{\sim}A.
\]
It follows immediately from \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:5.1.6]{(5.1.6)} and (\hyperref[sga5:I:lemma:5.1.9]{5.1.9(i)}) that \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:5.1.8:2]{(5.1.8)'} is a perfect pairing. This completes the proof of \hyperref[sga5:I:theorem:5.1]{(5.1)}.

\section*{Appendix}
\begin{center}
by L.~Illusie
\end{center}

The preceding theory extends easily to base Rings more general than the constant Ring $\Z/n\Z$. We shall briefly indicate how the theory is written for locally constant base Rings, killed by $n>0$, and whose geometric fibres are noetherian rings. This is, moreover, only a provisional framework\footnote{According to Grothendieck!}: sooner or later one will have to globalize, by taking as ``coefficients'' on a prescheme $X$ relative preschemes over the site $(X_{\mathrm{\acute et}},\Z/n\Z)$.

\subsection*{1. Constructible sheaves}\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:subsection:1}

\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:paragraph:1.1}\textbf{1.1.} Let $X$ be a prescheme, and let $A_X$ be a sheaf of rings on $X$ (for the étale topology), locally constant, whose geometric fibres are left noetherian rings. We denote by ${}_A X$ the category of left $A_X$-Modules, and by $\D({}_A X)$ (or $\D(X)$ if there is no risk of confusion) the corresponding derived category. The notion of a constructible left $A_X$-Module is defined as in (SGAA IX 2.3), by replacing the expression ``locally constant with value of finite presentation'' by ``of finite presentation as an $A_{U_i}$-Module''.

We denote by $\D_c(X)$ the full subcategory of $\D(X)$ consisting of complexes $E$ such that $\uH^i(E)$ is a constructible $A_X$-Module for every $i$. It is a triangulated subcategory of $\D(X)$.

\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:paragraph:1.2}\textbf{1.2.} Let $E\in\ob\D(X)$. We say (cf. Exp.~II 3.11) that $E$ is pseudo-coherent if, for every $i$, $\uH^i(E)$ is an $A_X$-Module of finite presentation (or locally constant constructible, if one prefers). We denote by $\D(X)_{\mathrm{coh}}$ (or $\D_{\mathrm{coh}}(X)$) the full subcategory of $\D(X)$ consisting of pseudo-coherent objects. It follows from (SGAA IX 2.1) that this is a triangulated subcategory of $\D_c(X)$.

\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:proposition:1.3:2}\textbf{Proposition 1.3} (dévissage lemma). Let $X$ be a quasi-compact and quasi-separated prescheme (respectively a noetherian prescheme), and let $C$ be a strictly full subcategory of the category $\Cons(X)$ of constructible left $A_X$-Modules. Suppose that $C$ is stable under extensions. The following conditions are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item $C=\Cons(X)$;
\item $C$ contains the sheaves of the form $i_!(F)$, where $i:Y\to X$ is a sub-prescheme of finite presentation (respectively an integral sub-prescheme), and $F$ is locally constant constructible on $Y$.
\end{enumerate}
If $A_X$ is constant with value $A$, a left noetherian ring killed by an integer $n>0$, the preceding conditions are equivalent to:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),start=3]
\item $C$ is stable under direct factors and contains the sheaves of the form $f_*i_!(M_U)$, where $i:U\to Y$ is an open immersion (respectively an open subset of an integral prescheme), $f:Y\to X$ is a finite morphism (respectively finite and such that the generic point of $Y$ is separable over its image), and $M_U$ is a left $A_U$-Module constant with value a monogenic $A$-module killed by a prime divisor of $n$.
\end{enumerate}
Finally, if $X$ is noetherian and satisfies condition (reg) (\hyperref[sga5:I:statement:3.1.1]{I 3.1.1}), condition (ii) (respectively (iii)) is equivalent to the analogous condition in which one further assumes $Y$ (respectively $U$) regular.

\emph{Proof.} The equivalence of conditions (i) and (ii) is proved as in the case where $A_X$ is constant; see (SGAA IX 2.4 and 2.5).

Suppose that $A_X$ is constant with value $A$ and killed by $n$, and prove that (iii) implies (ii). Let $E\in\ob\Cons(X)$; we show that $E\in\ob C$. By the equivalence (i)$\Leftrightarrow$(ii), we may suppose that $E=j_!F$, where $j:Z\to X$ is the immersion of a sub-prescheme of finite presentation (respectively an integral one), and $F$ is a constructible locally constant $A_Z$-Module, which we may even suppose, after a little more dévissage, to be trivialized by an étale covering $g:Z'\to Z$ with group $G$. The sheaf $F$ is therefore defined by a finite type $A$-module $M$ on which $G$ acts. If $\ell$ is a prime divisor of $n$, the subgroup of $\ell$-torsion in $M$ is a sub-$G$-$A$-module $M_\ell$ of $M$, and
\[
        M=\bigoplus_\ell M_\ell,
\]
where $\ell$ runs over the set of prime divisors of $n$. Since $C$ is stable under direct factors, we are reduced to the case where $M$ is $\ell$-torsion. Let $H$ be an $\ell$-Sylow subgroup of $G$, and let $h:U=Z'/H\to Z$ be the corresponding intermediate covering. Since the degree of $h$ is prime to $\ell$, the ``trace method'' (SGAA IX 5.1) shows that the adjunction map
\[
        F\longrightarrow h_*h^*(F)
\]
is an isomorphism onto a direct factor; it is therefore enough to prove that $j_!h_*h^*(F)\in\ob C$. By the ``Main theorem'' (EGA IV 8.12.6), there exists a commutative diagram
\[
\begin{tikzcd}
U \arrow[r,"i"] \arrow[d,"h"'] & Y \arrow[d,"f"]\\
Z \arrow[r,"j"'] & X,
\end{tikzcd}
\]
where $i$ is an open immersion and $f$ is finite. We have
\[
        j_!h_*h^*(F) \simeq f_* i_! h^*(F).
\]
But $h^*(F)$ is defined by $M$ considered as an $H$-$A$-module. Thus (iii)$\Rightarrow$(ii) follows from the next lemma.

\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:lemma:1.3.1}\textbf{Lemma 1.3.1.} Let $A$ be a left noetherian ring, $\ell$ a prime number, $H$ a finite $\ell$-group, and $M$ a left $H$-$A$-module of finite type killed by $\ell^\nu$ ($\nu\geq1$). There exists a finite filtration of $M$ by sub-$H$-$A$-modules such that the successive quotients are monogenic $A$-modules killed by $\ell$, and on which $H$ acts trivially.

\emph{Proof.} The exact sequence of $H$-$A$-modules
\[
        0\longrightarrow \ell M\longrightarrow M\longrightarrow M/\ell M\longrightarrow 0
\]
reduces, by induction on $\nu$, to the case where $M$ is killed by $\ell$. We show that then $M$ has a finite filtration by sub-$H$-$A$-modules on whose successive quotients $H$ acts trivially. Arguing by noetherian induction on the set of sub-$H$-$A$-modules of $M$ having the stated property, we are reduced to proving that $M\neq0$ implies $M^H\neq0$. But the kernel of the augmentation $\F_\ell[H]\to\F_\ell$ is plainly nilpotent, and Nakayama's lemma implies that every nonzero $H$-$\F_\ell$-module has a nonzero invariant vector. This proves the assertion. Thus $M$ has a finite filtration whose successive quotients are finite type $A$-modules killed by $\ell$ and on which $H$ acts trivially. By noetherian induction one may even arrange for the successive quotients to be monogenic $A$-modules, completing the proof of \hyperref[sga5:I:lemma:1.3.1]{(1.3.1)}.

The last assertion of \hyperref[sga5:I:proposition:1.3:2]{(1.3)} is immediate and left to the reader.

\subsection*{2. Quasi-injective dimension}\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:subsection:2}

With the notation of \hyperref[sga5:I:paragraph:1.1]{(1.1)}, one defines, as in (\hyperref[sga5:I:definition:1.1]{I 1.1} and \hyperref[sga5:I:definition:1.4]{1.4}), the notions of a quasi-injective $A_X$-Module and of quasi-injective dimension of an object of $\D(X)$. Propositions (\hyperref[sga5:I:proposition:1.3]{I 1.3}, \hyperref[sga5:I:proposition:1.5]{1.5} and \hyperref[sga5:I:proposition:1.6]{1.6}) extend without change.

It is sometimes convenient to introduce a notion of pointwise quasi-injective dimension. Let $x\in X$ and let $K\in\ob\D^+(X)$. We say that $K$ has quasi-injective dimension $\leq N$ at $\bar{x}$ if, for every constructible $A_X$-Module $F$, one has
\[
        \underline{\Ext}^{i}(F,K)_{\bar{x}}=0\qquad\text{for }i>N.
\]
The quasi-injective dimension of $K$ at $\bar{x}$, denoted $\dim.\mathrm{q.inj}_{\bar{x}}(K)$, is the greatest lower bound of the $N$ satisfying the preceding condition. Of course,
\[
        \dim.\mathrm{q.inj}(K)=\sup_{\bar{x}}\dim.\mathrm{q.inj}_{\bar{x}}(K),
\]
where $\bar{x}$ runs through the set of geometric points of $X$.

It is interesting to compare this with the notion of strict topological dimension introduced by Verdier in [1]. We say that $K\in\ob\D^+(X)$ has strict topological dimension $\leq N$ at a geometric point $\bar{x}$ of $X$ if, for every sub-prescheme $Y$ of $X$,
\[
        \underline{\Ext}^{i}(A_Y,K)_{\bar{x}}=\uH^i_Y(K)_{\bar{x}}=0\qquad\text{for }i>N.
\]
The strict topological dimension of $K$ at $\bar{x}$, denoted $\dim.\mathrm{top}_{\bar{x}}(K)$, is the greatest lower bound of the $N$ satisfying the preceding condition. Whereas quasi-injective dimension depends a priori on the base Ring, strict topological dimension depends only on the underlying complex of abelian sheaves.

One plainly has
\[
        \dim.\mathrm{top}_{\bar{x}}(K)\leq \dim.\mathrm{q.inj}_{\bar{x}}(K).
\]
We shall see below examples where equality holds.

\subsection*{3. Dualizing complexes: definition and formal properties}\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:subsection:3}

Keep the preceding notation, but suppose $A_X$ is commutative. The notions of bidualizing pair and dualizing complex are defined as in (\hyperref[sga5:I:definition:1.7]{I 1.7}). The statements (\hyperref[sga5:I:lemma:1.9]{I 1.9}, \hyperref[sga5:I:proposition:1.11]{1.11}, \hyperref[sga5:I:lemma:1.14]{1.14}) remain valid without modification. The statements (\hyperref[sga5:I:proposition:1.12]{I 1.12}, \hyperref[sga5:I:corollary:1.13]{1.13}, \hyperref[sga5:I:corollary:1.15]{1.15}) are also valid, but on condition that $A$ be killed by an integer $n>0$ prime to the residual characteristics of $Y$. Finally, the uniqueness theorem (\hyperref[sga5:I:theorem:2.1]{I 2.1}) is valid provided one assumes $\Spec(A_{\bar{x}})$ connected for every geometric point $\bar{x}$ of $X$.

\subsection*{4. Absolute purity}\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:subsection:4}

Return to the situation of absolute cohomological purity (\hyperref[sga5:I:statement:3.1.4]{I 3.1.4}). Let $X$ be a locally noetherian regular prescheme, let $n>0$ be an integer prime to the residual characteristics of $X$, and let $i:Y\to X$ be a regular sub-prescheme of codimension $d>0$ at every point. To say that the pair $(Y,X)$ satisfies absolute cohomological purity means, recall, that the ``local fundamental class'' homomorphism (SGA $4^{1/2}$ Cycle 2.2)
\begin{equation}\tag{4.1}\label{sga5:I:equation:4.1}
        (\mu_n^{\otimes -d})_Y[-2d]\longrightarrow \R i^!(\Z/n\Z)_X
\end{equation}
is an isomorphism.

Now put on $X$ a sheaf of rings $A_X$ killed by $n$. For the moment set $G=(\Z/n\Z)_X$. Then, for every $F\in\ob\D_A^+(X)$, one can define a canonical arrow in $\D_A^+(X)$:
\begin{equation}\tag{4.2}\label{sga5:I:equation:4.2}
        i^*(F)\otimes^L_{\Z/n\Z} \R i^!(G)\longrightarrow \R i^!\bigl(F\otimes^L_{\Z/n\Z}G\bigr).
\end{equation}
Indeed, by duality (SGAA XVIII 3.1.6), this is equivalent to defining an arrow
\[
        i_!\bigl(i^*(F)\otimes^L_{\Z/n\Z}\R i^!(G)\bigr)\longrightarrow F\otimes^L_{\Z/n\Z}G;
\]
we take this to be the composite of the Künneth arrow (SGAA XVII 5.4.2.2)
\[
        i_!\bigl(i^*(F)\otimes^L_{\Z/n\Z}\R i^!(G)\bigr)\longrightarrow F\otimes^L_{\Z/n\Z}i_!\R i^!(G),
\]
and the arrow deduced from the trace homomorphism (SGAA XVIII 3.1.6)
\[
        F\otimes^L_{\Z/n\Z}i_!\R i^!(G)\longrightarrow F\otimes^L_{\Z/n\Z}G.
\]
Notice that in the definition of \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:4.2]{(4.2)} only the fact that $G$ and $\R i^!(G)$ have finite Tor-dimension over $\Z/n\Z$ was used. Suppose on the other hand that the functor $i^!$ has finite cohomological dimension on the category of $(\Z/n\Z)_X$-Modules: this is the case, for example, if $X$ is of finite type over a field (SGAA XIV 3.1), or more generally if $X$ is excellent of equal characteristic (SGAA XIX 6.1) when resolution of singularities is available, or again if $X$ is of finite type over $\Spec(\Z)$ (SGAA X 6). Then the arrow \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:4.2]{(4.2)} is defined for all $F\in\ob\D({}_A X)$.

Composing \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:4.1]{(4.1)} and \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:4.2]{(4.2)}, one obtains, for $F\in\ob\D_A^+(X)$, a canonical arrow
\begin{equation}\tag{4.3}\label{sga5:I:equation:4.3}
        i^*(F)\otimes^L_{\Z/n\Z}(\mu_n^{\otimes -d})_Y[-2d]\longrightarrow \R i^!(F).
\end{equation}
Assume that $A_X$ is locally constant, and let $F\in\ob\D_A^+(X)$ be such that every $\uH^i(F)$ is a locally constant $A_X$-Module. Then, if $i^!$ has finite cohomological dimension, the arrow \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:4.2]{(4.2)}, and hence the arrow \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:4.3]{(4.3)} if $(Y,X)$ satisfies purity, is an isomorphism.

Indeed, by dévissage, one first reduces to the case where $F$ is concentrated in degree zero. Since the question is local, one may suppose $X$ affine and $A_X$ and $F$ constant. Resolving $F$ on the left by free $A_X$-Modules and applying the hypothesis that $i^!$ has finite cohomological dimension, one reduces to the case where $F$ is free. By an immediate passage to the limit, one reduces to $F$ free of finite type, and finally to $F=A_X$. It is therefore enough to prove the assertion when $A_X=(\Z/n\Z)_X$. But the preceding dévissage reduces to $F=(\Z/n\Z)_X$, and in that case \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:4.2]{(4.2)} is the identity.

\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:definition:4.4}\textbf{Definition 4.4.} Given the Ring $A_X$ (locally constant and killed by $n$), we shall say that the pair $(Y,X)$ satisfies cohomological purity in the strong sense if, for every $F\in\ob\D_A^+(X)$ such that the $\uH^i(F)$ are locally constant, the canonical arrow (\hyperref[sga5:I:equation:4.3]{4.3}) is an isomorphism.

We shall say that the purity theorem is true in the strong sense on $X$ if, locally for the étale topology, every pair $(Y,X)$ as above satisfies purity in the strong sense. From what has been shown, for $(Y,X)$ to satisfy purity in the strong sense it is enough that $(Y,X)$ satisfy ordinary purity (\hyperref[sga5:I:subsection:3.1]{I 3.1}.) and that the functor $i^!$ have finite cohomological dimension.

\subsection*{5. Bound for quasi-injective dimension}\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:subsection:5}

\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:proposition:5.1}\textbf{Proposition 5.1.} Let $X$ be a locally noetherian regular prescheme, equipped with a locally constant sheaf of rings $A_X$, killed by an integer $n>0$ prime to the residual characteristics of $X$, whose geometric fibres are left noetherian rings. Suppose that $X$ satisfies conditions (reg) (\hyperref[sga5:I:statement:3.1.1]{I 3.1.1}) and $(\mathrm{cdloc})_n$ (\hyperref[sga5:I:statement:3.1.3]{I 3.1.3}), and that the purity theorem in the strong sense is true on $X$ (\hyperref[sga5:I:definition:4.4]{4.4}) (conditions all satisfied if $X$ is smooth over a perfect field, or if $X$ is excellent of characteristic zero).

Let $F\in\ob\D_A^+(X)$ be such that the $\uH^i(F)$ are locally constant, and let $x$ be a point of $X$ such that $F_{\bar{x}}\neq0$ and $\dim.\mathrm{inj}_{A_{\bar{x}}}(F_{\bar{x}})<+\infty$. Then
\[
        \dim.\mathrm{q.inj}_{\bar{x}}(F)=2\dim(\mathcal{O}_{X,x})+\dim.\mathrm{inj}(F_{\bar{x}}),
\]
with the notation of no.~2.

\emph{Proof.} One merely repeats step by step the proof of (\hyperref[sga5:I:proposition:3.2.1]{I 3.2.1}). First show the inequality
\[
        \dim.\mathrm{q.inj}_{\bar{x}}(F)\leq 2\dim(\mathcal{O}_{X,x})+\dim.\mathrm{inj}(F_{\bar{x}})=N,
\]
that is, the relation
\begin{equation}\tag{*}\label{sga5:I:equation:unnumbered:6}
        \underline{\Ext}^{i}(E,F)_{\bar{x}}=0\quad\text{for } i>N\text{ and every constructible }A_X\text{-Module }E.
\end{equation}
Since the question is local, by dévissage \hyperref[sga5:I:proposition:1.3:2]{(1.3)} one may restrict to checking $(*)$ for $E=i_!(M)$, where $i:Y\to X$ is an integral regular sub-prescheme closed in an open $D(f)$ (with $f\in\Gamma(X,\mathcal{O}_X)$), of codimension $d$ at every point of $D(f)$, and $M$ is a locally constant constructible $A_Y$-Module ($A_Y=i^*A_X$).

Now there is the duality isomorphism (SGAA XVIII)
\[
        \R\underline{\Hom}(i_!(M),F)\simeq \R i_*\R\underline{\Hom}(M,\R i^!F).
\]
By the purity theorem, there is also the isomorphism \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:4.3]{(4.3)}
\[
        i^*(F)\otimes^L_{\Z/n\Z}(\mu_n^{\otimes -d})_Y[-2d]\xrightarrow{\sim}\R i^!(F).
\]
Let $\bar{y}$ be a geometric point of $Y$. Since $M$ is locally constant constructible, one has canonically
\[
        \R\underline{\Hom}(M,\R i^!F)_{\bar{y}}\simeq \R\underline{\Hom}(M_{\bar{y}},(\R i^!F)_{\bar{y}}),
\]
hence, by \hyperref[sga5:I:equation:4.3]{(4.3)},
\[
        \R\underline{\Hom}(M,\R i^!F)_{\bar{y}}\simeq \R\underline{\Hom}(M_{\bar{y}},F_{\bar{y}})[-2d].
\]
After replacing $X$ by a connected neighbourhood of $x$, one has $F_{\bar{x}}\simeq F_{\bar{y}}$ and $A_{\bar{x}}\simeq A_{\bar{y}}$, since $A_X$ and the $\uH^i(F)$ are locally constant. Thus
\[
        \underline{\Ext}^i(M_{\bar{y}},F_{\bar{y}})=0\quad\text{for }i>\dim.\mathrm{inj}(F_{\bar{x}}),
\]
and hence
\[
        \underline{\Ext}^{q}(M,\R i^!F)=0\quad\text{for }q>2d+\dim.\mathrm{inj}(F_{\bar{x}}).
\]
Set $G=\R\underline{\Hom}(M,\R i^!F)$ and compute $\R i_*(G)_{\bar{x}}$. Introduce, as in (\hyperref[sga5:I:proposition:3.2.1]{I 3.2.1}), the schematic closure $Z$ of $Y$ in $X$, giving a cartesian diagram of immersions
\[
\begin{tikzcd}
Y \arrow[r] \arrow[d,"j"'] & D(f) \arrow[d]\\
Z \arrow[r,"k"'] & X.
\end{tikzcd}
\]
Then $\R i_*(G)\simeq k_*\R j_*(G)$. Assume of course that $x\in Z$, since otherwise $\R i_*(G)=0$. Let $\bar{Z}=\Spec(\mathcal{O}_{Z,\bar{x}})$ be the strict localization of $Z$ at $\bar{x}$, let $\bar{f}$ be the image of $f$ in $\mathcal{O}_{Z,\bar{x}}$, let $\bar{Y}=\bar{Z}\times_ZY=\bar{Z}\cap D(\bar{f})$, and let $\bar{G}$ be the inverse image of $G$ on $\bar{Y}$. By (SGAA VIII 5.2),
\[
        \R i_*(G)_{\bar{x}}=\R j_*(G)_{\bar{x}}\simeq \R\Gamma(\bar{Y},\bar{G}).
\]
Condition $(\mathrm{cdloc})_n$ therefore implies, by a spectral sequence argument, that
\[
        R^p i_*(G)_{\bar{x}}=0\quad\text{for }p>2d+\dim.\mathrm{inj}(F_{\bar{x}})+\dim(\mathcal{O}_{Z,x}).
\]
But (cf. the proof of \hyperref[sga5:I:proposition:3.2.1]{I 3.2.1})
\[
        \dim(\mathcal{O}_{Z,x})=\dim(\mathcal{O}_{X,x})-d\geq0,
\]
so
\[
        R^p i_*(G)_{\bar{x}}=0\quad\text{for }p>d+\dim(\mathcal{O}_{X,x})+\dim.\mathrm{inj}(F_{\bar{x}}),
\]
and a fortiori for $p>2\dim(\mathcal{O}_{X,x})+\dim.\mathrm{inj}(F_{\bar{x}})$. This proves $(*)$.

It remains to prove that the bound $(*)$ is the best possible. Put $f=\dim.\mathrm{inj}(F_{\bar{x}})$, and choose a finite type $A_{\bar{x}}$-Module $M'$ such that
\[
        \underline{\Ext}^f(M',F_{\bar{x}})\neq0.
\]
Let $Z$ be the integral closed sub-prescheme of $X$ which is the closure of $x$, and let $Y=U\cap Z$ be a regular open subset of $Z$ containing $x$ and of codimension $d=\dim(\mathcal{O}_{X,x})$ at every point. After possibly shrinking $Y$, there exists a locally constant constructible $A_Y$-Module $M$ such that $M_{\bar{x}}=M'$. Applying the purity theorem, one finds
\[
        \underline{\Ext}^{2d+f}(M,F|U)_{\bar{x}}\simeq \underline{\Ext}^f(M',F_{\bar{x}})\neq0,
\]
which completes the proof.

If $F$ is an $A_X$-Module such that $F_{\bar{x}}$ is injective, then in the last part of the proof one may take $M=A_Y$; hence:

\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:corollary:5.1.1}\textbf{Corollary 5.1.1.} Under the hypotheses of (\hyperref[sga5:I:proposition:5.1]{5.1}), suppose that $F$ is a locally constant $A_X$-Module such that $F_{\bar{x}}$ is injective. Then
\[
        \dim.\mathrm{q.inj}_{\bar{x}}(F)=\dim.\mathrm{top}_{\bar{x}}(F)=2\dim(\mathcal{O}_{X,x}),
\]
with the notation of no.~2.

\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:remark:5.1.2}\textbf{Remarks 5.1.2.} a) With the notation of (\hyperref[sga5:I:proposition:5.1]{5.1}), if $F_{\bar{x}}=0$, then $F=0$ in a neighbourhood of $x$, hence $\R\underline{\Hom}(E,F)_{\bar{x}}=0$ for all $E\in\ob\D^{-}(X)$.

b) If $\dim.\mathrm{inj}(F_{\bar{x}})=+\infty$, then $\dim.\mathrm{q.inj}_{\bar{x}}(F)=+\infty$; indeed, for every $p$ there exists a finite type $A_{\bar{x}}$-module $M'$ such that $\underline{\Ext}^p(M',F_{\bar{x}})\neq0$, hence a constructible $A_X$-Module $M$, locally constant in a neighbourhood of $x$, such that $\underline{\Ext}^p(M,F)_{\bar{x}}\neq0$.

\subsection*{6. Constructibility of $\R\underline{\Hom}$}\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:subsection:6}

The equivalence of conditions a) of (\hyperref[sga5:I:proposition:3.3.1]{I 3.3.1}) remains valid when the Ring $(\Z/n\Z)_X$ is replaced by a commutative Ring, locally constant, whose geometric fibres are noetherian rings: nothing has to be changed in the proof.

The preceding conditions are verified when one has resolution of singularities and purity in the strong sense \hyperref[sga5:I:definition:4.4]{(4.4)}, in particular if $X$ has dimension $\leq1$, or if $X$ is excellent of characteristic zero, or locally of finite type over a field and of dimension $\leq2$.

\subsection*{7. Existence of dualizing complexes}\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:subsection:7}

In this number, $X$ denotes a prescheme equipped with a commutative sheaf of rings $A_X$, locally constant, whose geometric fibres are noetherian rings.

\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:lemma:7.1}\textbf{Lemma 7.1.} If $F\in\ob\D^{-}_{\mathrm{coh}}(X)$ and $G\in\ob\D^{+}_{\mathrm{coh}}(X)$, then $\R\underline{\Hom}(F,G)\in\ob\D^{+}_{\mathrm{coh}}(X)$ (notation of (\hyperref[sga5:I:paragraph:1.2]{1.2})).

\emph{Proof.} This is standard. One reduces, by a spectral sequence, to $F$ and $G$ concentrated in degree zero, and then argues as in (EGA $0_{\mathrm{III}}$ 12.3.3).

\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:lemma:7.2}\textbf{Lemma 7.2.} Let $g:X'\to X$ be a morphism of preschemes, and endow $X'$ with the sheaf of rings $A_{X'}=g^*(A_X)$. Then, for $F\in\ob\D^{-}_{\mathrm{coh}}(X)$ and $G\in\ob\D^{+}(X)$, the canonical map
\[
        g^*\R\underline{\Hom}(F,G)\longrightarrow \R\underline{\Hom}(g^*F,g^*G)
\]
is an isomorphism.

\emph{Proof.} This too is standard: one reduces by dévissage to $F=A_X$ (cf. EGA $0_{\mathrm{III}}$ 12.3.5).

\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:remark:7.2.1}\textbf{Remark 7.2.1.} The preceding lemmas are special cases of more general statements appearing in (SGA 6 I).

\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:lemma:7.3}\textbf{Lemma 7.3.} Suppose $X$ is quasi-compact, and let $K\in\ob\D^{+}_{\mathrm{coh}}(X)$ be such that, for every geometric point $\bar{x}$ of $X$, $K_{\bar{x}}$ is a dualizing complex in the sense of [H] V 2.1. Then, for every $F\in\ob\D^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}(X)$, one has $\R\underline{\Hom}(F,K)\in\ob\D^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}(X)$, and $F$ is reflexive relative to $K$.

\emph{Proof.} It suffices, of course, to check this for $F$ concentrated in degree zero. By \hyperref[sga5:I:lemma:7.1]{(7.1)}, $\R\underline{\Hom}(F,K)\in\ob\D^{+}_{\mathrm{coh}}(X)$. To prove boundedness, let $\bar{x}$ be a geometric point of $X$. By \hyperref[sga5:I:lemma:7.2]{(7.2)},
\[
        \R\underline{\Hom}(F,K)_{\bar{x}}\simeq \R\underline{\Hom}(F_{\bar{x}},K_{\bar{x}}).
\]
Putting $\dim.\mathrm{inj}(K_{\bar{x}})=N(\bar{x})$, one obtains
\[
        \underline{\Ext}^i(F,K)_{\bar{x}}=0\quad\text{for }i>N(\bar{x}).
\]
Since the sheaves $\underline{\Ext}^{i}(F,K)$ are locally constant, there is an open neighbourhood $U$ of $x$ such that $\underline{\Ext}^{i}(F,K)|U=0$ for $i>N(\bar{x})$, and quasi-compactness of $X$ gives the assertion. It remains to check that the canonical arrow
\[
        F\longrightarrow \R\underline{\Hom}(\R\underline{\Hom}(F,K),K)
\]
is an isomorphism. It is enough to evaluate at a geometric point $\bar{x}$ of $X$. In the commutative diagram
\[
\begin{tikzcd}
F_{\bar{x}} \arrow[r] \arrow[d,"\mathrm{Id}"'] & \R\underline{\Hom}(\R\underline{\Hom}(F,K),K)_{\bar{x}} \arrow[d]\\
F_{\bar{x}} \arrow[r] & \R\underline{\Hom}(\R\underline{\Hom}(F_{\bar{x}},K_{\bar{x}}),K_{\bar{x}}),
\end{tikzcd}
\]
the right vertical arrow is an isomorphism by \hyperref[sga5:I:lemma:7.2]{(7.2)}, and the bottom horizontal arrow is an isomorphism because $K_{\bar{x}}$ is dualizing; hence the top horizontal arrow is an isomorphism, q.e.d.

\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:lemma:7.4}\textbf{Lemma 7.4.} Suppose $X$ is a noetherian regular prescheme, $A_X$ is killed by an integer $n>0$ prime to the residual characteristics of $X$, and the purity theorem in the strong sense (\hyperref[sga5:I:definition:4.4]{4.4}) is true on $X$.

Let $D$ be a divisor with normal crossings on $X$ (\hyperref[sga5:I:statement:3.1.5]{I 3.1.5 a)}), and denote by $j:Y=V(D)\to X$ and $i:X-Y=U\to X$ the canonical inclusions. Let $K\in\ob\D^{+}_{\mathrm{coh}}(X)$ be such that, for every geometric point $\bar{x}$ of $X$, $K_{\bar{x}}$ is a dualizing complex for the ring $A_{\bar{x}}$ (in the sense of [H] V 2.1). Then, for every $E\in\ob\D^{b}_{\mathrm{coh}}(X)$, one has $\R\underline{\Hom}(i_!i^*(E),K)\in\ob\D^{b}_{c}(X)$, and the pair $(i_!i^*(E),K)$ is bidualizing.

\emph{Proof.} We may suppose $E$ is concentrated in degree zero, i.e. a locally constant constructible $A_X$-Module. Since the question is local on $X$ for the étale topology, we may suppose that (a) $A_X$ is constant with value $A$, and $E$ is constant with value $E$; and (b) $D$ has strict normal crossings, say $D=\sum_{i\in I}\mathrm{div}(s_i)$ with $s_i\in\Gamma(X,\mathcal{O}_X)$.

Argue by induction on $p=\operatorname{card}(I)$. For $p=0$, the assertions follow from \hyperref[sga5:I:lemma:7.3]{(7.3)}. Suppose $I=\{1,\ldots,p\}$, put $Y_i=V(s_i)$, $Y'=V(\sum_{1\leq i\leq p-1}\mathrm{div}(s_i))$, and $U'=X-Y'$. We get a cartesian diagram of immersions
\[
\begin{tikzcd}
\varnothing \arrow[r] \arrow[d] & U'\cap Y_p \arrow[r,"i'_p"] \arrow[d,"j'_p"'] & Y_p \arrow[d,"j_p"]\\
U \arrow[r] & U' \arrow[r,"i'"'] & X.
\end{tikzcd}
\]
Consider the exact sequence
\[
        0\longrightarrow E_{U,X}\longrightarrow E_{U',X}\longrightarrow E_{U'\cap Y_p,X}\longrightarrow0.
\]
By the induction hypothesis the theorem is true for $(E_{U',X},K)$. For fixed $K$, the $F$ such that $\R\underline{\Hom}(F,K)\in\ob\D_c^b(X)$ and $(F,K)$ is bidualizing form a triangulated subcategory of $\D_c(X)$; hence we are reduced to the case $F=E_{U'\cap Y_p,X}$. But
\[
        E_{U'\cap Y_p,X}=j_{p*}i'_{p!}(E_{U'\cap Y_p}),
\]
and, by (\hyperref[sga5:I:corollary:1.13]{I 1.13}), it is equivalent to prove the theorem for $(i'_{p!}(E_{U'\cap Y_p}),\R j_p^!(K))$ on $Y_p$. By purity, however,
\[
        \R j_p^!(K)\simeq j_p^*(K)\otimes^L_{\Z/n\Z}(\mu_n)_{Y_p}^{\otimes -1}[-2].
\]
Thus $\R j_p^!(K)\in\ob\D^{+}_{\mathrm{coh}}(Y_p)$ and, for every geometric point $\bar{y}$ of $Y_p$, $(\R j_p^!(K))_{\bar{y}}$ is dualizing. The induction hypothesis then applies.

\phantomsection\label{sga5:I:theorem:7.5}\textbf{Theorem 7.5.} Assume the following: $X$ is a noetherian regular prescheme; $A_X$ is killed by an integer $n>0$ prime to the residual characteristics of $X$; $X$ is strongly desingularizable (\hyperref[sga5:I:statement:3.1.5]{I 3.1.5}), satisfies conditions (reg) (\hyperref[sga5:I:statement:3.1.1]{I 3.1.1}) and $(\mathrm{cdloc})_n$ (\hyperref[sga5:I:statement:3.1.3]{I 3.1.3}); and the purity theorem in the strong sense (\hyperref[sga5:I:definition:4.4]{4.4}) is true on every prescheme smooth over $X$ (conditions satisfied if $X$ is excellent of characteristic zero, or of dimension $\leq2$ and smooth over a perfect field).

Then, if $K\in\ob\D^{+}_{\mathrm{coh}}(X)$ is such that $K_{\bar{x}}$ is dualizing (in the sense of [H] V 2.1) for every geometric point $\bar{x}$ of $X$, the complex $K$ is dualizing.

\emph{Proof.} We must show that $K$ satisfies conditions (i) to (iii) of (\hyperref[sga5:I:definition:1.7]{I 1.7}). For every geometric point $\bar{x}$ of $X$, $K_{\bar{x}}$ has finite injective dimension, and since $K\in\ob\D^{+}_{\mathrm{coh}}(X)$, the function $x\mapsto\dim.\mathrm{inj}(K_{\bar{x}})$ is locally constant; it is therefore bounded because $X$ is noetherian. It follows from \hyperref[sga5:I:proposition:5.1]{(5.1)} that $K$ has finite quasi-injective dimension.

By what was seen in no.~6, condition (ii) of loc. cit. is also satisfied.

It remains to prove condition (iii), i.e. that every $F\in\ob\D_c^b(X)$ is reflexive relative to $K$. By dévissage we may suppose $F$ is concentrated in degree zero. Since the question is local on $X$ for the étale topology, we may suppose $A_X$ constant with value $A$. Dévissage \hyperref[sga5:I:proposition:1.3:2]{(1.3)} then reduces us to $F=f_*i_!(E_U)$, where $f:Y\to X$ is finite, $Y$ is integral with generic point separable over its image, $i:U\to Y$ is a nonempty regular open, and $E_U$ is an $A_U$-Module constant with value a finite type $A$-module $E$. Since $X$ is strongly desingularizable, there exists (\hyperref[sga5:I:statement:3.1.5]{I 3.1.5}) a projective birational morphism $h:Y'\to Y$ such that $Y'$ is regular, $h$ induces an isomorphism $h^{-1}(U)\to U$, and $h^{-1}(U)$ is the complement of a normal crossings divisor in $Y'$. Identifying $h^{-1}(U)$ with $U$ via $h$, we have a commutative diagram
\[
\begin{tikzcd}
& Y' \arrow[d,"h"] \arrow[dr,"g"] & \\
U \arrow[ur,"i'"] \arrow[r,"i"'] & Y \arrow[r,"f"'] & X.
\end{tikzcd}
\]
Consequently,
\[
        f_*i_!(E_U)\simeq f_*\R h_* i'_!(E_U)\simeq \R g_* i'_!(E_U)\simeq \R g_* i'_! i'^*(E_{Y'}).
\]
By (\hyperref[sga5:I:corollary:1.13]{I 1.13}), it is enough to prove biduality for the pair $(i'_!i'^*(E_{Y'}),\R g^!(K))$. But, locally embedding $Y'$ into a prescheme $X'$ smooth over $X$ and applying strong purity on $X'$ \hyperref[sga5:I:definition:4.4]{(4.4)}, one finds that $\R g^!(K)$ is locally isomorphic, up to degree shift, to $g^*(K)$. Hence $\R g^!(K)\in\ob\D^{+}_{\mathrm{coh}}(Y')$ and $\R g^!(K)_{\bar{x}}$ is dualizing for every geometric point $\bar{x}$ of $Y'$. It remains only to apply \hyperref[sga5:I:lemma:7.4]{(7.4)}.

The preceding proof also shows that $\R\underline{\Hom}(F,K)\in\ob\D_c^b(X)$ (using the finiteness theorem for a proper morphism (SGAA XIV)), which will reassure the reader who did not trust the spiel of no.~6.

This completes the proof.

\begin{center}
$-:\!-:\!-$
\end{center}

\section*{Bibliography}

\begin{enumerate}[label={[\arabic*]}]
\item Verdier, J.-L., \emph{Dimension des espaces localement compacts}, Note aux C.R. Ac. Sc. Paris, 261, pp.~5293--5296 (1965). See also: Verdier, J.-L., \emph{Dualité dans la cohomologie des espaces localement compacts}, Séminaire Bourbaki, no.~300, 1965--66.
\end{enumerate}

SGA A = SGA 4.

\begin{center}
$-:\!-:\!-$
\end{center}

\clearpage
\begin{center}
\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:expose:iii}{\Large \textbf{SGA 5 --- Exposé III}}\\[0.4em]
{\large \textbf{LEFSCHETZ FORMULA}}\\[0.3em]
by A.~Grothendieck, written up by L.~Illusie
\end{center}

In this exposé we prove the Lefschetz formula in étale cohomology, for correspondences between complexes of sheaves, in Verdier's formulation (cf.~[18]). In an earlier version, distributed by the IHES about ten years ago, the formula was stated, under resolution hypotheses, but the required compatibilities were not proved. Deligne's finiteness theorems (SGA~$4^{1/2}$, Finiteness) made it possible to dispense with these hypotheses, provided one works with schemes separated and of finite type over a field. As for the compatibilities, the editor has verified them (painfully).

Let us say at once that the Lefschetz formula in question here, just like the trace formula for constant coefficients (SGA~$4^{1/2}$, Cycle), which it generalizes, is essentially trivial: it only translates certain compatibilities in the formalism of duality. Roughly speaking, it says that, under certain conditions, the trace of an endomorphism of a hypercohomology complex $R\Gamma(X,L)$, where $L$ is a complex of sheaves on $X$, can be calculated as an ``integral'' of certain cohomology classes on $X\times X$. In practice, the endomorphisms one considers are associated with correspondences $C\subset X\times X$, and the classes recovered on the product have support in $C\cap\Delta$, where $\Delta$ is the diagonal; these classes are ``local in nature'' in a neighbourhood of the fixed points, which in principle facilitates their calculation. See \hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:4.2]{4.2} and \hyperref[sga5:III:corollary:4.7]{4.7} for precise statements. In fact, the calculation of these ``local'' classes in terms of more comprehensible invariants, without which the present formula would have no interest, has so far been undertaken only in very special situations (see \hyperref[sga5:III:corollary:4.10]{4.10}, \hyperref[sga5:III:statement:4.11]{4.11}, and the following exposé, where in particular the case of the Frobenius correspondence on a curve is treated).

For lack of a good derived category of $\ell$-adic sheaves (Jouanolou's thesis unfortunately not having been published), we work systematically with torsion coefficients (prime to the residual characteristics). Despite minor technical difficulties in the definition of traces, this point of view in fact leads to finer formulae, also more manageable insofar as, for the calculation of local terms, one wants to trivialize the sheaves involved by coverings (see notably (SGA~$4^{1/2}$, Report), III~B, and XII).

In no.~1, we fix the notation and recall various Künneth formulae from (SGA~$4^{1/2}$, Finiteness), which play an essential role in what follows. We also introduce a certain number of fibred and cofibred categories, whose consideration later facilitates certain verifications of compatibilities. No.~2, which is very technical, may be skipped on first reading. Its main result \hyperref[sga5:III:proposition:2.5]{(2.5)} expresses a compatibility between external tensor product of $\RHom$, direct image, and base change. In no.~3, we define cohomological correspondences between complexes, and show how they act on hypercohomology; the result quoted above makes it possible to describe this action in several equivalent ways. The core of the exposé is no.~4, where Verdier's pairing between cohomological correspondences is defined and the Lefschetz formula is proved, in a relative situation appreciably more general than the one usually considered, with a view to later applications to computations of local terms. We give the most evident corollaries for correspondences with constant coefficients. In no.~5, we indicate rapidly how to generalize to correspondences between complexes certain usual constructions on correspondences with constant coefficients (cf.~[12]), such as passage to the dual, composition, and external tensor product. Most of the verifications, analogous to those in the preceding numbers, have been omitted. Finally, in the appendix, we paraphrase the preceding theory in the context of coherent sheaves, using the formalism of duality of [8]. Here one may work over any noetherian base $S$, provided that, when necessary, one imposes hypotheses of properness, finite tor-dimension, and tor-independence on the $S$-schemes considered. We show that the very general Lefschetz formula thereby obtained contains as a special case the Woods Hole formula [1], and we point out some applications.

\subsection*{Contents}
\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.]
\item Notation and recalls of Künneth formulae.
\item Functoriality of $\RHom$ and external tensor products.
\item Cohomological correspondences.
\item Pairings of correspondences. Lefschetz formula.
\item Complements.
\item Appendix. Lefschetz formula for coherent sheaves.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Notation and recalls of Künneth formulae}

\subsection*{1.1}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:subsection:1.1}
We denote by $S$ the spectrum of a field. Unless otherwise stated, the $S$-schemes considered in what follows will be $S$-schemes separated and of finite type.

We denote by $A$ a commutative noetherian ring, annihilated by an integer invertible on $S$. If $X$ is an $S$-scheme, we denote by $D(X)=D(X,A)$ the derived category of complexes of $A$-modules on $X$ (for the étale topology), and by $D_{\ctf}(X)$ the full subcategory consisting of complexes of finite tor-dimension, with constructible cohomology. The category $D_{\ctf}(X)$ is a triangulated subcategory of $D(X)$. If $E,F\in\ob D_{\ctf}(X)$, it follows readily from the definitions that $E\otimes^L F\in\ob D_{\ctf}(X)$. According to (SGA~$4^{1/2}$, Finiteness Theorem 1.6, \hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:1.7]{1.7}), one also has $\RHom(E,F)\in\ob D_{\ctf}(X)$.

\subsection*{1.2}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:subsection:1.2}
Let $f:X\to Y$ be an $S$-morphism. One has trivially
\[
f^{*}D_{\ctf}(Y) \subset D_{\ctf}(X),
\]
and, by (loc. cit.),
\[
Rf_{*}D_{\ctf}(X) \subset D_{\ctf}(Y),\qquad
Rf_{!}D_{\ctf}(X) \subset D_{\ctf}(Y),\qquad
Rf^{!}D_{\ctf}(Y) \subset D_{\ctf}(X),
\]
(the inclusion relative to $Rf_{!}$ uses only the finiteness theorem for proper morphisms (SGA~4~XIV) and the sorites (SGA~4~XVII~5.2.11), and hence holds more generally for every compactifiable morphism whose fibres have bounded dimension). In what follows, we shall sometimes write $f_{*}$, $f_{!}$, $f^{!}$ instead of $Rf_{*}$, $Rf_{!}$, $Rf^{!}$; the hypotheses of \hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:1.1]{1.1} imply that these functors are defined on the whole derived category.

\subsection*{1.3}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:subsection:1.3}
For $f:X\to S$, we shall put
\[
K_X=f^{!}A,
\]
and shall denote by $D_X$ (or $D$) the functor $\RHom(-,K_X)$. According to (SGA~$4^{1/2}$, Finiteness Theorem 4.3), the complex $K_X$ is dualizing: for $E\in\ob D_{\ctf}(X)$, one has canonically
\[
E \xrightarrow{\sim} DDE.
\]

\subsection*{1.4}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:subsection:1.4}
Let $f:X\to Y$ be an $S$-morphism, $L\in\ob D(X)$, $M\in\ob D(Y)$. We put
\[
\Hom_f^{(1)}(L,M)=\Hom(f^{*}M,L)\quad (\simeq \Hom(M,f_{*}L)),
\]
\[
\Hom_f^{(2)}(L,M)=\Hom(L,f^{*}M),
\]
\[
\Hom_f^{(3)}(L,M)=\Hom(f_{!}L,M)\quad (\overset{\text{(SGA~4~XVIII~3.1.5)}}{\simeq} \Hom(L,f^{!}M)),
\]
\[
\Hom_f^{(4)}(L,M)=\Hom(f^{!}M,L).
\]
The elements of $\Hom_f^{(i)}(L,M)$ will be called \emph{arrows of type $i$ from $L$ to $M$ over $f$} (or $f$-arrows of type $i$ from $L$ to $M$). The transitivity isomorphisms for the functors $f^{*}$, $f_{*}$, $f_{!}$, $f^{!}$ allow one to compose arrows of type $i$ for fixed $i$, and one thus obtains certain fibred or cofibred categories over the category of $S$-schemes: for example, for $i=1$ (resp. $i=3$) one obtains the fibred and cofibred category considered in (SGA~4~XVII~4.1.3) (resp. (SGA~4~XVIII~3.1.13)), whose fibre over $X$ is the category opposite to $D(X)$ (resp. $D(X)$).

\subsection*{1.5}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:subsection:1.5}
Let, for $i=1,2$, $X_i$ be an $S$-scheme, $X=X_1\times_SX_2$, and $L_i\in\ob D^{-}(X_i)$. We put
\[
L_1\otimes^L_SL_2=\pr_1^{*}L_1\otimes^L\pr_2^{*}L_2,
\]
where $\pr_i:X\to X_i$ is the canonical projection.

\subsection*{1.6}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:subsection:1.6}
Let, for $i=1,2$, $f_i:X_i\to Y_i$ be an $S$-morphism,
\[
f=f_1\times_Sf_2:X=X_1\times_SX_2\to Y=Y_1\times_SY_2,
\]
$L_i\in\ob D^{-}(X_i)$, $M_i\in\ob D^{-}(Y_i)$. The evident canonical isomorphism
\begin{equation}\tag{1.6.1}\label{sga5:III:equation:1.6.1}
f_1^{*}M_1\otimes^L_Sf_2^{*}M_2\xrightarrow{\sim}f^{*}(M_1\otimes^L_SM_2)
\end{equation}
allows one to define, for $u_i\in\Hom_{f_i}^{(1)}(L_i,M_i)$ (resp. $\Hom_{f_i}^{(2)}(L_i,M_i)$),
\begin{equation}\tag{1.6.2}\label{sga5:III:equation:1.6.2}
u_1\otimes^L_Su_2\in\Hom_f^{(1)}(L,M)\quad(\text{resp. }\Hom_f^{(2)}(L,M)),
\end{equation}
where $L=L_1\otimes^L_SL_2$, $M=M_1\otimes^L_SM_2$. If $u_i$, $v_i$ are composable, one has
\begin{equation}\tag{1.6.3}\label{sga5:III:equation:1.6.3}
(v_1u_1)\otimes^L_S(v_2u_2)=(v_1\otimes^L_Sv_2)(u_1\otimes^L_Su_2).
\end{equation}
Taking $M_i=f_{i*}L_i$, and $u_i$ of type~1, given by the identity of $f_{i*}L_i$, \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:1.6.2]{1.6.2} gives a \emph{Künneth arrow} (cf.~SGA~4~XVII~5.4.1.4)
\begin{equation}\tag{1.6.4}\label{sga5:III:equation:1.6.4}
f_{1*}L_1\otimes^L_Sf_{2*}L_2\longrightarrow f_{*}(L_1\otimes^L_SL_2).
\end{equation}
According to (SGA~$4^{1/2}$, Finiteness Theorem 1.9), \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:1.6.4]{1.6.4} is an isomorphism: by \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:1.6.3]{1.6.3} one is indeed reduced to the case where $X_2=Y_2$, $f_2=1$, and one applies (loc. cit. App.). When $f_1$ and $f_2$ are proper, it is not necessary to invoke (loc. cit.): \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:1.6.4]{1.6.4} is a special case of the Künneth isomorphism (SGA~4~XVII~5.4.3).

\subsection*{1.7}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:subsection:1.7}
Choose a compactification $f_i=p_i j_i$, where $j_i$ is an open immersion and $p_i$ is a proper morphism. Composing the evident canonical isomorphism (where $j=j_1\times_Sj_2$)
\[
j_{1!}L_1\otimes^L_Sj_{2!}L_2\xrightarrow{\sim}j_{!}(L_1\otimes^L_SL_2)
\]
with the isomorphism \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:1.6.4]{1.6.4} relative to $(p_i,j_{i!}L_i)$, one obtains a Künneth isomorphism (SGA~4~XVII~5.4.3)
\begin{equation}\tag{1.7.1}\label{sga5:III:equation:1.7.1}
f_{1!}L_1\otimes^L_Sf_{2!}L_2\xrightarrow{\sim}f_{!}(L_1\otimes^L_SL_2).
\end{equation}
One checks easily (loc. cit.) that \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:1.7.1]{1.7.1} does not depend on the compactifications chosen, and is compatible with the transitivity isomorphisms for composites $g_if_i$ (cf.~(loc. cit. 5.2.4)). Thanks to \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:1.7.1]{1.7.1}, one can define, for $u_i\in\Hom_{f_i}^{(3)}(L_i,M_i)$,
\begin{equation}\tag{1.7.2}\label{sga5:III:equation:1.7.2}
u_1\otimes^L_Su_2\in\Hom_f^{(3)}(L,M)
\end{equation}
with $L$ and $M$ as in \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:1.6.2]{1.6.2}, and one has a transitivity formula analogous to \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:1.6.3]{1.6.3}.

For $L_i=f_i^{!}M_i$, and $u_i$ given by the identity of $L_i$, \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:1.7.2]{1.7.2} gives a \emph{Künneth arrow}
\begin{equation}\tag{1.7.3}\label{sga5:III:equation:1.7.3}
f_1^{!}M_1\otimes^L_Sf_2^{!}M_2\longrightarrow f^{!}(M_1\otimes^L_SM_2).
\end{equation}

\subsection*{Proposition 1.7.4}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:proposition:1.7.4}
The arrow \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:1.7.3]{1.7.3} is an isomorphism.

By the transitivity formula, it suffices to treat the case where $X_2=Y_2$, $f_2=1$. By dévissage, one reduces to $M_2=g_{*}j_{!}A_V$, where $j:V\to U$ is an open immersion and $g:U\to Y_2$ a finite morphism, and then, by (SGA~4~XVIII~3.1.12.3), to the case where $M_2=A_{Y_2}$. By localization on $X_2$, one may suppose moreover that $f_1=g_1j_1$, where $j_1$ is a closed immersion and $g_1$ a smooth morphism purely of relative dimension $n_1$. By transitivity, it suffices to treat the cases
\[
\text{a) } f_1=g_1,\qquad \text{b) } f_1=j_1.
\]
In case a), the trace morphism supplies by adjunction (SGA~4~XVIII~3.2.5) an isomorphism
\[
f_1^{*}M_1(n_1)[2n_1]\xrightarrow{\sim}f_1^{!}M_1;
\]
this is compatible with the base change $Y_2\to S$, because the trace morphism is so (SGA~4~XVIII~2.9), whence \hyperref[sga5:III:proposition:1.7.4]{1.7.4} in this case. In case b), if $i_1$ denotes the inclusion of the complementary open, one has an exact sequence
\[
0\longrightarrow j_{1*}Rj_1^{!}M_1\longrightarrow M_1\longrightarrow Ri_{1*}i_1^{*}M_1\longrightarrow0,
\]
and the compatibility of $Ri_{1*}$ with base change, a special case of \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:1.6.4]{1.6.4}, gives the conclusion.

Thanks to \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:1.7.3]{1.7.3}, one can define, for $u_i\in\Hom_f^{(4)}(L_i,M_i)$,
\begin{equation}\tag{1.7.5}\label{sga5:III:equation:1.7.5}
u_1\otimes^L_Su_2\in\Hom_f^{(4)}(L,M)
\end{equation}
($L$ and $M$ as in \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:1.6.2]{1.6.2}), with a transitivity formula analogous to \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:1.6.3]{1.6.3}.

On the other hand, with the notation of \hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:1.3]{1.3}, \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:1.7.3]{1.7.3} gives an isomorphism
\begin{equation}\tag{1.7.6}\label{sga5:III:equation:1.7.6}
K_{X_1}\otimes^L_SK_{X_2}\xrightarrow{\sim}K_X.
\end{equation}

\subsection*{1.8}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:subsection:1.8}
Let $f:X\to Y$ be an $S$-morphism, $L\in\ob D(X)$, $M\in\ob D(Y)$, $S'$ an $S$-scheme, $P\in\ob D(S')$, and $f':X'\to Y'$ the morphism deduced from $f$ by the base change $S'\to S$. The identity arrow of $P$ may be regarded indifferently as an arrow of type $i$ ($1\leq i\leq4$) over the identity arrow of $S'$; for $u\in\Hom_f^{(i)}(L,M)$ ($1\leq i\leq4$), one may therefore, by \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:1.6.2]{1.6.2}, \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:1.7.2]{1.7.2}, \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:1.7.5]{1.7.5}, consider
\begin{equation}\tag{1.8.1}\label{sga5:III:equation:1.8.1}
u\otimes^L_S\Id_P\in\Hom_{f'}^{(i)}(L\otimes^L_SP,M\otimes^L_SP).
\end{equation}
We shall sometimes write $u\otimes^L_SP$ instead of $u\otimes^L_S\Id_P$.

\section{Functoriality of \texorpdfstring{$\RHom$}{RHom} and external tensor products}

\subsection*{2.1}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:subsection:2.1}
Let $f:X\to Y$ be an $S$-morphism, $P\in\ob D(Y)$, $Q\in\ob D^{+}(Y)$. The evident canonical arrow at the level of complexes
\[
f^{*}\mathcal Hom^{\bullet}(P,Q)\longrightarrow\mathcal Hom^{\bullet}(f^{*}P,f^{*}Q)
\]
gives by derivation a canonical arrow
\begin{equation}\tag{2.1.1}\label{sga5:III:equation:2.1.1}
f^{*}\RHom(P,Q)\longrightarrow\RHom(f^{*}P,f^{*}Q).
\end{equation}
Let $L\in\ob D(X)$, $M\in\ob D^{+}(X)$. Put
\begin{equation}\tag{2.1.2}\label{sga5:III:equation:2.1.2}
\Hom_f^{(i,j)}((L,M),(P,Q))=\Hom_f^{(i)}(L,P)\times\Hom_f^{(j)}(M,Q),
\end{equation}
with the notation of \hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:1.4]{1.4}. For $(u,v)\in\Hom_f^{(2,1)}((L,M),(P,Q))$ one defines
\begin{equation}\tag{2.1.3}\label{sga5:III:equation:2.1.3}
\RHom(u,v)\in\Hom_f^{(1)}(\RHom(L,M),\RHom(P,Q))
\end{equation}
by composing \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.1.1]{2.1.1} with the arrow $\RHom(f^{*}P,f^{*}Q)\to\RHom(L,M)$ defined by $u$ and $v$. For $L=f^{*}P$, $Q=f_{*}M$, $u$ (resp. $v$) given by the identity of $L$ (resp. $Q$), $\RHom(u,v)$ is the ``trivial duality'' isomorphism
\begin{equation}\tag{2.1.4}\label{sga5:III:equation:2.1.4}
\RHom(P,f_{*}M)\xrightarrow{\sim}f_{*}\RHom(f^{*}P,M).
\end{equation}
For $(u,v)\in\Hom_f^{(3,4)}((L,M),(P,Q))$, one defines
\begin{equation}\tag{2.1.5}\label{sga5:III:equation:2.1.5}
\RHom(u,v)\in\Hom_f^{(1)}(\RHom(L,M),\RHom(P,Q))
\end{equation}
as the composite of the arrows
\[
\RHom(P,Q)\xrightarrow{a}\RHom(f_{!}L,Q)\xrightarrow{b}f_{*}\RHom(L,f^{!}Q)\xrightarrow{c}f_{*}\RHom(L,M),
\]
where $a$ (resp. $c$) is defined by $u$ (resp. $v$), and $b$ is the inverse of the duality isomorphism (SGA~4~XVIII~3.1.9.6).

It follows easily from the definitions that the formation of $\RHom(u,v)$ is compatible with composition: for composable $(u,v)$, $(u',v')$ of type $(2,1)$ (resp. $(3,4)$), one has
\begin{equation}\tag{2.1.6}\label{sga5:III:equation:2.1.6}
\RHom(u'u,v'v)=\RHom(u',v')\,\RHom(u,v).
\end{equation}

\subsection*{2.2}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:subsection:2.2}
Let $X$ be an $S$-scheme. Recall that for $L\in\ob D^{-}(X)$, $M\in\ob D(X)$, $N\in\ob D^{+}(X)$, one has the adjunction isomorphism (``dear to Cartan,'' as Grothendieck says)
\begin{equation}\tag{2.2.1}\label{sga5:III:equation:2.2.1}
\Hom(L\otimes^{L}M,N)=\Hom(L,\RHom(M,N)).
\end{equation}
For $E\in\ob D^{-}(X)$, $F\in\ob D^{+}(X)$, the identity arrow of $\RHom(E,F)$ defines via \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.2.1]{2.2.1} an arrow
\begin{equation}\tag{2.2.2}\label{sga5:III:equation:2.2.2}
E\otimes^{L}\RHom(E,F)\longrightarrow F,
\end{equation}
called the \emph{evaluation arrow}: it is derived from the arrow at the level of complexes
\[
\mathcal Hom^{\bullet}(E,F)\otimes E\longrightarrow F,
\qquad f\otimes x\longmapsto f(x).
\]
Let, for $i=1,2$, $E_i,F_i\in\ob D_{\ctf}(X)$. By adjunction \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.2.1]{(2.2.1)}, the arrow
\[
E_1\otimes^{L}E_2\otimes^{L}\RHom(E_1,F_1)\otimes^{L}\RHom(E_2,F_2)\longrightarrow F_1\otimes^{L}F_2,
\]
composed of a symmetry isomorphism and of the tensor product of the evaluation arrows relative to $(E_1,F_1)$ and $(E_2,F_2)$, supplies an arrow
\begin{equation}\tag{2.2.3}\label{sga5:III:equation:2.2.3}
\RHom(E_1,F_1)\otimes^{L}\RHom(E_2,F_2)\longrightarrow\RHom(E_1\otimes^{L}E_2,F_1\otimes^{L}F_2).
\end{equation}
Let, for $i=1,2$, $X_i$ be an $S$-scheme, $X=X_1\times_SX_2$, $p_i:X\to X_i$ the canonical projection, $E_i,F_i\in\ob D_{\ctf}(X_i)$, $E=E_1\otimes^{L}_{S}E_2$, $F=F_1\otimes^{L}_{S}F_2$. One has a canonical arrow
\begin{equation}\tag{2.2.4}\label{sga5:III:equation:2.2.4}
\RHom(E_1,F_1)\otimes^{L}_{S}\RHom(E_2,F_2)\longrightarrow\RHom(E,F),
\end{equation}
obtained by composing the tensor product of the arrows $p_i^{*}\RHom(E_i,F_i)\to\RHom(p_i^{*}E_i,p_i^{*}F_i)$ and \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.2.3]{2.2.3} for $(p_i^{*}E_i,p_i^{*}F_i)$.

\subsection*{Proposition 2.3}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:proposition:2.3}
The arrow \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.2.4]{2.2.4} is an isomorphism.

By localization and dévissage, one reduces to $E_i=f_{i!}A_{U_i}$ for $f_i:U_i\to X_i$ étale. One has canonical isomorphisms (duality)
\begin{equation}\tag{1}\label{sga5:III:equation:1}
\RHom(f_{i!}A_{U_i},F_i)\xrightarrow{\sim}f_{i*}f_i^{*}F_i,
\qquad
\RHom(E,F)\xrightarrow{\sim}f_{*}f^{*}F\quad(f=f_1\times_S f_2).
\end{equation}
Taking into account the Künneth \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:1.6.4]{formula 1.6.4}, it suffices, to conclude, to verify that these isomorphisms make the square
\begin{equation}\tag{2}\label{sga5:III:equation:2}
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
\RHom(f_{1!}A_{U_1},F_1)\otimes^{L}_{S}\RHom(f_{2!}A_{U_2},F_2) \arrow[r,"{\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.2.3]{2.2.3}}"] \arrow[d,"\sim"'] & \RHom(f_{!}A_U,F) \arrow[d,"\sim"]\\
f_{1*}f_1^{*}F_1\otimes^{L}_{S}f_{2*}f_2^{*}F_2 \arrow[r,"{\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:1.6.4]{1.6.4}}"'] & f_{*}f^{*}F
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation}
commute, where $U=U_1\times_SU_2$. Since $f_i$ is étale, the isomorphism (1) relative to $f_i$ (resp. $f$) is adjoint to the composite arrow
\begin{equation}\tag{3}\label{sga5:III:equation:3}
f_i^{*}\RHom(f_{i!}A_{U_i},F_i)\xrightarrow{\sim}\RHom(f_i^{*}f_{i!}A_{U_i},f_i^{*}F_i)\longrightarrow f_i^{*}F_i,
\end{equation}
where the second arrow is deduced from the adjunction $A_{U_i}\to f_i^{*}f_{i!}A_{U_i}$ (resp. the analogous composite arrow with $f$). The commutativity of (2) is equivalent to that of the square
\begin{equation}\tag{4}\label{sga5:III:equation:4}
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
f_1^{*}\RHom(f_{1!}A_{U_1},F_1)\otimes^{L}_{S}f_2^{*}\RHom(f_{2!}A_{U_2},F_2) \arrow[r] \arrow[d] & f^{*}\RHom(f_{!}A_U,F) \arrow[d]\\
f_1^{*}F_1\otimes^{L}_{S}f_2^{*}F_2 \arrow[r,"\sim"'] & f^{*}F,
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation}
where the vertical arrows are given by (3). But the commutativity of (4) follows from the fact that \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.2.3]{2.2.3} is functorial in $E_i$ and reduces to the identity for $E_i=A$, whence the proposition.

\subsection*{2.4}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:subsection:2.4}
Let, for $i=1,2$, $f_i:X_i\to Y_i$ be an $S$-morphism,
\[
f=f_1\times_S f_2:X\to Y,
\]
$E_i,F_i\in\ob D_{\ctf}(X_i)$, $P_i,Q_i\in\ob D_{\ctf}(Y_i)$,
\[
E=E_1\otimes^{L}_{S}E_2,
\quad F=F_1\otimes^{L}_{S}F_2,
\quad P=P_1\otimes^{L}_{S}P_2,
\quad Q=Q_1\otimes^{L}_{S}Q_2.
\]
Consider the diagram
\begin{equation}\tag{2.4.0}\label{sga5:III:equation:2.4.0}
\resizebox{0.96\textwidth}{!}{%
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=latex,baseline=(current bounding box.center)]
\node (EF1) at (-5.2,2.0) {$(E_1,F_1)$};
\node (X1)  at (-3.55,2.0) {$X_1$};
\node (X1Y2) at (-1.0,2.0) {$X_1\times_S Y_2$};
\node (X) at (1.9,2.0) {$X$};
\node (EF) at (3.15,2.35) {$(E,F)$};
\node (PQ1) at (-5.2,0.45) {$(P_1,Q_1)$};
\node (Y1) at (-3.55,0.45) {$Y_1$};
\node (Y) at (-1.0,0.45) {$Y$};
\node (Y1X2) at (1.9,0.45) {$Y_1\times_S X_2$};
\node (S) at (-3.55,-1.25) {$S$};
\node (Y2) at (-1.0,-1.25) {$Y_2$};
\node (X2) at (1.9,-1.25) {$X_2$};
\node (PQ) at (-1.0,-0.25) {$(P,Q)$};
\node (PQ2) at (-1.0,-1.9) {$(P_2,Q_2)$};
\node (EF2) at (2.95,-1.9) {$(E_2,F_2)$};
\draw[->] (X1Y2) -- (X1);
\draw[->] (X) -- (X1Y2);
\draw[->] (X1) -- node[left] {$f_1$} (Y1);
\draw[->] (X1Y2) -- (Y);
\draw[->] (X) -- (Y1X2);
\draw[->] (X) -- node[below,sloped,pos=.58] {$f$} (Y);
\draw[->] (Y) -- (Y1);
\draw[->] (Y1X2) -- (Y);
\draw[->] (Y1) -- (S);
\draw[->] (Y) -- (Y2);
\draw[->] (Y1X2) -- (X2);
\draw[->] (Y2) -- (S);
\draw[->] (X2) -- node[below] {$f_2$} (Y2);
\end{tikzpicture}}
\end{equation}
Give ourselves $(u_1,v_1)\in\Hom_{f_1}^{(3,4)}((E_1,F_1),(P_1,Q_1))$, $(u_2,v_2)\in\Hom_{f_2}^{(2,1)}((E_2,F_2),(P_2,Q_2))$ (notation of \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.1.2]{2.1.2} and \hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:1.4]{1.4}). By \hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:1.8]{1.8}, one deduces from $(u_i,v_i)$ a square
\begin{equation}\tag{2.4.1}\label{sga5:III:equation:2.4.1}
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=huge,row sep=large]
(E,F) \arrow[d,"{(u_1\otimes^L_S E_2,\,v_1\otimes^L_S F_2)}"] & (E_1\otimes^L_S P_2,F_1\otimes^L_S Q_2) \arrow[l,"{(E_1\otimes^L_S u_2,\,F_1\otimes^L_S v_2)}"'] \arrow[d,"{(u_1\otimes^L_S P_2,\,v_1\otimes^L_S Q_2)}"]\\
(P_1\otimes^L_S E_2,Q_1\otimes^L_S F_2) \arrow[r,"{(P_1\otimes^L_S u_2,\,Q_1\otimes^L_S v_2)}"'] & (P,Q),
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation}
where the vertical arrows (resp. horizontal arrows) are of type $(3,4)$ (resp. $(2,1)$).

We may now state the main compatibility of this number, which will play an essential role in the verification of the Lefschetz--Verdier formula.

\subsection*{Proposition 2.5}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:proposition:2.5}
The diagram \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.5.1]{2.5.1} below, where the square on the right is deduced from \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.4.1]{2.4.1} by applying $\RHom$ in the sense of \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.1.3]{2.1.3} and \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.1.5]{2.1.5}, is commutative:
\begin{equation}\tag{2.5.1}\label{sga5:III:equation:2.5.1}
\resizebox{0.98\textwidth}{!}{%
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=latex,baseline=(current bounding box.center)]
\footnotesize
\node (A) at (0,2.6) {$\RHom(E_1,F_1)\otimes^L_S\RHom(E_2,F_2)$};
\node (B) at (0,1.3) {$\RHom(u_1,v_1)\otimes^L_S\RHom(u_2,v_2)$};
\node (C) at (0,0) {$\RHom(P_1,Q_1)\otimes^L_S\RHom(P_2,Q_2)$};
\node (D) at (8.8,2.6) {$\RHom(E,F)$};
\node (E) at (6.2,1.3) {$\RHom(E_1\otimes^L_SP_2,F_1\otimes^L_SQ_2)$};
\node (F) at (12.1,1.3) {$\RHom(P_1\otimes^L_SE_2,Q_1\otimes^L_SF_2)$};
\node (G) at (8.8,0) {$\RHom(P,Q)$};
\draw[->] (A) -- node[above] {$\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.2.4]{2.2.4}$} (D);
\draw[->] (A) -- (B);
\draw[->] (B) -- (C);
\draw[->] (C) -- node[below] {$\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.2.4]{2.2.4}$} (G);
\draw[->] (D) -- (E);
\draw[->] (D) -- (F);
\draw[->] (E) -- (G);
\draw[->] (F) -- (G);
\end{tikzpicture}}
\end{equation}

\subsection*{2.6}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:subsection:2.6}
The proof of \hyperref[sga5:III:proposition:2.5]{2.5} will occupy the rest of no.~2. It follows from \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:1.6.3]{1.6.3} that, if $w_i:L_i\to M_i$ is an arrow of type~1 over $f_i$, the arrow $w=w_1\otimes^{L}_{S}w_2$ makes the diagram
\[
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
& L_1\otimes^L_S L_2 \arrow[dl,"{L_1\otimes^L_S w_2}"'] \arrow[dr,"{w_1\otimes^L_S L_2}"] & \\
L_1\otimes^L_S M_2 \arrow[dr,"{w_1\otimes^L_S M_2}"'] & & M_1\otimes^L_S L_2 \arrow[dl,"{M_1\otimes^L_S w_2}"]\\
& M_1\otimes^L_S M_2 &
\end{tikzcd}
\]
commute. Applying this remark to $w_i=\RHom(u_i,v_i)$, one sees that it suffices to prove the following special cases of \hyperref[sga5:III:proposition:2.5]{2.5}.

\subsection*{Corollary 2.6.1}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:corollary:2.6.1}
Let $f:X\to Y$ be an $S$-morphism, $S'$ an $S$-scheme, $f':X'\to Y'$ the morphism deduced from $f$ by the base change $S'\to S$, $E,F\in\ob D_{\ctf}(X)$, $P,Q\in\ob D_{\ctf}(Y)$, $P',Q'\in\ob D_{\ctf}(S')$, and $(u,v)\in\Hom_f^{(2,1)}((E,F),(P,Q))$ (resp. $\Hom_f^{(3,4)}((E,F),(P,Q))$). Then one has a commutative square, whose vertical arrows are of type~1 over $f'$:
\begin{equation}\tag{2.6.1.1}\label{sga5:III:equation:2.6.1.1}
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
\RHom(E,F)\otimes^L_S\RHom(P',Q') \arrow[r,"{\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.2.4]{2.2.4}}"] \arrow[d,"{\RHom(u,v)\otimes^L_S\RHom(P',Q')}"'] & \RHom(E\otimes^L_SP',F\otimes^L_SQ') \arrow[d,"{\RHom(u\otimes^L_SP',v\otimes^L_SQ')}"]\\
\RHom(P,Q)\otimes^L_S\RHom(P',Q') \arrow[r,"{\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.2.4]{2.2.4}}"'] & \RHom(P\otimes^L_SP',Q\otimes^L_SQ').
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation}
First examine the case where $(u,v)$ is of type $(2,1)$. By functoriality, one may suppose that $E=f^{*}P$, $F=f^{*}Q$, $(u,v)$ being given by the identity of $(f^{*}P,f^{*}Q)$. The commutativity of \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.6.1.1]{2.6.1.1} in the case $P'=Q'=A_{S'}$ is evident; to establish it in the general case, it therefore suffices to verify that, for $L,M\in\ob D_{\ctf}(Y)$, the square
\[
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
f^{*}\RHom(E,F)\otimes^L f^{*}\RHom(L,M) \arrow[r,"{f^*\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.2.3]{(2.2.3)}}"] \arrow[d,"{{\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.1.1]{2.1.1}}\otimes {\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.1.1]{2.1.1}}}"'] & f^{*}\RHom(E\otimes^LL,F\otimes^LM) \arrow[d,"{\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.1.1]{2.1.1}}"]\\
\RHom(f^{*}E,f^{*}F)\otimes^L\RHom(f^{*}L,f^{*}M) \arrow[r,"{\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.2.3]{2.2.3}}"'] & \RHom(f^{*}(E\otimes^LL),f^{*}(F\otimes^LM))
\end{tikzcd}
\]
is commutative. But one sees immediately that the two composites of this square are adjoint to the tensor product of the evaluation arrows
\[
f^{*}E\otimes^L f^{*}\RHom(E,F)\longrightarrow f^{*}F
\quad\text{and}\quad
f^{*}L\otimes^L f^{*}\RHom(L,M)\longrightarrow f^{*}M,
\]
which proves the assertion.

\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:equation:2.6.2}Now pass to the case where $(u,v)$ is of type $(3,4)$. By functoriality, one may suppose that $P=f_{!}E$, $F=f^{!}Q$, $(u,v)$ being defined by $(\Id_P,\Id_F)$. One must prove the commutativity of the square
\[
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
f_*\RHom(E,f^!Q)\otimes^L_S\RHom(P',Q') \arrow[r,"b"] \arrow[d,"{a\otimes^L_S\RHom(P',Q')}"'] & f'_*\RHom(E\otimes^L_SP',f^!Q\otimes^L_SQ') \arrow[d,"a'"]\\
\RHom(f_!E,Q)\otimes^L_S\RHom(P',Q') \arrow[r,"{\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.2.4]{2.2.4}}"'] & \RHom(f_!E\otimes^L_SP',Q\otimes^L_SQ'),
\end{tikzcd}
\]
where $b$ is the composite of $f'_*(\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.2.4]{2.2.4})$ and a base-change isomorphism, $a$ is the duality isomorphism (SGA~4~XVIII~3.1.9.6), and $a'$ is the composite of the duality isomorphism (relative to $E\otimes^L_SP'$ and $Q\otimes^L_SQ'$) and the base-change isomorphisms
\[
f_!E\otimes^L_SP'\xrightarrow[\sim]{c}f'_!(E\otimes^L_SP'),
\qquad
f^!Q\otimes^L_SQ'\xrightarrow[\sim]{d}f'^!(Q\otimes^L_SQ').
\]
Recall that $a$ is the composite of the canonical arrow (SGA~4~XVIII~3.1.9.5)
\[
f_*\RHom(E,f^!Q)\longrightarrow\RHom(f_!E,f_!f^!Q)
\]
and of the arrow defined by the adjunction arrow $f_!f^!Q\to Q$. There is an analogous description of $a'$, as the composite of the quoted arrow
\begin{equation}\tag{*}\label{sga5:III:equation:unnumbered}
f'_*\RHom(E\otimes^L_SP',f^!Q\otimes^L_SQ')\longrightarrow
\RHom(f'_!(E\otimes^L_SP'),f'_!(f^!Q\otimes^L_SQ')),
\end{equation}
of the arrows defined by $c$ and $d$, and of the arrow defined by the adjunction arrow
\[
f'_!f'^!(Q\otimes^L_SQ')\longrightarrow Q\otimes^L_SQ'.
\]
By definition, $d$ is adjoint to the arrow
\[
f'_!(f^!Q\otimes^L_SQ')\longrightarrow Q\otimes^L_SQ'
\]
defined by the base-change isomorphism $f'_!(f^!Q\otimes^L_SQ')\simeq f_!f^!Q\otimes^L_SQ'$ and the adjunction arrow $f_!f^!Q\to Q$; in other words, the square
\[
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
f'_!(f^!Q\otimes^L_SQ') \arrow[r,"{f'_!d}"] \arrow[d,"{\text{base ch.}\ \sim}"'] & f'_!f'^!(Q\otimes^L_SQ') \arrow[d,"\mathrm{adj}"]\\
f_!f^!Q\otimes^L_SQ' \arrow[r,"{\mathrm{adj}\otimes 1}"'] & Q\otimes^L_SQ'
\end{tikzcd}
\]
is commutative. It follows that one may also describe $a'$ as the composite of (*), of the arrow defined by $c$, and of those defined by the base-change isomorphism
\[
f'_!(f^!Q\otimes^L_SQ')\xrightarrow{\sim}f_!f^!Q\otimes^L_SQ'
\]
and the adjunction arrow $f_!f^!Q\to Q$. This decomposition of $a'$ and that of $a$ given above, together with the commutativity of the square
\[
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
\RHom(f_!E,f_!f^!Q)\otimes^L_S\RHom(P',Q') \arrow[r] \arrow[d] & \RHom(f_!E\otimes^L_SP',f_!f^!Q\otimes^L_SQ') \arrow[d]\\
\RHom(f_!E,Q)\otimes^L_S\RHom(P',Q') \arrow[r] & \RHom(f_!E\otimes^L_SP',Q\otimes^L_SQ'),
\end{tikzcd}
\]
defined by \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.2.4]{2.2.4} and the adjunction arrow $f_!f^!Q\to Q$, show that, to establish the commutativity of \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.6.2]{2.6.2}, it suffices to prove the following compatibility.

\subsection*{Lemma 2.6.3}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:lemma:2.6.3}
Let $f$, $f'$, $E$, $F$, $P'$, $Q'$ be as in \hyperref[sga5:III:corollary:2.6.1]{2.6.1}. Then one has a commutative square
\[
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
f_*\RHom(E,F)\otimes^L_S\RHom(P',Q') \arrow[r,"(1)"] \arrow[d,"(2)"'] & f'_*\RHom(E\otimes^L_SP',F\otimes^L_SQ') \arrow[d,"(4)"]\\
\RHom(f_!E,f_!F)\otimes^L_S\RHom(P',Q') \arrow[r,"(3)"'] & \RHom(f_!E\otimes^L_SP',f_!F\otimes^L_SQ'),
\end{tikzcd}
\]
where (1) is the composite of the base-change isomorphism
\[
f_*\RHom(E,F)\otimes^L_S -\xrightarrow{\sim}f'_*\bigl(\RHom(E,F)\otimes^L_S-\bigr)
\]
and $f'_*(\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.2.4]{2.2.4})$, (2) is deduced from the canonical arrow
\[
f_*\RHom(E,F)\longrightarrow\RHom(f_!E,f_!F)
\]
(SGA~4~XVIII~3.1.9.5) by applying $\otimes^L_S\RHom(P',Q')$, (3) is given by \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.2.4]{2.2.4}, and finally (4) is the composite of the canonical arrow
\[
f'_*\RHom(E\otimes^L_SP',F\otimes^L_SQ')
\longrightarrow
\RHom(f'_!(E\otimes^L_SP'),f'_!(F\otimes^L_SQ'))
\]
(loc. cit.) and the arrows defined by the base-change isomorphisms
\[
f'_!(E\otimes^L_SP')\xrightarrow{\sim}f_!E\otimes^L_SP',
\qquad
f'_!(F\otimes^L_SQ')\xrightarrow{\sim}f_!F\otimes^L_SQ'.
\]
Returning to the definition of the arrow (SGA~4~XVIII~3.1.9.5), choose a compactification of $f$\footnote{The arrow quoted is defined with the aid of such a compactification, but it does not depend on it: this point, tacitly admitted in (loc. cit.), is easily verified.}; one is then reduced to proving the lemma separately in the case where $f$ is an open immersion and in the case where $f$ is a proper morphism.

\emph{a) Case where $f$ is an open immersion.} By adjunction \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.2.1]{(2.2.1)}, it suffices to see that, if one tensors the square \hyperref[sga5:III:lemma:2.6.3]{2.6.3} by $f_!E\otimes^L_SP'$ and composes with the evaluation arrow \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.2.2]{2.2.2}
\[
