(f_!E\otimes^L_SP')\otimes^L\RHom(f_!E\otimes^L_SP',f_!F\otimes^L_SQ')\longrightarrow f_!F\otimes^L_SQ',
\]
the square obtained is commutative. But the sums of this square are zero outside $X'$, so that it suffices to verify that the square induced on $X'$ commutes, which is trivial.

\emph{b) Case where $f$ is a proper morphism.} Then $f_!=f_*$, $f'_!=f'_*$. One sees easily that the composite of
\[
f_*\RHom(E,F)\longrightarrow\RHom(f_*E,f_*F)
\]
with the trivial-duality isomorphism
\[
\RHom(f_*E,f_*F)\xrightarrow{\sim}f_*\RHom(f^{*}f_*E,F)
\]
\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.1.4]{(2.1.4)} is none other than the arrow deduced from the adjunction arrow $f^{*}f_*E\to E$ by applying $f_*\RHom(-,F)$. Similarly, the composite of (4) with the trivial-duality isomorphism
\[
\RHom(f_*E\otimes^L_SP',f_*F\otimes^L_SQ')\xrightarrow{\sim}f'_*\RHom(f^{*}f_*E\otimes^L_SP',F\otimes^L_SQ')
\]
($f_*F\otimes^L_SQ'$ being identified with $f'_*(F\otimes^L_SQ')$ by the canonical isomorphism) is none other than the arrow deduced from the adjunction arrow $f^{*}f_*E\to E$ by applying $f'_*\RHom(-\otimes^L_SP',F\otimes^L_SQ')$. Thus one is reduced to verifying the commutativity of the square
\[
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
\RHom(f_*E,f_*F)\otimes^L_S\RHom(P',Q') \arrow[r,"(3)"] \arrow[d] & \RHom(f_*E\otimes^L_SP',f_*F\otimes^L_SQ') \arrow[d]\\
f_*\RHom(f^{*}f_*E,F)\otimes^L_S\RHom(P',Q') \arrow[r,"(1')"'] & f'_*\RHom(f^{*}f_*E\otimes^L_SP',F\otimes^L_SQ'),
\end{tikzcd}
\]
where the vertical arrows are the trivial-duality isomorphisms and $(1')$ is (1) with $E$ replaced by $f^{*}f_*E$. But this is a special case of \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.6.1.1]{2.6.1.1} in the case, already treated, where $(u,v)$ is of type $(2,1)$ (take $u:f^{*}f_*E\to f_*E$ given by the identity of $f^{*}f_*E$, and $v:F\to f_*F$ given by the identity of $f_*F$). We have therefore proved \hyperref[sga5:III:lemma:2.6.3]{2.6.3}. As we have seen, the commutativity of \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.6.2]{2.6.2} follows, which completes the proof of \hyperref[sga5:III:corollary:2.6.1]{2.6.1} and, finally, of \hyperref[sga5:III:proposition:2.5]{2.5}.

\clearpage
\section{Cohomological Correspondences}

\subsection*{3.1}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:subsection:3.1}
Let, for $i=1,2$, $X_i$ be an $S$-scheme and $L_i\in\ob\Dctf(X_i)$, and let
\[
X=X_1\times_S X_2,
\qquad
p_i:X\longrightarrow X_i,
\qquad
q_i:X_i\longrightarrow S
\]
be the canonical projections:
\[
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=1.8em,row sep=1.5em]
& X \arrow[dl,"P_1"'] \arrow[dr,"P_2"] & \\
L_1\quad X_1 \arrow[dr,"q_1"'] & & X_2\quad L_2 \arrow[dl,"q_2"] \\
& S &
\end{tikzcd}
\]
With the notation of \hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:1.3]{1.3}, one has a canonical isomorphism \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.2.4]{(2.2.4)}
\[
DL_1\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S L_2
\xrightarrow{\sim}
\RHom\bigl(L_1\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S A_{X_2},
q_1^!A_S\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S L_2\bigr).
\]
Composing with the Künneth isomorphism
\[
q_1^!A_S\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S L_2
\xrightarrow{\sim}p_2^!L_2 \qquad \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:1.7.3]{(1.7.3)},
\]
one obtains an isomorphism
\begin{equation}
DL_1\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S L_2
\xrightarrow{\sim}
\RHom(p_1^*L_1,p_2^!L_2).
\tag{3.1.1}\label{sga5:III:equation:3.1.1}
\end{equation}
This isomorphism will play a fundamental role throughout. For $X_i=S$, \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.1.1]{3.1.1} reduces to
\[
L_1^\vee\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}L_2\xrightarrow{\sim}\RHom(L_1,L_2),
\]
a special case of an isomorphism valid for perfect complexes on an arbitrary ringed topos (SGA 6 I 7.7). We set
\begin{equation}
\RHom(p_1^*L_1,p_2^!L_2)=\RHom_S(L_1,L_2),
\qquad
\Hom(p_1^*L_1,p_2^!L_2)=\Hom_S(L_1,L_2).
\tag{3.1.2}\label{sga5:III:equation:3.1.2}
\end{equation}
The elements of $\Hom_S(L_1,L_2)$ will be called \emph{cohomological correspondences from $L_1$ to $L_2$}. Instead of ``cohomological correspondence from $A_{X_1}$ to $A_{X_2}$'', we shall simply say \emph{cohomological correspondence from $X_1$ to $X_2$} (understood: with coefficients in $A$).

If $X_1$ is \emph{smooth}, purely of relative dimension $r_1$, then the same is true of $p_2$, and by the duality theorem (SGA 4 XVIII 3.2.5) one has
\[
p_2^!L_2\xrightarrow{\sim}p_2^*L_2(r_1)[2r_1],
\]
therefore
\begin{equation}
\RHom_S(L_1,L_2)=\RHom(p_1^*L_1,p_2^*L_2)(r_1)[2r_1],
\qquad
\Hom_S(L_1,L_2)=\H^{2r_1}\bigl(X,\RHom(p_1^*L_1,p_2^*L_2)(r_1)\bigr).
\tag{3.1.3}\label{sga5:III:equation:3.1.3}
\end{equation}
In particular, the group of cohomological correspondences from $X_1$ to $X_2$ is identified in this case with $\H^{2r_1}(X,A(r_1))$.

\subsection*{3.2}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:subsection:3.2}
The cohomological correspondences met in practice are associated with $S$-morphisms $X_2\to X_1$, or more generally with cycles on $X$, or with schemes over $X$. Let
\[
c:C\longrightarrow X
\]
be a scheme over $X$ (one sometimes says that $c$ is a correspondence between $X_1$ and $X_2$), and put $c_i=p_ic:C\to X_i$ for the projections. We shall call \emph{cohomological correspondences from $L_1$ to $L_2$ with support in $c$} (or in $C$, if there is no risk of confusion) the elements of the group
\[
\H^0\bigl(X,c_!\RHom(c_1^*L_1,c_2^!L_2)\bigr)
\quad(=\Hom(c_1^*L_1,c_2^!L_2)\text{ if }c\text{ is proper}).
\]
By the induction formula (SGA 4 XVIII 3.1.12.2), one has a canonical isomorphism
\begin{equation}
c^!\RHom(p_1^*L_1,p_2^!L_2)
\xrightarrow{\sim}
\RHom(c_1^*L_1,c_2^!L_2),
\tag{3.2.1}\label{sga5:III:equation:3.2.1}
\end{equation}
whence, applying $c_!$ and composing with the adjunction arrow $c_!c^!\to \Id$, canonical arrows
\begin{equation}
c_!\RHom(c_1^*L_1,c_2^!L_2)
\longrightarrow
\RHom(p_1^*L_1,p_2^!L_2),
\tag{3.2.2}\label{sga5:III:equation:3.2.2}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\H^0\bigl(X,c_!\RHom(c_1^*L_1,c_2^!L_2)\bigr)
\longrightarrow
\Hom_S(L_1,L_2).
\tag{3.2.3}\label{sga5:III:equation:3.2.3}
\end{equation}
Thus every correspondence from $L_1$ to $L_2$ with support in $C$ canonically defines, by \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.2.3]{3.2.3}, a correspondence (without support) from $L_1$ to $L_2$.

\emph{Examples.} a) Suppose that $c$ is proper and that $c_2$ has finite tor-dimension and relative dimension $\leq N$. The trace morphism (SGA $4^{1/2}$ Cycle 2.3.3)
\[
\mathrm{Tr}_{c_2}:c_{2!}A_C\longrightarrow A_{X_2}(-N)[-2N]
\]
defines by adjunction a cohomological correspondence from $A_{X_1}$ to $A_{X_2}(-N)[2N]$ with support in $C$, which will be called the \emph{class of $(c,N)$}, and will be denoted
\begin{equation}
\operatorname{cl}(c,N)\in \H^{-2N}\bigl(C,c_2^!A_{X_2}(-N)\bigr).
\tag{3.2.4}\label{sga5:III:equation:3.2.4}
\end{equation}
The most important case is that in which $N=0$, i.e. $c_2$ is finite (and of finite tor-dimension); in that case $\operatorname{cl}(c)$ is a cohomological correspondence from $X_1$ to $X_2$ with support in $C$. If $X_1$ is smooth, purely of relative dimension $r_1$, then, by \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.1.3]{3.1.3} and \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.2.1]{3.2.1},
\begin{equation}
\H^{-2N}\bigl(C,c_2^!A_{X_2}(-N)\bigr)
=
\H^{2(r_1-N)}\bigl(C,c^!A_X(r_1-N)\bigr).
\tag{3.2.5}\label{sga5:III:equation:3.2.5}
\end{equation}
If moreover $c$ is a closed immersion, the second member rewrites as
\[
\H^{2(r_1-N)}_C\bigl(X,A_X(r_1-N)\bigr),
\]
and the class of $(c,N)$, considered as an element of this group, is precisely the cohomology class associated with $c$ in the sense of (SGA $4^{1/2}$ Cycle 2.3.1), as follows immediately from the definitions.

b) Let $F:X_2\to X_1$ be an $S$-morphism, and denote by $c(F):C(F)\to X$ its graph, i.e. the image of the section of $p_2$ defined by $F$ (``the set of points $(Fx,x)$''): $c_2$ is an isomorphism and $c_1c_2^{-1}=F$. One therefore has canonical isomorphisms
\begin{equation}
\RHom\bigl(c_1(F)^*L_1,c_2(F)^!L_2\bigr)=\RHom(F^*L_1,L_2),
\qquad
\Hom\bigl(c_1(F)^*L_1,c_2(F)^!L_2\bigr)=\Hom(F^*L_1,L_2).
\tag{3.2.6}\label{sga5:III:equation:3.2.6}
\end{equation}
In other words, the cohomological correspondences from $L_1$ to $L_2$ with support in the graph of $F$ are the ``lifts'' of $F$ to a homomorphism $F^*L_1\to L_2$. Here are two important examples of such correspondences:

(i) $X_1=X_2$, $L_1=L_2$, $F=1$ is the identity of $X_1$, whose graph $\Delta$ is the diagonal. The correspondence with support in $\Delta$ defined by the identity of $L_1$ is called the \emph{diagonal correspondence}. It is the element
\[
1\in \Hom(L_1,L_1)=\H^0\bigl(X,\Delta_*\Delta^!\RHom_S(L_1,L_1)\bigr)
\]
(a group that is identified with $\H^{2r}_\Delta(X,\RHom_S(L_1,L_1)(r))$ when $X_1$ is smooth, purely of relative dimension $r$).

(ii) Suppose that $S$ is the spectrum of a finite field $\mathbb F_q$, that $X_1=X_2$, $L_1=L_2$, and take for $F:X_1\to X_1$ the relative Frobenius endomorphism over $\mathbb F_q$, the identity on the underlying space and raising to the $q$-th power on $\mathcal O_{X_1}$ (cf. XIV), and take as lift of $F$ to $L_1$ the canonical isomorphism
\[
F^*L_1\xrightarrow{\sim}L_1
\]
(loc. cit. §2, Prop. 1) (more precisely, the $a$-th iterate of the isomorphism of (loc. cit.) if $q=p^a$). The cohomological correspondence from $L_1$ to $L_1$ with support in the graph of $F$ so defined is called the \emph{Frobenius correspondence} (relative to $\mathbb F_q$).

c) Certain cohomological correspondences between sheaves on curves, related to Hecke operators, have been studied by Langlands in ([13] §7).

d) Cohomological correspondences between constant sheaves (the most frequent ones in nature) furnish in a natural way, by ``integration'', cohomological correspondences between complexes, as we shall now see.

\subsection*{3.3}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:subsection:3.3}
Let, for $i=1,2$, $f_i:X_i\to Y_i$ be an $S$-morphism, and put
\[
f=f_1\times_S f_2:X\to Y,
\qquad
f'_1=f_1\times_S Y_2,
\qquad
f'_2=Y_1\times_S f_2,
\]
\[
p'_1=X_1\times_S f_2,
\qquad
p'_2=f_1\times_S X_2.
\]
\begin{equation}
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=3.8em,row sep=2.4em]
X_1\times_S Y_2 \arrow[d,"f'_1"'] & X \arrow[l,"p'_1"'] \arrow[dl,"f" description] \arrow[d,"p'_2"] \\
Y & Y_1\times_S X_2 \arrow[l,"f'_2"]
\end{tikzcd}
\tag{3.3.0}\label{sga5:III:equation:3.3.0}
\end{equation}
Let $L_i\in\ob\Dctf(X_i)$. There exists a unique isomorphism
\begin{equation}
f_*\RHom_S(L_1,L_2)
\xrightarrow{\sim}
\RHom_S(f_{1!}L_1,f_{2*}L_2)
\tag{3.3.1}\label{sga5:III:equation:3.3.1}
\end{equation}
whose inverse makes the square
\[
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=4.5em,row sep=2.6em]
D(f_{1!}L_1)\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S f_{2*}L_2
  \arrow[r,"{\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.1.1]{3.1.1}}"] \arrow[d,"(1)"']
& \RHom_S(f_{1!}L_1,f_{2*}L_2) \arrow[d] \\
f_{1*}DL_1\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S f_{2*}L_2
  \arrow[r,"(2)"']
& f_*\RHom_S(L_1,L_2),
\end{tikzcd}
\]
commutative, where (1) is deduced from the inverse of the duality isomorphism (SGA 4 XVIII 3.1.9.6)
\[
Df_{1!}L_1\xrightarrow{\sim}f_{1*}DL_1
\]
by applying $-\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S f_{2*}L_2$, and (2) is the composite of the Künneth isomorphism
\[
f_{1*}DL_1\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S f_{2*}L_2
\xrightarrow{\sim}
f_*(DL_1\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S L_2)
\]
and $f_*(\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.1.1]{3.1.1})$. Applying $R\Gamma(Y,-)$ to \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.3.1]{3.3.1}, one obtains an isomorphism
\begin{equation}
f_*:\Hom_S(L_1,L_2)
\xrightarrow{\sim}
\Hom_S(f_{1!}L_1,f_{2*}L_2).
\tag{3.3.2}\label{sga5:III:equation:3.3.2}
\end{equation}
For $u\in\Hom_S(L_1,L_2)$, the correspondence $f_*u$ will be called the \emph{direct image} of $u$ by $f$. When $Y_1=Y_2=S$, we shall write $u_*$ rather than $f_*u$. If $Y_1=Y_2=S$ is the spectrum of a separably closed field, \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.3.2]{3.3.2} reads
\begin{equation}
\Hom_S(L_1,L_2)
\xrightarrow{\sim}
\Hom\bigl(R\Gamma_c(X_1,L_1),R\Gamma(X_2,L_2)\bigr).
\tag{3.3.3}\label{sga5:III:equation:3.3.3}
\end{equation}
Thus cohomological correspondences ``operate'' on cohomology. The following statement will allow us to interpret this operation in several ways, and in particular to relate it to the usual definitions in situations such as a) or b) above.

\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:proposition:3.4}\begin{statement}{Proposition 3.4}
With the notation of \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.3.0]{3.3.0}, one has a commutative diagram of isomorphisms in $\Dctf(Y)$:
\begin{equation}
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=4.0em,row sep=2.8em]
& \RHom_S(f_{1!}L_1,f_{2*}L_2)
  \arrow[dl,"(1)"'] \arrow[dr,"(2)"] \arrow[dd,"(5)" description] & \\
f'_{1*}\RHom_S(L_1,f_{2*}L_2) \arrow[dr,"f'_{1*}(3)"'] &&
f'_{2*}\RHom_S(f_{1!}L_1,L_2) \arrow[dl,"f'_{2*}(4)"] \\
& f_*\RHom_S(L_1,L_2) &
\end{tikzcd}
\tag{3.4.1}\label{sga5:III:equation:3.4.1}
\end{equation}
where (5) is the inverse of \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.3.1]{3.3.1} and the arrows (1) to (4) are defined by the commutative diagrams below; in these diagrams ``base ch.'' denotes an arrow deduced from a base-change or Künneth isomorphism of one of the types considered in no.~1, ``dual.'' an arrow deduced from a duality isomorphism of type \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.1.4]{2.1.4} or (SGA 4 XVIII 3.1.9.6), and ``triv.'' a trivial isomorphism:
\[
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=3.7em,row sep=1.7em]
\RHom(f_{1!}L_1\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S A_{Y_2},K_{Y_1}\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S f_{2*}L_2)
  \arrow[r,"\mathrm{base\ ch.}"] \arrow[d,"\mathrm{base\ ch.}"']
& \RHom_S(f_{1!}L_1,f_{2*}L_2) \arrow[ddd,"(1)"] \\
\RHom(f'_{1!}(L_1\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S A_{Y_2}),K_{Y_1}\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S f_{2*}L_2) \arrow[d,"\mathrm{dual.}"']
& \\
f'_{1*}\RHom(L_1\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S A_{Y_2},f_1'^!(K_{Y_1}\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S f_{2*}L_2)) \arrow[d,"\mathrm{base\ ch.}"']
& \\
f'_{1*}\RHom(L_1\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S A_{Y_2},K_{X_1}\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S f_{2*}L_2) \arrow[r,"\mathrm{base\ ch.}"']
& f'_{1*}\RHom_S(L_1,f_{2*}L_2),
\end{tikzcd}
\]
\[
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=3.7em,row sep=1.7em]
\RHom(f_{1!}L_1\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S A_{Y_2},K_{Y_1}\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S f_{2*}L_2)
  \arrow[r,"\mathrm{base\ ch.}"] \arrow[d,"\mathrm{base\ ch.}"']
& \RHom_S(f_{1!}L_1,f_{2*}L_2) \arrow[ddd,"(2)"] \\
\RHom(f_{1!}L_1\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S A_{Y_2},f'_{2*}(K_{Y_1}\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S L_2)) \arrow[d,"\mathrm{dual.}"']
& \\
f'_{2*}\RHom(f_2'^*(f_{1!}L_1\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S A_{Y_2}),K_{Y_1}\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S L_2) \arrow[d,"\mathrm{triv.}"']
& \\
f'_{2*}\RHom(f_{1!}L_1\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S A_{X_2},K_{Y_1}\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S L_2) \arrow[r,"\mathrm{base\ ch.}"']
& f'_{2*}\RHom_S(f_{1!}L_1,L_2),
\end{tikzcd}
\]
\[
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=3.7em,row sep=1.7em]
\RHom(L_1\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S A_{Y_2},K_{X_1}\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S f_{2*}L_2)
  \arrow[r,"\mathrm{base\ ch.}"] \arrow[d,"\mathrm{base\ ch.}"']
& \RHom_S(L_1,f_{2*}L_2) \arrow[ddd,"(3)"] \\
\RHom(L_1\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S A_{Y_2},p'_{1*}(K_{X_1}\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S L_2)) \arrow[d,"\mathrm{dual.}"']
& \\
p'_{1*}\RHom(p_1'^*(L_1\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S A_{Y_2}),K_{X_1}\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S L_2) \arrow[d,"\mathrm{triv.}"']
& \\
p'_{1*}\RHom(L_1\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S A_{X_2},K_{X_1}\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S L_2) \arrow[r,"\mathrm{base\ ch.}"']
& p'_{1*}\RHom_S(L_1,L_2),
\end{tikzcd}
\]
\[
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=3.7em,row sep=1.7em]
\RHom(f_{1!}L_1\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S A_{X_2},K_{Y_1}\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S L_2)
  \arrow[r,"\mathrm{base\ ch.}"] \arrow[d,"\mathrm{base\ ch.}"']
& \RHom_S(f_{1!}L_1,L_2) \arrow[ddd,"(4)"] \\
\RHom(p'_{2!}(L_1\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S A_{X_2}),K_{Y_1}\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S L_2) \arrow[d,"\mathrm{dual.}"']
& \\
p'_{2*}\RHom(L_1\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S A_{X_2},p_2'^!(K_{Y_1}\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S L_2)) \arrow[d,"\mathrm{base\ ch.}"']
& \\
p'_{2*}\RHom(L_1\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S A_{X_2},K_{X_1}\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S L_2) \arrow[r,"\mathrm{base\ ch.}"']
& p'_{2*}\RHom_S(L_1,L_2).
\end{tikzcd}
\]
\end{statement}
This is only an explication of \hyperref[sga5:III:proposition:2.5]{2.5} in the particular case where $(u_1,v_1):(L_1,K_{X_1})\to(f_{1!}L_1,K_{Y_1})$ is given by the identity of $f_{1!}L_1$ and the canonical isomorphism $f_1^!K_{Y_1}\xrightarrow{\sim}K_{X_1}$, and where $(u_2,v_2):(A_{X_2},L_2)\to(A_{Y_2},f_{2*}L_2)$ is given by the canonical isomorphism $A_{X_2}\xrightarrow{\sim}f_2^!A_{Y_2}$ and the identity of $f_{2*}L_2$.

\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:corollary:3.5}\begin{statement}{Corollary 3.5}
Assume that $Y_1=Y_2=S$. Let $u\in\Hom_S(L_1,L_2)$. The direct image $f_*u\in\Hom(f_{1!}L_1,f_{2*}L_2)$ \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.3.2]{(3.3.2)} can be obtained in the following two equivalent ways:
\end{statement}
\begin{enumerate}[label=\alph*)]
\item Identify $u$, by trivial duality, with an arrow
\[
L_1\longrightarrow p_{1*}p_2^!L_2;
\]
compose with the base-change isomorphism
\[
p_{1*}p_2^!L_2\xrightarrow{\sim}f_1^!f_{2*}L_2;
\]
$f_*u$ is the adjoint of this composite arrow.
\item Identify $u$, by adjunction, with an arrow
\[
p_{2!}p_1^*L_1\longrightarrow L_2;
\]
compose with the base-change isomorphism
\[
f_2^*f_{1!}L_1\xrightarrow{\sim}p_{2!}p_1^*L_1;
\]
$f_*u$ is the adjoint of this composite arrow.
\end{enumerate}
Description a) (respectively b)) indeed corresponds to the path $(f_{1*}(3)\circ(1))^{-1}$ (respectively $f_{2*}(4)\circ(2)$) in \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.4.1]{3.4.1}.

\subsection*{3.6}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:subsection:3.6}
In practice, it is mainly description \hyperref[sga5:III:corollary:3.5]{3.5 b)} that is used. Let us spell it out at the level of cohomology classes, assuming, in order to lighten the notation, that $S$ is the spectrum of a separably closed field. Let
\[
u\in\Hom_S(L_1,L_2)=\Hom(p_1^*L_1,p_2^!L_2).
\]
For $x\in\H_c^i(X_1,L_1)$, $u_*(x)\in\H^i(X_2,L_2)$ is defined as follows: consider the element $p_1^*x\in\H^i(X_2,p_{2!}p_1^*L_1)$ deduced from $x$ by inverse image, apply $u$ to it, i.e. form the product
\[
u\cdot p_1^*x\in \H^i(X_2,p_{2!}p_2^!L_2),
\]
then apply the arrow
\[
a:\H^i(X_2,p_{2!}p_2^!L_2)\longrightarrow \H^i(X_2,L_2)
\]
defined by the adjunction arrow $p_{2!}p_2^!\to\Id$; in short:
\begin{equation}
u_*(x)=a(u\cdot p_1^*x).
\tag{3.6.1}\label{sga5:III:equation:3.6.1}
\end{equation}
Let $c:C\to X$ be a proper morphism, and let $u\in\Hom(c_1^*L_1,c_2^!L_2)$. Denote by
\[
u_*\in\Hom\bigl(R\Gamma_c(X_1,L_1),R\Gamma(X_2,L_2)\bigr)
\]
the image of $u$ under the composite of \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.2.3]{3.2.3} and \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.3.3]{3.3.3}. It follows from \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.6.1]{3.6.1} that, for $x\in\H_c^i(X_1,L_1)$, one has
\begin{equation}
u_*(x)=a(u\cdot c_1^*x),
\tag{3.6.2}\label{sga5:III:equation:3.6.2}
\end{equation}
where $c_1^*x$ is the inverse image of $x$ in $\H^i(X_2,c_{2!}c_1^*L_1)$ and
\[
a:\H^i(X_2,c_{2!}c_2^!L_2)\longrightarrow \H^i(X_2,L_2)
\]
is the arrow deduced from the adjunction $c_{2!}c_2^!L_2\to\Id$. In the situation of \hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:3.2]{Example 3.2} a), one thus finds
\begin{equation}
\operatorname{cl}(c,N)_*(x)=\mathrm{Tr}_{c_2}(c_1^*x),
\tag{3.6.3}\label{sga5:III:equation:3.6.3}
\end{equation}
where
\[
\mathrm{Tr}_{c_2}:\H^i(X_2,Rc_{2!}A_C)\longrightarrow\H^{i-2N}(X_2,A(-N))
\]
is the arrow induced by the trace morphism relative to $c_2$. For $N=0$, this formula agrees with (SGA $4^{1/2}$ Cycle 3.6): $\operatorname{cl}(c)_*$ is the homomorphism denoted $\eta_{X_2,X_1}$ there. If $X_1$ is smooth, purely of relative dimension $r_1$, and $c$ is a closed immersion, it follows from \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.6.1]{3.6.1} and from the remark made at the end of \hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:3.2]{3.2} a) that one also has
\begin{equation}
\operatorname{cl}(c,N)_*(x)=\mathrm{Tr}_{p_2}(\operatorname{cl}(c)\,p_1^*x),
\tag{3.6.4}\label{sga5:III:equation:3.6.4}
\end{equation}
where $\operatorname{cl}(C)\in \H_C^{2(r_1-N)}(X,A(r_1-N))$ is the cohomology class of $C$ and
\[
\mathrm{Tr}_{p_2}:\H^*(X_2,Rp_{2!}A_X)\longrightarrow \H^{*-2r_1}(X_2,A(-r_1))
\]
is the arrow defined by the trace morphism relative to $p_2$. This recovers the usual definition of the homomorphism induced on cohomology by a correspondence ([12] 1.3), (SGA $4^{1/2}$ Cycle 3.2).

\subsection*{3.6.5}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:subsection:3.6.5}
In the situation of \hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:3.2]{3.2} b), it follows from \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.6.2]{3.6.2} that, for $u\in\Hom(F^*L_1,L_2)$,
\[
u_*:\H_c^i(X_1,L_1)\longrightarrow \H^i(X_2,L_2)
\]
is the composite of the ``inverse image'' homomorphism
\[
\H_c^i(X_1,L_1)\longrightarrow \H^i(X_2,F^*L_1)
\]
and the homomorphism induced by $u$ (more generally, $f_*u:Rf_{1!}L_1\to Rf_{2*}L_2$ is the composite of the canonical homomorphism
\[
Rf_{1!}L_1\longrightarrow Rf_{2*}F^*L_1
\]
and of $Rf_{2*}u$). In particular, the diagonal correspondence induces the canonical arrow
\[
Rf_{1!}L_1\longrightarrow Rf_{1*}L_1
\]
(the identity if $X_1$ is proper over $S$).

\subsection*{3.7}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:subsection:3.7}
Return to the situation of the beginning of \hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:3.3]{3.3}, assuming in addition that a commutative square
\begin{equation}
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=3em,row sep=2em]
X \arrow[d,"f"'] & C \arrow[l,"c"'] \arrow[d,"g"] \\
Y & D \arrow[l,"d"]
\end{tikzcd}
\tag{3.7.1}\label{sga5:III:equation:3.7.1}
\end{equation}
is given, with $c$ proper. Put $c_i=p_ic$, $d_i=q_id$, where $p_i:X\to X_i$, $q_i:Y\to Y_i$ are the projections. From \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.7.1]{3.7.1} one deduces a commutative diagram whose inner square is cartesian:
\begin{equation}
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=latex,baseline=(current bounding box.center),scale=1]
\node (X) at (0,0) {$X$};
\node (Y) at (0,-1.45) {$Y$};
\node (Cp) at (1.65,0) {$C'$};
\node (D) at (1.65,-1.45) {$D$};
\node (C) at (2.55,1.1) {$C$};
\draw[->] (C) -- node[above,sloped,pos=.55] {$c$} (X);
\draw[->] (C) -- node[above,sloped,pos=.50] {$i$} (Cp);
\draw[->] (C) -- node[right,pos=.55] {$g$} (D);
\draw[->] (Cp) -- node[above] {$c'$} (X);
\draw[->] (Cp) -- node[right] {$g'$} (D);
\draw[->] (X) -- node[left] {$f$} (Y);
\draw[->] (D) -- node[below] {$d$} (Y);
\end{tikzpicture}
\tag{3.7.2}\label{sga5:III:equation:3.7.2}
\end{equation}
For $K\in\ob\Dctf(X)$, there is a canonical arrow
\begin{equation}
f_*c_!c^!K\longrightarrow d_*d^!f_*K,
\tag{3.7.3}\label{sga5:III:equation:3.7.3}
\end{equation}
which is the composite of the arrow
\[
f_*c_!c^!K=f_*c'_*(i_*i^!)c'^!K\longrightarrow f_*c'_*c'^!K=d_*g'_*c'^!K
\]
defined by the adjunction arrow $i_*i^!\to\Id$, and the isomorphism
\[
d_*g'_*c'^!K\xrightarrow{\sim}d_*d^!f_*K
\]
defined by the canonical isomorphism $g'_*d'^!\xrightarrow{\sim}d^!f_*$ (SGA 4 XVIII 3.1.12.3). Taking $K=\RHom_S(L_1,L_2)$, and composing \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.7.3]{3.7.3} with the isomorphism deduced from \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.3.1]{3.3.1}, one obtains a canonical arrow
\begin{equation}
f_*c_*c^!\RHom_S(L_1,L_2)
\longrightarrow
 d_*d^!\RHom_S(f_{1!}L_1,f_{2*}L_2),
\tag{3.7.4}\label{sga5:III:equation:3.7.4}
\end{equation}
which, by \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.2.1]{3.2.1}, may be rewritten as
\begin{equation}
f_*c_!\RHom(c_1^*L_1,c_2^!L_2)
\longrightarrow
 d_*\RHom(d_1^*(f_{1!}L_1),d_2^!(f_{2*}L_2)).
\tag{3.7.5}\label{sga5:III:equation:3.7.5}
\end{equation}
One deduces
\begin{equation}
f_*:\Hom(c_1^*L_1,c_2^!L_2)
\longrightarrow
\Hom(d_1^*(f_{1!}L_1),d_2^!(f_{2*}L_2)).
\tag{3.7.6}\label{sga5:III:equation:3.7.6}
\end{equation}
The arrow \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.7.5]{3.7.5} (respectively \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.7.6]{3.7.6}) generalizes \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.3.1]{3.3.1} (respectively \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.3.2]{3.3.2}), with which it coincides when $c$ and $d$ are the identity. Also note that, for $c$ proper, \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.2.2]{3.2.2} (respectively \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.2.3]{3.2.3}) is nothing other than \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.7.5]{3.7.5} (respectively \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.7.6]{3.7.6}) when $f_1$, $f_2$, and $d$ are the identity arrows. When $Y_1=Y_2=S=D$, we shall abbreviate $f_*u$ as $u_*$, as above.

\section{Pairings of Correspondences. Lefschetz Formula}

\subsection*{4.1}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:subsection:4.1}
Let, for $i=1,2$, $X_i$ be an $S$-scheme, $X=X_1\times_SX_2$, $p_i:X\to X_i$ the projections, $L_i\in\ob\Dctf(X_i)$, and $K_{X_i}$ the dualizing complex \hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:1.3]{(1.3)}. The canonical arrows
\begin{equation}
p_i^*\RHom(L_i,K_{X_i})\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}p_i^*L_i
\longrightarrow p_i^*K_{X_i},
\tag{4.1.1}\label{sga5:III:equation:4.1.1}
\end{equation}
composed from \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.1.1]{2.1.1} and the evaluation arrow \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.2.2]{2.2.2}, give, by tensor product on $X$ and composition with the Künneth isomorphism \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:1.7.6]{1.7.6}, a pairing
\begin{equation}
(DL_1\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S L_2)\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}(L_1\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S DL_2)
\longrightarrow K_X,
\tag{4.1.2}\label{sga5:III:equation:4.1.2}
\end{equation}
which may be rewritten, by \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.1.1]{3.1.1} and \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.1.2]{3.1.2}, as
\begin{equation}
\RHom_S(L_1,L_2)\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}\RHom_S(L_2,L_1)
\longrightarrow K_X.
\tag{4.1.3}\label{sga5:III:equation:4.1.3}
\end{equation}
One deduces a pairing, called cup product,
\begin{equation}
\langle\ ,\ \rangle:\Hom_S(L_1,L_2)\otimes\Hom_S(L_2,L_1)
\longrightarrow \H^0(X,K_X).
\tag{4.1.4}\label{sga5:III:equation:4.1.4}
\end{equation}
One may also regard \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.1.2]{4.1.2} as the external tensor product of the evaluation arrows
\[
DL_i\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}L_i\longrightarrow K_{X_i}.
\]
In view of the Künneth isomorphism \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.2.4]{2.2.4}, one thus sees that \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.1.2]{4.1.2} is a ``perfect duality'', i.e. identifies by the adjunction isomorphism \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.2.1]{2.2.1} each of the complexes $DL_1\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S L_2$ and $L_1\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S DL_2$ with the dual of the other (with values in $K_X$).

When $X_1=X_2=S$, \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.1.3]{4.1.3} is a special case of a pairing valid for perfect complexes on an arbitrary ringed topos (SGA 6 I 7.8), and, with the notation of (SGA 6 I 8.3), one has
\begin{equation}
\langle u,v\rangle=\mathrm{Tr}(vu)=\mathrm{Tr}(uv).
\tag{4.1.5}\label{sga5:III:equation:4.1.5}
\end{equation}

\subsection*{4.2}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:subsection:4.2}
Let $c:C\to X$, $d:D\to X$, and $e=c\times_Xd:E=C\times_XD\to X$. For $P,Q\in\ob\Dctf(X)$, one has a canonical arrow (which is not in general an isomorphism)
\begin{equation}
c^!P\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_X d^!Q
\longrightarrow e^!(P\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}Q),
\tag{4.2.1}\label{sga5:III:equation:4.2.1}
\end{equation}
defined, in the same way as \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:1.7.3]{1.7.3}, as the adjoint of the composite arrow
\[
e_!(c^!P\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_X d^!Q)
\xrightarrow[(1)]{\sim}
 c_!c^!P\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}d_!d^!Q
\xrightarrow{(2)} P\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}Q,
\]
where (1) is the Künneth isomorphism (SGA 4 XVII 5.4.2.2) and (2) is the tensor product of the adjunction arrows. Applying $e_!$ to \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.2.1]{4.2.1} and composing with the Künneth isomorphism just cited, one obtains a canonical arrow
\begin{equation}
c_!c^!P\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}d_!d^!Q
\longrightarrow e_!e^!(P\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}Q).
\tag{4.2.2}\label{sga5:III:equation:4.2.2}
\end{equation}
Similarly, composing the Künneth arrow
\[
c_*c^!P\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}d_*d^!Q
\longrightarrow e_*(c^!P\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_X d^!Q)
\]
with the arrow deduced from \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.2.1]{4.2.1} by applying $e_*$, one obtains a canonical arrow
\begin{equation}
c_*c^!P\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}d_*d^!Q
\longrightarrow e_*e^!(P\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}Q).
\tag{4.2.3}\label{sga5:III:equation:4.2.3}
\end{equation}
For $P=DL_1\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S L_2$, $Q=L_1\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}_S DL_2$, one deduces from \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.2.2]{4.2.2} and \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.2.3]{4.2.3}, by composition with \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.1.2]{4.1.2} and the isomorphism $e^!K_X=K_E$, and with the identifications \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.1.1]{3.1.1} and \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.2.1]{3.2.1}, pairings
\begin{equation}
c_!\RHom(c_1^*L_1,c_2^!L_2)\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}
 d_!\RHom(d_2^*L_2,d_1^!L_1)
\longrightarrow e_!K_E,
\tag{4.2.4}\label{sga5:III:equation:4.2.4}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
c_*\RHom(c_1^*L_1,c_2^!L_2)\overset{\mathbf L}{\otimes}
 d_*\RHom(d_2^*L_2,d_1^!L_1)
\longrightarrow e_*K_E,
\tag{4.2.4'}\label{sga5:III:equation:4.2.4:2}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\langle\ ,\ \rangle:
\Hom(c_1^*L_1,c_2^!L_2)\otimes\Hom(d_2^*L_2,d_1^!L_1)
\longrightarrow \H^0(E,K_E).
\tag{4.2.5}\label{sga5:III:equation:4.2.5}
\end{equation}
These pairings are compatible with étale localization: given a commutative square (not necessarily cartesian)
\[
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=2.5em,row sep=1.5em]
& E' \arrow[dl] \arrow[dr] & && & E \arrow[dl] \arrow[dr] & \\
C' \arrow[dr] && D' \arrow[dl] & \text{ étale over } & C \arrow[dr] && D \arrow[dl] \\
& X &&&& X &
\end{tikzcd}
\]
if $c'$, $d'$, $e'$ denote the composites $C'\to C\to X$, $D'\to D\to X$, $E'\to E\to X$, one has a commutative square.


\begin{equation}
\tag{4.2.6}\label{sga5:III:equation:4.2.6}
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
\Hom(c_1^*L_1,c_2^!L_2)\otimes\Hom(d_2^*L_2,d_1^!L_1)
  \arrow[r,"{\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.2.5]{4.2.5}}"] \arrow[d] &
\H^0(E,K_E)\arrow[d] \\
\Hom(c_1'{}^*L_1,c_2'{}^!L_2)\otimes\Hom(d_2'{}^*L_2,d_1'{}^!L_1)
  \arrow[r] &
\H^0(E',K_{E'})
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation}
where the vertical arrows are the restrictions, and the lower horizontal arrow is the composite of the arrow analogous to \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.2.5]{4.2.5} and the restriction
\[
\H^0(E'',K_{E''})\longrightarrow \H^0(E',K_{E'}),
\]
with $E''=C'\times_{X'}D'$ (there are analogous commutative squares with arrows of type \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.2.4]{4.2.4}, \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.2.4]{4.2.4}', which we leave to the reader to write down). If $Z$ is étale over $E$, we shall denote, for
\[
u\in\Hom(c_1^*L_1,c_2^!L_2),\qquad
v\in\Hom(d_2^*L_2,d_1^!L_1),
\]
\begin{equation}
\tag{4.2.7}\label{sga5:III:equation:4.2.7}
\langle u,v\rangle_Z\in \H^0(Z,K_Z)
\end{equation}
the image of $\langle u,v\rangle$ in $\H^0(Z,K_Z)$ by restriction (in practice, $Z$ will be a connected component of $E$). If $z$ is an isolated closed point of $E$, and $\bar z$ a geometric point above $z$, it follows from \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.2.6]{4.2.6} that
\[
\langle u,v\rangle_z\in \H^0(z,K_z)=A
\]
depends only on the situation deduced by strict localization at the images of $\bar z$ in $C,D,X$.

\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:example:4.3}\begin{statement}{Example 4.3}
Assume that $X_i$ is smooth, purely of relative dimension $r_i$, and that $c$ and $d$ are closed immersions, with $c_2$ (respectively $d_1$) finite. For $L_1=A_{X_1}$, $L_2=A_{X_2}$, we have, by \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.1.3]{3.1.3},
\[
DL_1\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}L_2=A_X(r_1)[2r_1],\qquad
L_1\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}DL_2=A_X(r_2)[2r_2],
\]
and \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.1.2]{4.1.2} is identified with the product
\[
A_X(r_1)[2r_1]\otimes^{\mathbf L}A_X(r_2)[2r_2]\longrightarrow A_X(r)[2r],
\]
where $r=r_1+r_2$, and hence, taking \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.2.1]{3.2.1} into account, \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.2.5]{4.2.5} is identified with the usual cup product
\[
\H_C^{2r_1}(X,A(r_1))\otimes \H_D^{2r_2}(X,A(r_2))
\longrightarrow
\H_{C\cap D}^{2r}(X,A(r)).
\]
Take as cohomological correspondence from $X_1$ to $X_2$ (respectively from $X_2$ to $X_1$) the class of $c$ (respectively $d$) (\hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:3.2]{3.2 a)}). It follows from (SGA $4^{1/2}$ Cycle 2.3.8) that, at an isolated point $z$ of $C\cap D$, $\langle \cl(c),\cl(d)\rangle_z$ is nothing but the image in $A$ of the intersection multiplicity of $C$ and $D$ at $z$.
\end{statement}

We now give the main result of the exposé.

\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:theorem:4.4}\begin{statement}{Theorem 4.4}
Let, for $i=1,2$, $f_i:X_i\longrightarrow Y_i$ be a proper $S$-morphism,
\[
f=f_1\times_S f_2:X\longrightarrow Y,\qquad
p_i:X\longrightarrow X_i,\qquad q_i:Y\longrightarrow Y_i
\]
the projections. Let moreover
\begin{equation}
\tag{4.4.0}\label{sga5:III:equation:4.4.0}
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=latex,baseline=(current bounding box.center),scale=0.98]
\node (Cp) at (0,2.1) {$C'$};
\node (C) at (3.0,2.1) {$C$};
\node (X) at (-1.25,1.0) {$X$};
\node (Cpp) at (1.45,1.0) {$C''$};
\node (Dp) at (0,0) {$D'$};
\node (D) at (3.0,0) {$D$};
\node (Y) at (-1.25,-1.15) {$Y$};
\node (Dpp) at (1.45,-1.15) {$D''$};
\draw[->] (C) -- (Cp);
\draw[->] (Cp) -- node[left,pos=.55] {$c'$} (X);
\draw[->] (C) -- node[above,sloped,pos=.45] {$c$} (X);
\draw[->] (C) -- (Cpp);
\draw[->] (Cpp) -- node[below,pos=.45] {$c''$} (X);
\draw[->] (Cp) -- node[right] {$f'$} (Dp);
\draw[->] (Cpp) -- node[right] {$f''$} (Dpp);
\draw[->] (C) -- node[right] {$g$} (D);
\draw[->] (X) -- node[left] {$f$} (Y);
\draw[->] (D) -- (Dp);
\draw[->] (Dp) -- node[left,pos=.55] {$d'$} (Y);
\draw[->] (D) -- node[above,sloped,pos=.45] {$d$} (Y);
\draw[->] (D) -- (Dpp);
\draw[->] (Dpp) -- node[below] {$d''$} (Y);
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{equation}
be a commutative cube with cartesian horizontal faces, with $c'$, $c''$, $d'$, $d''$ proper. Put
\[
c_i'=p_i c',\qquad c_i''=p_i c'',\qquad d_i'=q_i d',\qquad d_i''=q_i d''.
\]
Finally, let $L_i\in\ob \Dctf(X_i)$. Then there is a commutative square
\begin{equation}
\tag{4.4.1}\label{sga5:III:equation:4.4.1}
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=huge,row sep=huge]
f_*c'_*\RHom(c_1'{}^*L_1,c_2'{}^!L_2)\otimes^{\mathbf L}
f_*c''_*\RHom(c_2''{}^*L_2,c_1''{}^!L_1)
 \arrow[r,"(1)"] \arrow[d,"(2)"'] &
f_*c_*K_C \arrow[d,"(4)"] \\
d'_*\RHom(d_1'{}^*M_1,d_2'{}^!M_2)\otimes^{\mathbf L}
d''_*\RHom(d_2''{}^*M_2,d_1''{}^!M_1)
 \arrow[r,"(3)"'] &
d_*K_D ,
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation}
where $M_i=f_{i*}L_i$, $(3)$ is given by \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.2.4]{4.2.4}, $(1)$ is deduced from \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.2.4]{4.2.4} by applying $f_*$ and composing with the canonical arrow
\[
f_*c'_*(-)\otimes^{\mathbf L} f_*c''_*(-)\longrightarrow f_*(c'_*(-)\otimes^{\mathbf L}c''_*(-)),
\]
$(2)$ is the tensor product of the arrows \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.7.5]{3.7.5} relative to the faces containing $f$, and $(4)$ is defined by the adjunction arrow $g_*K_C=g_!g^!K_D\longrightarrow K_D$.
\end{statement}

\begin{prooftext}[Proof]
Put
\[
DL_1\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}L_2=P,\qquad
L_1\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}DL_2=Q,\qquad
DM_1\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}M_2=E,\qquad
M_1\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}DM_2=F.
\]
Consider the diagram
\begin{equation}
\tag{4.4.2}\label{sga5:III:equation:4.4.2}
{\scriptsize
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=2.6em,row sep=2.6em]
f_*c'_*c'{}^!P\otimes^{\mathbf L} f_*c''_*c''{}^!Q
  \arrow[rr,"(1)"] \arrow[d,"(8)"'] &&
f_*c_*c^!\bigl(P\otimes^{\mathbf L}Q\bigr)
  \arrow[r,"(2)"] \arrow[d,"(9)"'] &
f_*c_*c^!K_X \arrow[d,"(10)"]\\
d'_*d'{}^!f_*P\otimes^{\mathbf L}d''_*d''{}^!f_*Q
  \arrow[r,"(3)"'] \arrow[d,"(11)"'] &
d_*d^!\bigl(f_*P\otimes^{\mathbf L}f_*Q\bigr)
  \arrow[r,"(4)"'] \arrow[d,"(12)"'] &
d_*d^!f_*\bigl(P\otimes^{\mathbf L}Q\bigr)
  \arrow[r,"(5)"'] &
d_*d^!f_*K_X \arrow[d,"(13)"]\\
d'_*d'{}^!E\otimes^{\mathbf L}d''_*d''{}^!F
  \arrow[r,"(6)"'] &
d_*d^!\bigl(E\otimes^{\mathbf L}F\bigr)
  \arrow[rr,"(7)"'] &&
d_*d^!K_Y,
\arrow[from=1-1,to=2-2, phantom, "\scriptstyle (A)"]
\arrow[from=1-3,to=2-4, phantom, "\scriptstyle (B)"]
\arrow[from=2-1,to=3-2, phantom, "\scriptstyle (C)"]
\arrow[from=2-2,to=3-4, phantom, "\scriptstyle (D)"]
\end{tikzcd}
}
\end{equation}
The arrows are as follows: $(1)$ is deduced from \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.2.2]{4.2.2} by applying $f_*$; $(2)$ is defined by \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.1.2]{4.1.2}; $(3)$ is given by \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.2.2]{4.2.2}; $(4)$ is given by the canonical arrow $f_*P\otimes^{\mathbf L}f_*Q\longrightarrow f_*(P\otimes^{\mathbf L}Q)$; $(5)$ by \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.1.2]{4.1.2}; $(6)$ by \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.2.2]{4.2.2}; $(7)$ by \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.1.2]{4.1.2}; $(8)$ is the tensor product of arrows \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.7.3]{3.7.3}; $(9)$ and $(10)$ are defined by \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.7.3]{3.7.3}; $(11)$ and $(12)$ by the isomorphisms $f_*P\longrightarrow E$, $f_*Q\longrightarrow F$ \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.3.1]{(3.3.1)}; and $(13)$ by the adjunction arrow $f_*K_X\longrightarrow K_Y$. By definition, \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.4.1]{4.4.1} is identified, via \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.1.1]{3.1.1} and \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.2.1]{3.2.1}, with the outer boundary of \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.4.2]{4.4.2}. It remains only to prove that the squares $(A)$, $(B)$, $(C)$, $(D)$ are commutative. The commutativity of $(B)$ and $(C)$ is clear by functoriality. It remains to prove that of $(A)$ and $(D)$.

a) Commutativity of $(D)$. It is enough to prove that the square
\begin{equation}
\tag{4.4.3}\label{sga5:III:equation:4.4.3}
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
f_*P\otimes^{\mathbf L}f_*Q \arrow[r] \arrow[d] & f_*K_X \arrow[d]\\
E\otimes^{\mathbf L}F \arrow[r] & K_Y
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation}
(corresponding to $(D)$ for $d=\Id$) commutes. Recall that the pairing
\[
P\otimes^{\mathbf L}Q\longrightarrow K_X
\quad(\text{respectively }E\otimes^{\mathbf L}F\longrightarrow K_Y)
\]
is the external tensor product of the evaluation arrows
\[
DL_i\otimes^{\mathbf L}L_i\longrightarrow K_{X_i}
\quad(\text{respectively }DM_i\otimes^{\mathbf L}M_i\longrightarrow K_{Y_i}).
\]
By the Künneth isomorphisms
\[
f_{1*}DL_1\otimes_S^{\mathbf L} f_{2*}L_2 \lisom f_*P,\qquad
f_{1*}L_1\otimes_S^{\mathbf L} f_{2*}DL_2 \lisom f_*Q,
\]
\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.4.3]{4.4.3} is therefore identified with the external tensor product of the squares
\begin{equation}
\tag{4.4.4}\label{sga5:III:equation:4.4.4}
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
f_{i*}DL_i\otimes^{\mathbf L} f_{i*}L_i
 \arrow[r,"(1)"] \arrow[d,"(3)"'] &
f_{i*}K_{X_i} \arrow[d,"(4)"]\\
DM_i\otimes^{\mathbf L}M_i \arrow[r,"(2)"'] & K_{Y_i},
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation}
where $(2)$ is the evaluation arrow, $(1)$ the composite of the canonical arrow
\[
f_{i*}DL_i\otimes^{\mathbf L} f_{i*}L_i\longrightarrow f_{i*}(DL_i\otimes^{\mathbf L}L_i)
\]
and the arrow defined by the evaluation arrow, $(3)$ is the tensor product of the duality isomorphism $f_{i*}DL_i\longrightarrow DM_i$ and the identity arrow of $M_i$, and $(4)$ is the adjunction arrow. It is therefore enough to verify the commutativity of the squares \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.4.4]{4.4.4}. Since the above duality isomorphism is the composite of the canonical arrow (SGA 4 XVIII 3.1.9.5)
\[
f_{i*}\RHom(L_i,K_{X_i})\longrightarrow \RHom(f_{i*}L_i,f_{i*}K_{X_i})
\]
and the arrow deduced from the adjunction arrow
\[
f_{i*}K_{X_i}\longrightarrow K_{Y_i},
\]
it is enough to see that the following square commutes:
\begin{equation}
\tag{4.4.5}\label{sga5:III:equation:4.4.5}
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
f_{i*}\RHom(L_i,K_{X_i})\otimes^{\mathbf L} f_{i*}L_i
 \arrow[r,"(1)"] \arrow[d,"(3)"'] &
f_{i*}K_{X_i} \arrow[d,"\Id"]\\
\RHom(f_{i*}L_i,f_{i*}K_{X_i})\otimes^{\mathbf L} f_{i*}L_i
 \arrow[r,"(2)"'] &
f_{i*}K_{X_i},
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation}
where $(1)$ is the arrow $(1)$ of \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.4.4]{4.4.4}, $(2)$ the evaluation arrow, and $(3)$ the tensor product by $f_{i*}L_i$ of the canonical arrow just mentioned. More generally, for any arrow $N_i\longrightarrow f_{i*}L_i$ in $\Dctf(X_i)$, i.e. any arrow $L_i\xrightarrow{u}N_i$ of type $1$ over $f_i$, the triangle
\[
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
f_{i*}\RHom(L_i,K_{X_i})\otimes^{\mathbf L}N_i
 \arrow[dr] \arrow[d] & \\
\RHom(N_i,f_{i*}K_{X_i})\otimes^{\mathbf L}N_i \arrow[r] &
f_{i*}K_{X_i}
\end{tikzcd}
\]
deduced from \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.4.5]{4.4.5} commutes: indeed, by functoriality, one may assume that $L_i=f_i^*N_i$, with $u$ given by the identity of $L_i$; the arrow
\[
f_{i*}\RHom(L_i,K_{X_i})\longrightarrow\RHom(N_i,f_{i*}K_{X_i})
\]
is then the inverse of the trivial duality isomorphism, and the commutativity of the triangle is verified immediately at the level of complexes, resolving $N_i$ flatly and $K_{X_i}$ injectively.

b) Commutativity of $(A)$. Referring to the definition of \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.7.3]{3.7.3}, one sees that it is enough to verify this commutativity in each of the following cases:
\[
\text{(i) } X_i=Y_i,\quad f_i=\text{the identity arrow of }X_i;\qquad
\text{(ii) the lateral faces of \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.4.0]{4.4.0} are cartesian.}
\]
Place ourselves in case (i). The square $(A)$ is the outer boundary of the diagram
\[
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
c'_*c'{}^!P\otimes^{\mathbf L}c''_*c''{}^!Q
 \arrow[r,"\sim"] \arrow[d,"(8)"'] &
c_*\bigl(c'{}^!P\otimes_X^{\mathbf L}c''{}^!Q\bigr)
 \arrow[r] \arrow[d] &
c_*c^!(P\otimes^{\mathbf L}Q)\arrow[d,"(9)"]\\
d'_*d'{}^!P\otimes^{\mathbf L}d''_*d''{}^!Q
 \arrow[r,"\sim"'] &
d_*\bigl(d'{}^!P\otimes_X^{\mathbf L}d''{}^!Q\bigr)
 \arrow[r] &
d_*d^!(P\otimes Q),
\arrow[from=1-1,to=2-2,phantom,"\scriptstyle A_1"]
\arrow[from=1-2,to=2-3,phantom,"\scriptstyle A_2"]
\end{tikzcd}
\]
in which the horizontal isomorphisms of $A_1$ are Künneth isomorphisms, the horizontal arrows of $A_2$ are given by \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.2.1]{4.2.1}, and the middle vertical arrow is deduced by applying $d_*$ to the arrow
\[
g_!(c'{}^!P\otimes_X^{\mathbf L}c''{}^!Q)\longrightarrow d'{}^!P\otimes_X^{\mathbf L}d''{}^!Q
\]
defined, via Künneth, by the external tensor product of the adjunction arrows
\[
f'_!c'{}^!P\longrightarrow d'{}^!P,\qquad
f''_!c''{}^!Q\longrightarrow d''{}^!Q.
\]
These adjunction arrows and the identity arrows of $d'_!d'{}^!P$, $d''_!d''{}^!Q$ define commutative triangles of arrows of type $3$ \hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:1.4]{(1.4)}
\[
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
& c'{}^!P \arrow[d] \arrow[dl] & & c''{}^!Q \arrow[d]\arrow[dl]\\
d'_!d'{}^!P & d'{}^!P\arrow[l] & d''_!d''{}^!Q & d''{}^!Q\arrow[l]
\end{tikzcd}
\]
over
\[
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
& C' \arrow[d,"f'"] \arrow[dl,"c'"']\\
X & D'\arrow[l,"d'"]
\end{tikzcd}
\qquad
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
& C'' \arrow[d,"f''"] \arrow[dl,"c''"']\\
X & D''\arrow[l,"d''"]
\end{tikzcd}.
\]
By external tensor product (over $X$), these triangles give a commutative triangle
\[
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=huge,row sep=large]
& c'{}^!P\otimes_X^{\mathbf L}c''{}^!Q \arrow[d] \arrow[dl]\\
d'_!d'{}^!P\otimes d''_!d''{}^!Q &
d'{}^!P\otimes_X^{\mathbf L}d''{}^!Q\arrow[l]
\end{tikzcd}
\qquad\text{over}\qquad
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
& C\arrow[d,"g"]\arrow[dl,"c"']\\
X & D\arrow[l,"d"]
\end{tikzcd}.
\]
The commutativity of this triangle is equivalent to that of the square deduced from $A_1$ by inverting the horizontal arrows. On the other hand, there are commutative triangles of arrows of type $3$
\[
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
& c'{}^!P \arrow[d]\arrow[dl]\\
P & d'{}^!P\arrow[l]
\end{tikzcd}
\qquad
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
& c''{}^!Q \arrow[d]\arrow[dl]\\
Q & d''{}^!Q\arrow[l]
\end{tikzcd}
\]
defined by the identity arrows of $d'{}^!P$, $c'{}^!P$, $d''{}^!Q$, $c''{}^!Q$. By external tensor product, one obtains a commutative triangle
\[
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=huge,row sep=large]
& c'{}^!P\otimes_X^{\mathbf L}c''{}^!Q \arrow[d]\arrow[dl]\\
P\otimes^{\mathbf L}Q & d'{}^!P\otimes_X^{\mathbf L}d''{}^!Q\arrow[l]
\end{tikzcd}
\]
which can be interpreted as a morphism of arrows of type $3$ over $g:C\longrightarrow D$:
\[
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
c'{}^!P\otimes_X^{\mathbf L}c''{}^!Q \arrow[r]\arrow[d] &
c'{}^!(P\otimes^{\mathbf L}Q)\arrow[d]\\
d'{}^!P\otimes_X^{\mathbf L}d''{}^!Q \arrow[r] &
d'{}^!(P\otimes Q),
\end{tikzcd}
\]
or again as a commutative square over $D$:
\[
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
g_*(c'{}^!P\otimes_X^{\mathbf L}c''{}^!Q) \arrow[r]\arrow[d] &
g_*c'{}^!(P\otimes^{\mathbf L}Q)\arrow[d]\\
d'{}^!P\otimes_X^{\mathbf L}d''{}^!Q \arrow[r] &
d^!(P\otimes^{\mathbf L}Q).
\end{tikzcd}
\]
But the square deduced from the latter by applying $d_*$ is none other than $A_2$, whence the commutativity of $(A)$ in case (i).

It remains to treat case (ii). Since the faces of \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.4.0]{4.4.0} containing $f$ are cartesian, the arrows
\[
f'_*c'{}^!P\longrightarrow d'{}^!f_*P,\qquad
f''_*c''{}^!Q\longrightarrow d''{}^!f_*Q
\]
\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.7.3]{(3.7.3)} are isomorphisms. Their inverses define arrows of type $1$
\[
c'{}^!P\longrightarrow d'{}^!f_*P,\qquad
c''{}^!Q\longrightarrow d''{}^!f_*Q
\]
over $f'$, $f''$, whence, by direct image, commutative squares of arrows of type $1$
\[
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
c'_*c'{}^!P \arrow[d] & c'{}^!P\arrow[l]\arrow[d]\\
d'_*d'{}^!P & d'{}^!f_*P\arrow[l]
\end{tikzcd}
\qquad
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
c''_*c''{}^!Q \arrow[d] & c''{}^!Q\arrow[l]\arrow[d]\\
d''_*d''{}^!Q & d''{}^!f_*Q\arrow[l]
\end{tikzcd}
\]
over the faces of \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.4.0]{4.4.0} containing $f$. Taking the tensor product, one obtains a commutative square in $\Dctf(Y)$
\begin{equation}
\tag{4.4.6}\label{sga5:III:equation:4.4.6}
{\scriptsize
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
d'_*d'{}^!f_*P\otimes^{\mathbf L}d''_*d''{}^!f_*Q
 \arrow[r]\arrow[d] &
d_*\bigl(d'{}^!f_*P\otimes_Y^{\mathbf L}d''{}^!f_*Q\bigr)\arrow[d]\\
f_*c'_*c'{}^!P\otimes^{\mathbf L}f_*c''_*c''{}^!Q
 \arrow[r] &
d_*g_*\bigl(c'{}^!P\otimes_X^{\mathbf L}c''{}^!Q\bigr),
\end{tikzcd}
}
\end{equation}
where the left vertical arrow is the inverse of $(8)$, the horizontal arrows are given by Künneth, and the right vertical arrow by the external tensor product over $Y$ of the arrows
\[
f'_*c'{}^!P\longrightarrow d'{}^!f_*P,\qquad
f''_*c''{}^!Q\longrightarrow d''{}^!f_*Q.
\]
The same tensor product defines the left vertical arrow of the square
\begin{equation}
\tag{4.4.7}\label{sga5:III:equation:4.4.7}
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
d'{}^!f_*P\otimes_Y^{\mathbf L}d''{}^!f_*Q
 \arrow[r]\arrow[d] &
d^!f_*(P\otimes^{\mathbf L}Q)\arrow[d]\\
g_*(c'{}^!P\otimes_X^{\mathbf L}c''{}^!Q)
 \arrow[r] &
g_*c'{}^!(P\otimes^{\mathbf L}Q),
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation}
where the right vertical arrow is \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.7.3]{3.7.3}, and the horizontal arrows are defined by \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.2.1]{4.2.1}. The outer boundary of the diagram composed of \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.4.6]{4.4.6} and $d_*(\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.4.7]{4.4.7})$ is the square deduced from $(A)$ by inverting the vertical arrows. It is therefore enough to prove that \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.4.7]{4.4.7} commutes, in other words that the arrows \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.2.1]{4.2.1}
\[
c'{}^!P\otimes_X^{\mathbf L}c''{}^!Q\longrightarrow c'{}^!(P\otimes^{\mathbf L}Q),\qquad
d'{}^!f_*P\otimes_Y^{\mathbf L}d''{}^!f_*Q\longrightarrow d^!f_*(P\otimes^{\mathbf L}Q)
\]
define a morphism from
\[
c'{}^!P\otimes_X^{\mathbf L}c''{}^!Q\longrightarrow d'{}^!f_*P\otimes_Y^{\mathbf L}d''{}^!f_*Q
\]
to
\[
c'{}^!(P\otimes^{\mathbf L}Q)\longrightarrow d^!f_*(P\otimes^{\mathbf L}Q),
\]
viewed as arrows of type $1$ over $g$. By adjunction, we are reduced to verifying that the tensor products of adjunction arrows
\[
c'_*c'{}^!P\otimes_X^{\mathbf L}c''_*c''{}^!Q\longrightarrow P\otimes^{\mathbf L}Q,
\qquad
d'_*d'{}^!f_*P\otimes_Y^{\mathbf L}d''_*d''{}^!f_*Q
\longrightarrow f_*P\otimes^{\mathbf L}f_*Q
\]
define a morphism, between arrows of type $1$ over $f$, from the tensor product of the arrows
\[
c'_*c'{}^!P\longrightarrow d'_*d'{}^!f_*P,\qquad
c''_*c''{}^!Q\longrightarrow d''_*d''{}^!f_*Q
\]
to the tensor product of the arrows
\[
P\longrightarrow f_*P,\qquad Q\longrightarrow f_*Q.
\]
For this it is enough to verify that the adjunction arrows
\[
c'_*c'{}^!P\longrightarrow P,\qquad d'_*d'{}^!f_*P\longrightarrow f_*P
\]
define a morphism from
\[
c'_*c'{}^!P\longrightarrow d'_*d'{}^!f_*P
\]
to
\[
P\longrightarrow f_*P,
\]
and likewise for the pair $(c'',d'')$. It is enough to consider the case of $(c',d')$, the statement being the same in the two cases. We have to see that the triangle
\[
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
& f_*c'_*c'{}^!P \arrow[d,"\sim"]\arrow[dl]\\
f_*P & d'_*d'{}^!f_*P\arrow[l]
\end{tikzcd}
\]
where the horizontal and oblique arrows are given by adjunction and the vertical arrow by the isomorphism \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.7.3]{3.7.3}, is commutative. Taking into account the definition of \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.7.3]{3.7.3} (SGA 4 XVIII 3.1.12.3) as the transpose of a base-change isomorphism, we are reduced, by applying the functor $\Hom(R,-)$ ($R\in\ob\Dctf(Y)$), to verifying the commutativity of the square
\[
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
c'_*c'{}^*f^*R \arrow[r,"\Id"] &
c'_*f'{}^*d'{}^*R\\
f^*R \arrow[u] \arrow[r] &
f^*d'_*d'{}^*R\arrow[u,"\sim"']
\end{tikzcd}
\]
where the left vertical arrow (respectively the lower horizontal arrow) is defined by the adjunction arrow $1\longrightarrow c'_*c'{}^*$ (respectively $1\longrightarrow d'_*d'{}^*$), and the right vertical arrow by the base-change isomorphism
\[
f^*d'_*\lisom c'_*f'{}^*.
\]
But this square commutes by the very definition of the base-change arrow. The commutativity of $(A)$ is therefore established, and this completes the proof of the theorem.
\end{prooftext}

\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:corollary:4.5}\begin{statement}{Corollary 4.5}
Under the hypotheses of \hyperref[sga5:III:theorem:4.4]{4.4}, there is a commutative square
\[
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
\Hom(c_1'{}^*L_1,c_2'{}^!L_2)\otimes
\Hom(c_2''{}^*L_2,c_1''{}^!L_1)
 \arrow[r,"{\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.2.5]{4.2.5}}"] \arrow[d,"{\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.7.6]{3.7.6}}\otimes {\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.7.6]{3.7.6}}"'] &
\H^0(C,K_C)\arrow[d,"g_*"]\\
\Hom(d_1'{}^*f_{1*}L_1,d_2'{}^!f_{2*}L_2)\otimes
\Hom(d_2''{}^*f_{2*}L_2,d_1''{}^!f_{1*}L_1)
 \arrow[r,"{\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.2.5]{4.2.5}}"'] &
\H^0(D,K_D),
\end{tikzcd}
\]
where $g_*$ is defined by the adjunction arrow
\[
g_*K_C=g_*g^!K_D\longrightarrow K_D.
\]
In other words, for
\[
u\in\Hom(c_1'{}^*L_1,c_2'{}^!L_2),\qquad
v\in\Hom(c_2''{}^*L_2,c_1''{}^!L_1),
\]
one has
\begin{equation}
\tag{4.5.1}\label{sga5:III:equation:4.5.1}
\langle f_*u,f_*v\rangle=g_*\langle u,v\rangle.
\end{equation}
\end{statement}

For every proper $S$-scheme $t:T\longrightarrow S$, denote by
\begin{equation}
\tag{4.6}\label{sga5:III:equation:4.6}
\int_T:\H^0(T,K_T)\longrightarrow \H^0(S,K_S)=\H^0(S,A)
\end{equation}
the arrow defined by the adjunction arrow
\[
t_!K_T\longrightarrow A.
\]

\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:corollary:4.7}\begin{statement}{Corollary 4.7 -- Lefschetz--Verdier formula}
Under the hypotheses of \hyperref[sga5:III:theorem:4.4]{4.4}, suppose that $Y_1=Y_2=S=D'=D''$. For
\[
u\in\Hom(c_1'{}^*L_1,c_2'{}^!L_2),\qquad
v\in\Hom(c_2''{}^*L_2,c_1''{}^!L_1),
\]
one has
\begin{equation}
\tag{4.7.1}\label{sga5:III:equation:4.7.1}
\langle u_*,v_*\rangle=\int_C\langle u,v\rangle,
\end{equation}
where
\[
u_*\in\Hom(f_{1*}L_1,f_{2*}L_2),\qquad
v_*\in\Hom(f_{2*}L_2,f_{1*}L_1)
\]
are the homomorphisms deduced from $u$, $v$ by direct image over $S$ \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.7.6]{(3.7.6)}, and
\[
\langle u_*,v_*\rangle=\Tr(u_*v_*)=\Tr(v_*u_*)
\]
is the cup product, in the sense of (SGA 6 I 8.3), of the homomorphisms of perfect complexes $u_*$, $v_*$ \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.1.5]{(4.1.5)}.
\end{statement}

Taking \hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:3.6.5]{3.6.5} into account, in particular one has:

\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:corollary:4.8}\begin{statement}{Corollary 4.8}
Let $t:T\longrightarrow S$ be a proper $S$-scheme, $\Delta\subset T\times_S T$ the diagonal, $F:T\longrightarrow T$ an $S$-endomorphism of $T$, $T^F=(\text{graph of }F)\times_{(T\times_S T)}\Delta$ the scheme of fixed points of $F$, $L\in\ob\Dctf(T)$, $u\in\Hom(F^*L,L)$ a correspondence from $L$ to $L$ with support in the graph of $F$, and $1\in\Hom(L,L)$ the identity correspondence, with support in $\Delta$ (\hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:3.2]{3.2 b)}). Then
\[
\Tr(u_*)=\int_{T^F}\langle u,1\rangle,
\]
where $u_*$ is the endomorphism of $t_*L$ obtained by composing $t_*L\longrightarrow t_*F^*L$ (inverse image) with $t_*u:t_*F^*L\longrightarrow t_*L$.
\end{statement}

From the remarks of \hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:3.2]{3.2} a) and \hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:3.6]{3.6} one also deduces:

\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:corollary:4.9}\begin{statement}{Corollary 4.9}
Under the hypotheses of \hyperref[sga5:III:corollary:4.7]{4.7}, assume that $X_i$ is smooth over $S$, purely of relative dimension $r_i$, and that $C'$ (respectively $C''$) is a closed subscheme of $X$ finite and of finite tor-dimension over $X_2$ (respectively $X_1$). Let
\[
\cl(C')\in \H_{C'}^{2r_1}(X,A(r_1)),\qquad
\cl(C'')\in \H_{C''}^{2r_2}(X,A(r_2))
\]
be the cohomology classes of $C'$ and $C''$, and let
\[
\cl(C')_*\in\Hom(\R f_{1*}A,\R f_{2*}A),\qquad
\cl(C'')_*\in\Hom(\R f_{2*}A,\R f_{1*}A)
\]
be the corresponding homomorphisms (\hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:3.2]{3.2 a)}, \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.7.6]{3.7.6}). Then
\[
\langle \cl(C')_*,\cl(C'')_*\rangle
=
\int_{C'\cap C''}\cl(C)\cl(D),
\]
where
\[
\cl(C)\cl(D)\in
\H_{C'\cap C''}^{2(r_1+r_2)}(X,A(r_1+r_2))
=
\H^0(C'\cap C'',K_{C'\cap C''})
\]
is the usual cup product.
\end{statement}

Taking \hyperref[sga5:III:example:4.3]{4.3} into account, in particular one has:

\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:corollary:4.10}\begin{statement}{Corollary 4.10}
Under the hypotheses of \hyperref[sga5:III:corollary:4.9]{4.9}, suppose that $C'\cap C''$ is finite. For $z\in C'\cap C''$, denote by $(C'\!\cdot C'')_z$ the image in $A$ of the intersection multiplicity of $C'$ and $C''$ at $z$. Then
\[
\langle \cl(C')_*,\cl(C'')_*\rangle
=
\sum_{z\in C'\cap C''}(C'\!\cdot C'')_z.
\]
\end{statement}

\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:statement:4.11}\begin{statement}{4.11}
Under the hypotheses of \hyperref[sga5:III:corollary:4.9]{4.9}, one has in general
\begin{equation}
\tag{4.11.1}\label{sga5:III:equation:4.11.1}
\langle \cl(C')_*,\cl(C'')_*\rangle
=
\sum_{Z\in\pi_0(C'\cap C'')}(\cl(C')\cl(C''))_Z,
\end{equation}
where $\pi_0(C'\cap C'')$ denotes the set of connected components of $C'\cap C''$, and $(\cl(C')\cl(C''))_Z$ the restriction of $\cl(C')\cl(C'')$ to $\H^0(Z,K_Z)$. When $Z$ is a component of dimension $\geq 1$, the calculation of $(\cl(C')\cl(C''))_Z$ presents problems, because one is in an ``excess'' situation. If $Z$ is smooth, purely of dimension $d$, if $C'$ and $C''$ are smooth in a neighbourhood of $Z$, and if $X$ is projective, one can show that
\begin{equation}
\tag{4.11.2}\label{sga5:III:equation:4.11.2}
\int_Z(\cl(C')\cl(C''))_Z=\int_Z c_d(N_Z),
\end{equation}
where $N_Z$ is the locally free sheaf of rank $d$ defined by the exact sequence of normal bundles
\[
0\longrightarrow N_{Z/C'}\oplus N_{Z/C''}\longrightarrow N_{Z/X}\longrightarrow N_Z\longrightarrow 0,
\]
and $c_d(N_Z)\in \H^{2d}(Z,A(d))$ denotes its $d$-th Chern class (SGA 5 VII) (use (SGA 6 XIV 6.3) to reduce to a calculation of intersection number in K-theory, and apply (SGA 6 VII 2.5), cf. ([10] 4.1); the projectivity hypothesis is no doubt unnecessary).

Let $T$ be a smooth projective $S$-scheme; take as correspondence $C'$ (respectively $C''$) the graph of an $S$-endomorphism $F$ (respectively the diagonal), and suppose that the scheme of fixed points $T^F=C'\cap C''$ is smooth. The sheaf $N_{(T^F)}$ above is nothing other than the tangent sheaf to $T^F$, so that \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.11.2]{4.11.2} implies, by the Gauss--Bonnet formula (SGA 5 \hyperref[sga5:VII:corollary:4.9]{VII 4.9}), the analogue of a formula well known to topologists:
\begin{equation}
\tag{4.11.3}\label{sga5:III:equation:4.11.3}
\Tr(F,\R\Gamma(T,A))=\EP(T^F),
\end{equation}
where $\EP(T^F)$ denotes the Euler--Poincaré characteristic of $T_s^F$, for $s$ a geometric point above $S$ (loc. cit.).
\end{statement}

The calculation of $(\cl(C')\cl(C''))_Z$ for a non-smooth component $Z$ of dimension $\geq 1$ has not been treated in general, at least to the knowledge of the editor.

\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:statement:4.12}\begin{statement}{4.12}
In the situation of \hyperref[sga5:III:corollary:4.7]{4.7}, let $z$ be an isolated point of $C$, with projection $z_i$ on $X_i$, $a'$ (respectively $a''$) on $C'$ (respectively $C''$), and let $\bar z$ be a geometric point above $z$, with image $\bar z_i$ above $z_i$. Assume that $c_2'$ (respectively $c_1''$) is quasi-finite and flat at $a'$ (respectively $a''$), that $L_i$ is perfect in a neighbourhood of $\bar z_i$, and that $u$ (respectively $v$) is given in a neighbourhood of $a'$ (respectively $a''$) by
\[
\Tr_{c_2'}:c_{2!}'c_2'{}^*L_2\longrightarrow L_2
\quad\text{and}\quad
s\in\Hom(c_1'{}^*L_1,c_2'{}^*L_2)
\]
(respectively
\[
\Tr_{c_1''}:c_{1!}''c_1''{}^*L_1\longrightarrow L_1
\quad\text{and}\quad
t\in\Hom(c_2''{}^*L_2,c_1''{}^*L_1)).
\]
Let $s_{\bar z}\in\Hom(L_{1\bar z_1},L_{2\bar z_2})$ (respectively $t_{\bar z}\in\Hom(L_{2\bar z_2},L_{1\bar z_1})$) be the homomorphism deduced from $s$ (respectively $t$) by passage to the fibres. Let again, as in \hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:3.2]{3.2} a), $\cl(c')$ (respectively $\cl(c'')$) denote the correspondence from $X_1$ to $X_2$ (respectively from $X_2$ to $X_1$) defined, in a neighbourhood of $a'$ (respectively $a''$), by $c_2'$ (respectively $c_1''$), and let $(C'\!\cdot C'')_z$ be the corresponding local term $\langle\cl(c'),\cl(c'')\rangle_z$ (equal to the image in $A$ of the intersection multiplicity of $C'$ and $C''$ at $z$ when $C'$ and $C''$ are closed subschemes of $X$, with $X_1$ and $X_2$ smooth). Then:
\begin{equation}
\tag{4.12.1}\label{sga5:III:equation:4.12.1}
\langle u,v\rangle_z=(C'\!\cdot C'')_z\langle s_{\bar z},t_{\bar z}\rangle.
\end{equation}
This follows easily from \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.2.6]{4.2.6}.
\end{statement}

\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:statement:4.13}\begin{statement}{4.13}
If $T$ is a scheme over $S$, denote by $DF(T)$ the filtered derived category of complexes of $A_T$-modules with finite filtration ([9] V 1), and by $DF_{\ctf}(T)$ the full subcategory formed by the $L$ such that $\gr(L)\in\ob\Dctf(T)$. The kind of compatibility of filtered derived functors with passage to associated graded objects implies that $DF_{\ctf}$ is stable under the six operations $f^*$, $f_*$, $f_!$, $f^!$, $\otimes^{\mathbf L}$, $\RHom$. The definitions and results of the preceding numbers are valid, mutatis mutandis, in $DF_{\ctf}$. The pairing \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.1.2]{4.1.2} is compatible with passage to associated graded objects ($K_X$ being regarded as endowed with the trivial filtration), and it follows that, with the notation of \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.2.5]{4.2.5}, for $L_i\in\ob DF_{\ctf}(X_i)$,
\[
u\in\Hom_{DF}(c_1^*L_1,c_2^!L_2),\qquad
v\in\Hom_{DF}(d_2^*L_2,d_1^!L_1),
\]
one has the following equality in $\H^0(E,K_E)$:
\begin{equation}
\tag{4.13.1}\label{sga5:III:equation:4.13.1}
\langle u,v\rangle=\sum_i\langle \gr^i u,\gr^i v\rangle.
\end{equation}
As Deligne observes, this remark applies in particular to the situation considered by Langlands in ([13] §7). The correspondences that he considers in (loc. cit. 7.11) indeed preserve, near the fixed points, finite filtrations on the sheaves with associated graded objects inducing, on the strict localization at the image of a fixed point, either a constant sheaf or a constant sheaf on the complement of the closed point extended by zero. The calculation of the local terms is easily reduced, by \hyperref[sga5:III:statement:4.13]{4.13}, to \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.12.1]{4.12.1} and to the case of sheaves concentrated on the closed points that are the images of the fixed points, a case treated directly. Applying \hyperref[sga5:III:corollary:4.7]{4.7}, one obtains the Lefschetz formula required in ([13] 7.11). We point out that the general statement (loc. cit. 7.12) is true; the proof, more delicate, will be given in III B.
\end{statement}



\section*{5. Complements}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:section:5}

\subsection*{5.1. Dual of a correspondence}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:subsection:5.1}

Let, for $i=1,2$, $X_i$ be an $S$-scheme, let $X=X_1\times_S X_2$, and let $p_i:X\to X_i$ be the projections. Let $L_i\in\operatorname{ob}D_{\mathrm{ctf}}(X_i)$. Every cohomological correspondence $u$ from $L_1$ to $L_2$ gives, by applying $D_X$ and identifying $Dp_2^!$ with $p_2^*D$ and $Dp_1^*$ with $p_1^!D$, a correspondence $Du$ from $DL_2$ to $DL_1$, called the \emph{dual of $u$}. In other words, $D$ defines a homomorphism
\begin{equation}
\tag{5.1.1}\label{sga5:III:equation:5.1.1}
D:\Hom(p_1^*L_1,p_2^!L_2)\longrightarrow \Hom(p_2^*DL_2,p_1^!DL_1).
\end{equation}
One checks that \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:5.1.1]{5.1.1} is obtained, by applying $H^0(X,-)$, from the isomorphism
\begin{equation}
\tag{5.1.2}\label{sga5:III:equation:5.1.2}
D:\RHom(p_1^*L_1,p_2^!L_2)\xrightarrow{\sim}\RHom(p_2^*DL_2,p_1^!DL_1)
\end{equation}
which makes the square
\begin{equation}
\tag{5.1.3}\label{sga5:III:equation:5.1.3}
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
p_1^*DL_1\otimes^{\mathbf L}p_2^*L_2 \arrow[r,"{\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.1.1]{3.1.1}}"] \arrow[d]
& \RHom(p_1^*L_1,p_2^!L_2) \arrow[d] \\
p_2^*DDL_2\otimes^{\mathbf L}p_1^*DL_1 \arrow[r,"{\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.1.1]{3.1.1}}"]
& \RHom(p_2^*DL_2,p_1^!DL_1),
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation}
commutative. Here the left vertical arrow is the composite of the symmetry isomorphism and the one defined by the biduality isomorphism $L_2\to DDL_2$. It follows easily from the definitions that the following square is commutative:
\begin{equation}
\tag{5.1.4}\label{sga5:III:equation:5.1.4}
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
\RHom_S(L_1,L_2)\otimes^{\mathbf L}\RHom_S(L_2,L_1) \arrow[r,"{\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.1.3]{4.1.3}}"] \arrow[d,"D\otimes^{\mathbf L} D"']
& K_X \arrow[d,"\operatorname{Id}"] \\
\RHom_S(DL_2,DL_1)\otimes^{\mathbf L}\RHom_S(DL_1,DL_2) \arrow[r,"{\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.1.3]{4.1.3}}"]
& K_X.
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation}
Let $c:C\to X$ be an $S$-morphism and put $c_i=p_ic$. Applying $c_*c^!$ to \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:5.1.2]{5.1.2}, and using \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.2.1]{(3.2.1)}, one deduces an isomorphism
\begin{equation}
\tag{5.1.5}\label{sga5:III:equation:5.1.5}
D:\Hom(c_1^*L_1,c_2^!L_2)\xrightarrow{\sim}\Hom(c_2^*DL_2,c_1^!DL_1),
\end{equation}
which is checked to identify with the one obtained by applying the functor $D_X$ and the identifications of $Dc_1^*$ with $c_1^!D$ and $Dc_2^!$ with $c_2^*D$. If $c':C'\to X$ and $c'':C''\to X$ are $S$-morphisms, it follows from \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:5.1.4]{5.1.4} that, for
\[
u\in\Hom(c_1'{}^*L_1,c_2'{}^!L_2),\qquad v\in\Hom(c_2''{}^*L_2,c_1''{}^!L_1),\]
one has
\begin{equation}
\tag{5.1.6}\label{sga5:III:equation:5.1.6}
\langle u,v\rangle=\langle Du,Dv\rangle,
\end{equation}
with the notation of \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.2.5]{4.2.5}.

On the other hand, one checks that, in the situation of \hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:3.3]{3.3}, the following square is commutative:
\begin{equation}
\tag{5.1.7}\label{sga5:III:equation:5.1.7}
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
f_*\RHom_S(L_1,L_2) \arrow[r,"{\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.3.1]{3.3.1}}"] \arrow[d,"f_*D"']
& \RHom_S(f_{1!}L_1,f_{2*}L_2) \arrow[d,"D"] \\
f_*\RHom_S(DL_2,DL_1) \arrow[r,"{\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.3.1]{3.3.1}}"]
& \RHom_S(f_{2!}DL_2,f_{1*}DL_1),
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation}
and consequently, in the situation of \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.7.6]{3.7.6}, one has
\begin{equation}
\tag{5.1.8}\label{sga5:III:equation:5.1.8}
f_*Du=Df_*u.
\end{equation}

\subsection*{5.2. Composition of correspondences}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:subsection:5.2}

Let, for $i=1,2,3$, $X_i$ be an $S$-scheme, let $X_{ij}=X_i\times_SX_j$, $X=X_1\times_SX_2\times_SX_3$, and let $p_i:X\to X_i$, $p_{ij}:X\to X_{ij}$ be the projections. Let $L_i\in\operatorname{ob}D_{\mathrm{ctf}}(X_i)$. One has $X=X_{12}\times_{X_2}X_{23}$, and
\[
(DL_1\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}L_2)\otimes_{X_2}^{\mathbf L}(DL_2\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}L_3)
=p_1^*DL_1\otimes^{\mathbf L}p_2^*L_2\otimes^{\mathbf L}p_2^*DL_2\otimes^{\mathbf L}p_3^*L_3.
\]
The evaluation arrow $L_2\otimes^{\mathbf L}DL_2\to K_{X_2}$ therefore defines a pairing
\begin{equation}
\tag{5.2.1}\label{sga5:III:equation:5.2.1}
(DL_1\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}L_2)\otimes_{X_2}^{\mathbf L}(DL_2\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}L_3)\longrightarrow p_{13}^!(DL_1\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}L_3),
\end{equation}
the second member being identified with $(DL_1\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}L_3)\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}K_{X_2}$ by Künneth \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:1.7.3]{(1.7.3)}. Taking \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.1.1]{3.1.1} and \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.1.2]{3.1.2} into account, one may rewrite \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:5.2.1]{5.2.1} in the form
\begin{equation}
\tag{5.2.2}\label{sga5:III:equation:5.2.2}
\RHom_S(L_1,L_2)\otimes_{X_2}^{\mathbf L}\RHom_S(L_2,L_3)\longrightarrow p_{13}^!\RHom_S(L_1,L_3)=\RHom(p_1^*L_1,p_3^!L_3),
\end{equation}
the identification with the second member being given by the induction isomorphism (SGA~4~XVIII 3.1.12.2). Hence one obtains a pairing, called \emph{composition of correspondences},
\begin{equation}
\tag{5.2.3}\label{sga5:III:equation:5.2.3}
\Hom_S(L_1,L_2)\otimes\Hom_S(L_2,L_3)\longrightarrow\Hom(p_1^*L_1,p_3^!L_3),
\end{equation}
which will be denoted $(u,v)\mapsto vu$. One checks that, if $p_{ij,i}:X_{ij}\to X_i$ and $p_{ij,j}:X_{ij}\to X_j$ denote the projections, \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:5.2.2]{5.2.2} can also be obtained by composing the arrow
\[
\RHom_S(L_1,L_2)\otimes_{X_2}^{\mathbf L}\RHom_S(L_2,L_3)
\longrightarrow
\RHom(p_1^*L_1,p_{12}^*p_{12,2}^!L_2)
\otimes
\RHom(p_{23}^!p_{23,2}^*L_2,p_3^!L_3)
\]
(the tensor product of the canonical arrows $p_{12}^*\RHom_S(L_1,L_2)\to\RHom(p_1^*L_1,p_{12}^*p_{12,2}^!L_2)$ and $p_{23}^*\RHom_S(L_2,L_3)\to\RHom(p_{23}^!p_{23,2}^*L_2,p_3^!L_3)$), the arrow defined by the base-change arrow $p_{12}^!p_{12,2}^*L_2\to p_{23}^*p_{23,2}^*L_2$, and a usual composition arrow on the $\RHom$'s.

Let $c':C'\to X_{12}$ and $c'':C''\to X_{23}$ be correspondences, and let $c:C=C'\times_{X_2}C''\to X$ be the ``composite'' correspondence. From \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:5.2.2]{5.2.2} one deduces, or else defines directly by a composition analogous to the one just described, a pairing
\begin{equation}
\tag{5.2.4}\label{sga5:III:equation:5.2.4}
\RHom(c_1'{}^*L_1,c_2'{}^!L_2)\otimes_{X_2}^{\mathbf L}\RHom(c_2''{}^*L_2,c_3''{}^!L_3)
\longrightarrow\RHom(c_1^*L_1,c_3^!L_3),
\end{equation}
hence a ``composition'' pairing $(u,v)\mapsto vu$:
\begin{equation}
\tag{5.2.5}\label{sga5:III:equation:5.2.5}
\Hom(c_1'{}^*L_1,c_2'{}^!L_2)\otimes\Hom(c_2''{}^*L_2,c_3''{}^!L_3)
\longrightarrow\Hom(c_1^*L_1,c_3^!L_3).
\end{equation}

One checks that composition of correspondences is compatible with direct images in the following sense. Let, for $1\leq i\leq 3$, $f_i:X_i\to Y_i$ be an $S$-morphism, with $f_2$ proper, and let $c'$, $c''$, $c$ be morphisms as above, with $c'$ and $c''$ proper. Let $d':D'\to Y_{12}$ and $d'':D''\to Y_{23}$ be proper morphisms, $d:D=D'\times_{Y_2}D''\to Y$, and suppose given the commutative squares
\[
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
X_{12} \arrow[d,"f_{12}"'] & C' \arrow[l] \arrow[d] \\
Y_{12} & D' \arrow[l]
\end{tikzcd}
\qquad
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
X_{23} \arrow[d,"f_{23}"'] & C'' \arrow[l] \arrow[d] \\
Y_{23} & D'' \arrow[l]
\end{tikzcd}
\]
Then one has a commutative square
\begin{equation}
\tag{5.2.6}\label{sga5:III:equation:5.2.6}
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
\Hom(c_1'{}^*L_1,c_2'{}^!L_2)\otimes\Hom(c_2''{}^*L_2,c_3''{}^!L_3) \arrow[r,"{\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:5.2.5]{5.2.5}}"] \arrow[d,"f_{12*}\otimes f_{23*}"']
& \Hom(c_1^*L_1,c_3^!L_3) \arrow[d,"f_{13*}"] \\
\Hom(d_1'{}^*M_1,d_2'{}^!M_2)\otimes\Hom(d_2''{}^*M_2,d_3''{}^!M_3) \arrow[r,"{\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:5.2.5]{5.2.5}}"]
& \Hom(d_1^*M_1,d_3^!M_3),
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation}
where $M_1=f_{1!}L_1$, $M_2=f_{2*}L_2$, and $M_3=f_{3*}L_3$.

Finally, suppose that $X_1=X_3$ and $L_1=L_3$. The fundamental pairing \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.1.2]{4.1.2} may be interpreted as the composite of the pairing
\begin{equation}
\tag{5.2.7}\label{sga5:III:equation:5.2.7}
q_1^*DL_1\otimes^{\mathbf L}q_2^*L_2\otimes^{\mathbf L}q_2^*DL_2\otimes^{\mathbf L}q_1^*L_1
\longrightarrow
q_1^*DL_1\otimes^{\mathbf L}q_2^*K_{X_2}\otimes^{\mathbf L}q_1^*L_1
\end{equation}
defined by $L_2\otimes^{\mathbf L}DL_2\to K_{X_2}$, or, equivalently, induced by \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:5.2.1]{5.2.1} on $X_{12}$ via the diagonal $X_{12}\to X_1\times_SX_2\times_SX_1$ ($q_i:X_{12}\to X_i$ denoting the projection), and the pairing
\begin{equation}
\tag{5.2.8}\label{sga5:III:equation:5.2.8}
q_1^*DL_1\otimes^{\mathbf L}q_2^*K_{X_2}\otimes^{\mathbf L}q_1^*L_1\longrightarrow K_{X_{12}}
\end{equation}
defined by $DL_1\otimes^{\mathbf L}L_1\to K_{X_1}$. It follows that, for $u\in\Hom_S(L_1,L_2)$ and $v\in\Hom_S(L_2,L_1)$, one has, in $H^0(X_1\times_SX_2,K_{X_1\times_SX_2})$,
\begin{equation}
\tag{5.2.9}\label{sga5:III:equation:5.2.9}
\langle u,v\rangle=\langle vu,1\rangle,
\end{equation}
where $1$ denotes the identity correspondence from $L_1$ to $L_1$ on $X_1\times_SX_1$, supported on the diagonal (\hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:3.2]{3.2 b)}), and the cup product in the second member is taken in the sense of \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.2.5]{4.2.5}, taking account of the fact that the fibred product $X\times_{(X_1\times_SX_1)}X_1$ (via $p_{13}:X\to X_{13}=X_1\times_SX_1$ and the diagonal $X_1\to X_1\times_SX_1$) identifies with $X_1\times_SX_2$. Likewise, more generally, if $c':C'\to X_{12}$ and $c'':C''\to X_{21}$ are correspondences, if $c:C=C'\times_{X_2}C''\to X_1\times_SX_2\times_SX_1$ is the composite correspondence, and if $d:C'\times_{X_{12}}C''=D\to X_{12}$ is the intersection correspondence, then, for $u\in\Hom(c_1'{}^*L_1,c_2'{}^!L_2)$ and $v\in\Hom(c_2''{}^*L_2,c_1''{}^!L_1)$, one has the following equality in $H^0(D,K_D)$:
\begin{equation}
\tag{5.2.10}\label{sga5:III:equation:5.2.10}
\langle u,v\rangle=\langle vu,1\rangle,
\end{equation}
where $1$ denotes, as above, the diagonal correspondence, and $D$ is identified with the fibred product of $C\to X_1\times_SX_1$ and the diagonal $X_1\to X_1\times_SX_1$. \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:5.2.9]{Formulas 5.2.9} and \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:5.2.10]{5.2.10} generalize a well-known formula in the case of constant coefficients ([12] 1.3.6 (ii) c)).

\subsection*{5.3. External tensor product of correspondences}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:subsection:5.3}

Let, for $1\leq i\leq 4$, $X_i$ be an $S$-scheme, let $X=X_1\times_SX_2\times_SX_3\times_SX_4$, $X_{ij}=X_i\times_SX_j$, and let $L_i\in\operatorname{ob}D_{\mathrm{ctf}}(X_i)$. The Künneth isomorphism (\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:1.7.6]{1.7.6}, \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.2.4]{2.2.4})
\[
DL_1\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}DL_3\xrightarrow{\sim}D(L_1\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}L_3)
\]
defines an isomorphism on $X$
\begin{equation}
\tag{5.3.1}\label{sga5:III:equation:5.3.1}
(DL_1\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}L_2)\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}(DL_3\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}L_4)
\xrightarrow{\sim}D(L_1\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}L_3)\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}(L_2\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}L_4),
\end{equation}
which, by \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.1.1]{3.1.1}, may be rewritten in the form
\begin{equation}
\tag{5.3.2}\label{sga5:III:equation:5.3.2}
\RHom_S(L_1,L_2)\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}\RHom_S(L_3,L_4)
\xrightarrow{\sim}\RHom_S(L_1\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}L_3,L_2\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}L_4).
\end{equation}
Hence a pairing, called the \emph{external tensor product},
\begin{equation}
\tag{5.3.3}\label{sga5:III:equation:5.3.3}
\Hom_S(L_1,L_2)\otimes\Hom_S(L_3,L_4)
\longrightarrow\Hom_S(L_1\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}L_3,L_2\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}L_4),
\end{equation}
which will be denoted $(u,v)\mapsto u\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}v$. The pairing \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:5.3.2]{5.3.2} may also be interpreted as the composite of the external tensor product
\[
\RHom(E_1,E_2)\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}\RHom(E_3,E_4)
\xrightarrow{\sim}\RHom(E_1\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}E_3,E_2\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}E_4)
\]
(where $E_1=p_{12,1}^*L_1$, etc., with the evident notation) and of the isomorphism defined by Künneth
\[
E_2\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}E_4\xrightarrow{\sim}p_{24}^!(L_2\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}L_4)\quad \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:1.7.3]{(1.7.3)}.
\]

Let $c':C'\to X_{12}$ and $c'':C''\to X_{34}$ be correspondences, and let $c:C=C'\times_SC''\to X=X_{13}\times_SX_{24}$ be the product correspondence. From \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:5.3.2]{5.3.2} one deduces, or else defines analogously, a pairing
\begin{equation}
\tag{5.3.4}\label{sga5:III:equation:5.3.4}
\RHom(c_1'{}^*L_1,c_2'{}^!L_2)\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}\RHom(c_3''{}^*L_3,c_4''{}^!L_4)
\longrightarrow\RHom(c_{13}^*(L_1\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}L_3),c_{24}^!(L_2\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}L_4)),
\end{equation}
hence an external tensor product pairing, $(u,v)\mapsto u\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}v$,
\begin{equation}
\tag{5.3.5}\label{sga5:III:equation:5.3.5}
\Hom(c_1'{}^*L_1,c_2'{}^!L_2)\otimes\Hom(c_3''{}^*L_3,c_4''{}^!L_4)
\longrightarrow\Hom(c_{13}^*(L_1\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}L_3),c_{24}^!(L_2\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}L_4)).
\end{equation}

One checks that these pairings are compatible with direct images, in the following sense. Let, for $1\leq i\leq4$, $f_i:X_i\to Y_i$ be an $S$-morphism, let $c'$, $c''$, $c$ be as above, with $c'$ and $c''$ proper, let $d':D'\to Y_{12}$ and $d'':D''\to Y_{34}$ be proper morphisms, let $d:D=D'\times_SD''\to Y=Y_{12}\times_SY_{34}$, and suppose given the commutative squares
\[
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
X_{12} \arrow[d,"f_{12}"'] & C' \arrow[l] \arrow[d] \\
Y_{12} & D' \arrow[l]
\end{tikzcd}
\qquad
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
X_{34} \arrow[d,"f_{34}"'] & C'' \arrow[l] \arrow[d] \\
Y_{34} & D'' \arrow[l]
\end{tikzcd}
\]
Then one has a commutative square
\begin{equation}
\tag{5.3.6}\label{sga5:III:equation:5.3.6}
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
\Hom(c_1'{}^*L_1,c_2'{}^!L_2)\otimes\Hom(c_3''{}^*L_3,c_4''{}^!L_4) \arrow[r,"{\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:5.3.5]{5.3.5}}"] \arrow[d,"f_{12}\otimes f_{34}"']
& \Hom(c_{13}^*L_{13},c_{24}^!L_{24}) \arrow[d,"f_{13,24*}"] \\
\Hom(d_1'{}^*M_1,d_2'{}^!M_2)\otimes\Hom(d_3''{}^*M_3,d_4''{}^!M_4) \arrow[r,"{\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:5.3.5]{5.3.5}}"]
& \Hom(d_{13}^*M_{13},d_{24}^!M_{24}),
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation}
where $M_i=f_{i!}L_i$ if $i=1,3$ and $f_{i*}L_i$ otherwise, $L_{ij}=L_i\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}L_j$, and $M_{ij}=M_i\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}M_j$ (there is also an analogous square with the $\RHom$'s).

Suppose that $X_3=X_2$, $X_4=X_1$, $L_3=L_2$, and $L_4=L_1$. A reasoning analogous to that of the last paragraph of \hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:5.2]{5.2} shows that, for $u\in\Hom_S(L_1,L_2)$ and $v\in\Hom_S(L_2,L_1)$, one has
\begin{equation}
\tag{5.3.7}\label{sga5:III:equation:5.3.7}
\langle u,v\rangle=\langle u\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}v,1\rangle,
\end{equation}
where $1$ denotes the diagonal correspondence from $L_{12}$ to $L_{12}$ on $X_{12}$, and the cup product in the second member is taken in the sense of \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.2.5]{4.2.5}. More generally, if $c':C'\to X_{12}$ and $c'':C''\to X_{21}$ are correspondences, their ``intersection'' $C=C'\times_{X_{12}}C''$ identifies with the fibred product of $C'\times_SC''\to X=X_{12}\times_SX_{21}$ and the diagonal $X_{12}\to X$, and, for $u\in\Hom(c_1'{}^*L_1,c_2'{}^!L_2)$ and $v\in\Hom(c_2''{}^*L_2,c_1''{}^!L_1)$, one has the following equality in $H^0(C,K_C)$:
\begin{equation}
\tag{5.3.8}\label{sga5:III:equation:5.3.8}
\langle u,v\rangle=\langle u\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}v,1\rangle,
\end{equation}
where $1$ denotes, as above, the diagonal correspondence. Thus there are two ``diagonal'' procedures (\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:5.2.10]{5.2.10}, \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:5.3.8]{5.3.8}) for the calculation of the ``Verdier local term'' $\langle u,v\rangle$.

\section*{6. Appendix. Lefschetz formula for coherent sheaves}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:section:6}

In this number, $S$ denotes a noetherian scheme. The $S$-schemes considered will be assumed separated and of finite type. Recall that, by Nagata, every $S$-morphism between such schemes is compactifiable, i.e. factors as an open immersion followed by a proper morphism. For every $S$-scheme $X$, we shall write $D(X)$ for the derived category of the category of $\cO_X$-modules.

\subsection*{6.1}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:subsection:6.1}
Recall ([8] App.) that for every $S$-morphism $f:X\to Y$ one has a functor
\[
f^!:D^+(Y)\longrightarrow D^+(X)
\]
(and transitivity isomorphisms $(gf)^! = f^!g^!$ for a composite $gf$, with a cocycle condition). If $f$ is proper, $f^!$ is right ``adjoint'' to the functor $f_*:D^+(X)_{\mathrm{coh}}\to D^+(Y)_{\mathrm{coh}}$ (where the subscript ``coh'' denotes the full subcategory formed by complexes with coherent cohomology), this adjunction being made precise by an isomorphism
\begin{equation}
\tag{6.1.1}\label{sga5:III:equation:6.1.1}
f_*\RHom(E,f^!F)\xrightarrow{\sim}\RHom(f_*E,F)
\end{equation}
for $E\in\operatorname{ob}D(X)_{\mathrm{coh}}$, $F\in\operatorname{ob}D^+(Y)$, defined analogously to the isomorphism (SGA~4 XVIII 3.1.9.6). The considerations of ([8] App.5), together with the duality theorem for projective space, show that, if $f$ is smooth, purely of dimension $d$, there is a canonical isomorphism
\begin{equation}
\tag{6.1.2}\label{sga5:III:equation:6.1.2}
f^!L\xleftarrow{\sim} f^*L\otimes\Omega^d_{X/Y}[d];
\end{equation}
and, if moreover $f$ is proper, the arrow
\begin{equation}
\tag{6.1.3}\label{sga5:III:equation:6.1.3}
\operatorname{Tr}_f:f_*(f^*L\otimes\Omega^d_{X/Y}[d])\longrightarrow L
\end{equation}
deduced, by \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.1.2]{6.1.2}, from the adjunction arrow $f_*f^!\to I$, coincides with the trace morphism ([8] VI).

\subsection*{6.2}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:subsection:6.2}
Contrary to what happens in the case of discrete coefficients, there is no reasonable subcategory of $D$ here that is stable under the ``six operations'' $(f^*,f_*,f^!,f_!,\otimes^{\mathbf L},\RHom)$. The most natural finiteness notion seems to be relative perfection (SGA~6 III 4). If $X$ is an $S$-scheme and $L\in\operatorname{ob}D(X)$, recall that $L$ is said to be perfect relative to $S$ if $L$ has finite tor-dimension relative to $S$ and coherent cohomology. We shall denote by $D(X)_{\mathrm{parf}/S}$ the full subcategory of $D(X)$ formed by the complexes perfect relative to $S$. It is not stable in general either under $\otimes^{\mathbf L}$ or under $\RHom$. However, if $E$ is perfect in the absolute sense and $F$ is perfect relative to $S$, then $\RHom(E,F)$ is perfect relative to $S$ (SGA~6 III 4.9). On the other hand, let, for $i=1,2$, $X_i$ be an $S$-scheme and $L_i\in\operatorname{ob}D^{-}(X_i)$. We shall put, as in \hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:1.5]{1.5},
\[
L_1\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}L_2=Lp_1^*L_1\otimes^{\mathbf L}Lp_2^*L_2,
\]
where $p_i:X_1\times_SX_2\to X_i$ is the projection. Considering this object is reasonable only if $X_1$ and $X_2$ are tor-independent over $S$, i.e. (SGA~6 III 1.5) if $\Tor_i^S(\cO_X,\cO_Y)=0$ for $i\ne0$.
