Suppose this is the case: then, if $L_1$ and $L_2$ are perfect relative to $S$, so is $L_1\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}L_2$ (SGA~6 III 4.7).

Let $f:X\to Y$ be an $S$-morphism. If $f$ is proper, then $f_*D(X)_{\mathrm{parf}/S}\subset D(Y)_{\mathrm{parf}/S}$ (SGA~6 III 4.8). If $f$ has finite tor-dimension, then $f^*D(Y)_{\mathrm{parf}/S}\subset D(X)_{\mathrm{parf}/S}$ (SGA~6 III 4.5), and $f^!D(Y)_{\mathrm{parf}/S}\subset D(X)_{\mathrm{parf}/S}$ (SGA~6 III 4.9).

For every $S$-scheme $a:X\to S$, we put
\begin{equation}
\tag{6.2.1}\label{sga5:III:equation:6.2.1}
K_X=a^!\cO_S,
\end{equation}
and write $D_X$ (or simply $D$) for the functor $\RHom(-,K_X)$. If $X$ has finite tor-dimension over $S$, then $K_X$ is perfect relative to $S$ and, for every $L\in\operatorname{ob}D(X)_{\mathrm{parf}/S}$, one has $DL\in\operatorname{ob}D(X)_{\mathrm{parf}/S}$, and the canonical arrow $L\to DDL$ is an isomorphism (SGA~6 III 4.9).

Let $f:X\to Y$ be an $S$-morphism, $L\in\operatorname{ob}D(X)$, and $M\in\operatorname{ob}D(Y)$. If $f^*M$ is defined (for example if $M\in\operatorname{ob}D^-(Y)$, or if $f$ has finite tor-dimension), put
\[
\Hom_f^{(1)}(L,M)=\Hom(f^*M,L),\qquad
\Hom_f^{(2)}(L,M)=\Hom(L,f^*M).
\]
If $f^!M$ is defined (for example if $M\in\operatorname{ob}D^+(Y)$, or if $f$ is smooth, or finite of finite tor-dimension, or a composite of such morphisms, since in these cases $f^!$ extends, by \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.1.1]{6.1.1} and \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.1.2]{6.1.2}, to a functor defined on the whole category $D(Y)$), put
\[
\Hom_f^{(3)}(L,M)=\Hom(L,f^!M),\qquad
\Hom_f^{(4)}(L,M)=\Hom(f^!M,L).
\]
The elements of $\Hom_f^{(i)}(L,M)$ will be called, as in \hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:1.4]{1.4}, \emph{arrows of type $i$ from $L$ to $M$ over $f$} (or $f$-arrows of type $i$ from $L$ to $M$). We leave to the reader the task of making explicit the composition of arrows of type $i$, for fixed $i$, and the corresponding fibred and cofibred categories. We recall only the partial adjunction formulas
\[
\Hom_f^{(1)}(L,M)=\Hom(M,f_*L)\quad(M\in\operatorname{ob}D^{-}(Y),\ L\in\operatorname{ob}D^+(X)),
\]
\[
\Hom_f^{(3)}(L,M)=\Hom(f_*L,M)\quad(f\text{ proper},\ L\in\operatorname{ob}D(X)_{\mathrm{coh}},\ M\in\operatorname{ob}D^+(Y)).
\]

\subsection*{6.3}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:subsection:6.3}
In the situation of \hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:1.6]{1.6}, suppose that $X_1$ and $X_2$ are tor-independent over $S$, and likewise $Y_1$ and $Y_2$. Then, for $L_i\in\operatorname{ob}D(X_i)_{\mathrm{parf}/S}$, the Künneth arrow \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:1.6.4]{1.6.4} is defined (use the fact that finite relative tor-dimension is preserved by direct image (SGA~6 III 3.7.1)) and is an isomorphism (to verify this, one may assume $S$ and the $Y_i$ affine, and, for a Čech calculation, reduce to also assuming $X_1$ and $X_2$ affine; the conclusion is then a trivial consequence of the tor-independence hypotheses). On the other hand, for $M_i\in\operatorname{ob}D(Y_i)_{\mathrm{parf}/S}$, with $f_1^!M_1$ or $f_2^!M_2$ in $D^b$, the Künneth arrow \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:1.7.3]{1.7.3} is defined\footnote{In the proper or lissifiable case, the definition of the arrow is immediate; the general case can be reduced to the lissifiable case by cohomological descent.}, and is an isomorphism when $f_1$ and $f_2$ have finite tor-dimension, as is easily checked with the help of the Künneth isomorphism of type \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:1.6.4]{1.6.4} just indicated, conjugated with \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.1.2]{6.1.2}, and with \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.1.1]{6.1.1} in the case where $f_i$ is a closed immersion. In particular, if $X_1$ and $X_2$ have finite tor-dimension over $S$ and are tor-independent, \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:1.7.6]{1.7.6} is an isomorphism.

One defines $\RHom(u,v)$ as in \hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:2.1]{2.1} for $(u,v)$ of type $(2,1)$ (respectively $(3,4)$, with $f$ proper in order to avoid the pro-objects caused by an $f_!$). Under suitable degree hypotheses (see for example ([9] V 2.3)), the evaluation arrows \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.2.2]{(2.2.2)} and tensor-product arrows (\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.2.3]{2.2.3}, \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.2.4]{2.2.4}) are defined, and the arrows \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.2.3]{2.2.3} and \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.2.4]{2.2.4} (provided they are defined) are isomorphisms as soon as $E_1$ and $E_2$ are perfect in the absolute sense.

In the situation of \hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:2.4]{2.4}, suppose that the pairs $(X_1,X_2)$, $(X_1,Y_2)$, $(Y_1,X_2)$, $(Y_1,Y_2)$ are tor-independent over $S$, that $f_1$ is proper, that $E_i,F_i$, $P_i,Q_i$, $\RHom(E_i,F_i)$, $\RHom(P_i,Q_i)$ are perfect relative to $S$, and that the Künneth arrows
\[
f_1^!Q_1\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}Q_2\to(f_1\times_SY_2)^!(Q_1\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}Q_2),\qquad
f_1^!Q_1\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}F_2\to(f_1\times_SY_2)^!(Q_1\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}F_2)
\]
are isomorphisms. Then \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.4.1]{2.4.1} and the diagram of \hyperref[sga5:III:proposition:2.5]{2.5} are defined, and the latter is commutative (however, the horizontal arrows are not necessarily isomorphisms). The verification is the same as for \hyperref[sga5:III:proposition:2.5]{2.5}.

\subsection*{6.4}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:subsection:6.4}
Let, for $i=1,2$, $X_i$ be an $S$-scheme, let $X=X_1\times_SX_2$, and let $p_i:X\to X_i$, $q_i:X_i\to S$ be the canonical projections. Suppose that $X_1$ and $X_2$ are tor-independent and that $q_1$ has finite tor-dimension. Let $L_i\in\operatorname{ob}D(X_i)_{\mathrm{parf}/S}$. By the analogue of \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.2.4]{2.2.4}, one has a canonical arrow
\begin{equation}
\tag{6.4.1}\label{sga5:III:equation:6.4.1}
DL_1\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}L_2\longrightarrow \RHom(L_1\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}\cO_{X_2},q_1^!\cO_S\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}L_2),
\end{equation}
and hence, by composing with the Künneth isomorphism \hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:6.3]{(6.3)} $q_1^!\cO_S\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}L_2\xrightarrow{\sim}p_2^!L_2$, an arrow
\begin{equation}
\tag{6.4.2}\label{sga5:III:equation:6.4.2}
DL_1\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}L_2\longrightarrow\RHom(p_1^*L_1,p_2^!L_2).
\end{equation}
The arrow \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.4.1]{6.4.1}, and therefore also \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.4.2]{6.4.2}, is an isomorphism, as one sees by reducing to the case where $q_1$ is a closed immersion.

As in \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.1.2]{3.1.2}, we shall put
\begin{equation}
\tag{6.4.3}\label{sga5:III:equation:6.4.3}
\RHom(p_1^*L_1,p_2^!L_2)=\RHom_S(L_1,L_2),\qquad
\Hom(p_1^*L_1,p_2^!L_2)=\Hom_S(L_1,L_2),
\end{equation}
and call the elements of $\Hom_S(L_1,L_2)$ \emph{cohomological correspondences from $L_1$ to $L_2$}. If $c:C\to X$ is a correspondence (proper in practice), then by the induction formula ([8] III 8.8)\footnote{The ``lissifiability'' hypothesis of (loc. cit.) is unnecessary.}
\begin{equation}
\tag{6.4.4}\label{sga5:III:equation:6.4.4}
c^!\RHom(p_1^*L_1,p_2^!L_2)=\RHom(c_1^*L_1,c_2^!L_2),
\end{equation}
and the elements of
\[
\Hom(c_1^*L_1,c_2^!L_2)=H^0(X,c_*c^!\RHom(p_1^*L_1,p_2^!L_2))
\]
will be called \emph{cohomological correspondences from $L_1$ to $L_2$ with support in $C$}. We shall see below examples analogous to those of \hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:3.2]{3.2}.

\subsection*{6.5}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:subsection:6.5}
Let, for $i=1,2$, $f_i:X_i\to Y_i$ be a proper $S$-morphism, and let $f=f_1\times_Sf_2:X\to Y$. Suppose that the $X_i$ and $Y_i$ have finite tor-dimension over $S$, and that the squares of \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:2.4.0]{2.4.0} are tor-independent. Let $L_i\in\operatorname{ob}D(X_i)_{\mathrm{parf}/S}$. As in \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.3.1]{3.3.1}, one defines an isomorphism
\begin{equation}
\tag{6.5.1}\label{sga5:III:equation:6.5.1}
f_*\RHom_S(L_1,L_2)\xrightarrow{\sim}\RHom_S(f_{1*}L_1,f_{2*}L_2),
\end{equation}
hence, by applying $R\Gamma(Y,-)$, an isomorphism
\begin{equation}
\tag{6.5.2}\label{sga5:III:equation:6.5.2}
f_*:\Hom_S(L_1,L_2)\xrightarrow{\sim}\Hom_S(f_{1*}L_1,f_{2*}L_2),
\end{equation}
which we shall simply denote by $u\mapsto u_*$ when $Y_1=Y_2=S$. There is a commutative diagram analogous to \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.4.1]{3.4.1}, as follows from the analogue of \hyperref[sga5:III:proposition:2.5]{2.5} indicated in \hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:6.3]{6.3}. One deduces from it a corollary analogous to \hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:6.5]{6.5} and, in the case where $Y_1=Y_2=S$ is the spectrum of a field, a description, analogous to that of \hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:3.6]{3.6}, of the operations $u_*$ on cohomology.

Given a commutative square \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.7.1]{3.7.1}, with $c$ proper, one has, for $K\in\operatorname{ob}D^+(C)$, a canonical arrow
\begin{equation}
\tag{6.5.3}\label{sga5:III:equation:6.5.3}
f_*c_*c^!K\longrightarrow d_*d^!f_*K,
\end{equation}
defined in the same way as \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.7.3]{3.7.3} (the isomorphism $g_*'c'^!\xrightarrow{\sim}d^!f_*$ is also valid in the present context: definition of the arrow by transposition of a base-change arrow, and verification that it is an isomorphism by reduction to the cases where $d$ is smooth and where $d$ is a closed immersion). Taking $K=\RHom_S(L_1,L_2)$, one deduces from \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.5.3]{6.5.3} and \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.5.1]{6.5.1} canonical arrows
\begin{equation}
\tag{6.5.4}\label{sga5:III:equation:6.5.4}
f_*c_*\RHom(c_1^*L_1,c_2^!L_2)\longrightarrow d_*\RHom(d_1^*(f_{1*}L_1),d_2^!(f_{2*}L_2)),
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\tag{6.5.5}\label{sga5:III:equation:6.5.5}
f_*:\Hom(c_1^*L_1,c_2^!L_2)\longrightarrow\Hom(d_1^*(f_{1*}L_1),d_2^!(f_{2*}L_2)),
\end{equation}
analogous to \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.7.5]{3.7.5} and \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.7.6]{3.7.6}.

\subsection*{6.6}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:subsection:6.6}
Let, for $i=1,2$, $X_i$ be $S$-schemes of finite tor-dimension and tor-independent over $S$, and let $L_i\in\operatorname{ob}D(X_i)_{\mathrm{parf}/S}$. The external tensor product of the evaluation arrows $DL_i\otimes^{\mathbf L}L_i\to K_{X_i}$ defines, as in \hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:4.1]{4.1}, ``cup-product'' pairings analogous to \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.1.2]{4.1.2}, \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.1.3]{4.1.3}, and \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.1.4]{4.1.4}.

Let $c:C\to X$ and $d:D\to X$ be proper morphisms, let $e=c\times_Xd:E\to X$, and let $P,Q\in\operatorname{ob}D(X)_{\mathrm{parf}/S}$. By \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.1.1]{6.1.1}, one has
\[
c_*c^!P=\RHom(Rc_*\cO_C,P)
\]
(and an analogous isomorphism with $d$). Assuming $c$ and $d$ of finite tor-dimension at the points above $e(E)$, one deduces, by tensor product, a canonical arrow
\begin{equation}
\tag{6.6.1}\label{sga5:III:equation:6.6.1}
c_*c^!P\otimes^{\mathbf L}d_*d^!Q\longrightarrow
\RHom(Rc_*\cO_C\otimes^{\mathbf L}Rd_*\cO_D,P\otimes^{\mathbf L}Q).
\end{equation}
When $c$ and $d$ are tor-independent, the second member is rewritten, by Künneth and \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.1.1]{6.1.1}, as $e_*e^!(P\otimes^{\mathbf L}Q)$, and \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.6.1]{6.6.1} takes the form \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.2.3]{4.2.3}. For $P=DL_1\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}L_2$, $Q=L_1\otimes_S^{\mathbf L}DL_2$, one deduces from \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.6.1]{6.6.1} and the pairings indicated above pairings analogous to \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.2.4]{4.2.4} and \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.2.5]{4.2.5}:
\begin{equation}
\tag{6.6.2}\label{sga5:III:equation:6.6.2}
c_*\RHom(c_1^*L_1,c_2^!L_2)\otimes^{\mathbf L}d_*\RHom(d_2^*L_2,d_1^!L_1)
\longrightarrow\RHom(Rc_*\cO_C\otimes^{\mathbf L}Rd_*\cO_D,K_X),
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\tag{6.6.3}\label{sga5:III:equation:6.6.3}
\langle\ ,\ \rangle:\Hom(c_1^*L_1,c_2^!L_2)\otimes\Hom(d_2^*L_2,d_1^!L_1)
\longrightarrow\Hom(Rc_*\cO_C\otimes^{\mathbf L}Rd_*\cO_D,K_X).
\end{equation}
Just like the pairings of \hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:4.2]{4.2}, these are compatible with étale localization. When $c$ and $d$ are tor-independent, the second member of \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.6.2]{6.6.2} (respectively \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.6.3]{6.6.3}) identifies canonically with $e_*K_E$ (respectively $H^0(E,K_E)$).

\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:theorem:4}Theorem \hyperref[sga5:III:theorem:4.4]{4.4} has the following analogue:

\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:theorem:6.7}\begin{statement}{Theorem 6.7}
Let, for $i=1,2$, $f_i:X_i\to Y_i$ be a proper $S$-morphism, let $f=f_1\times_Sf_2:X\to Y$, and let $p_i:X\to X_i$, $q_i:Y\to Y_i$ be the projections. Suppose given, on the other hand, a cube \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.4.0]{4.4.0} with cartesian horizontal faces, with $c'$, $c''$, $d'$, $d''$ proper, and with $c'$, $c''$ (respectively $d'$, $d''$) of finite tor-dimension at the points above $c(C)$ (respectively $d(D)$). Put $c_i'=p_ic'$, $c_i''=p_ic''$, $d_i'=q_id'$, and $d_i''=q_id''$. Finally, let $L_i\in\operatorname{ob}D(X_i)_{\mathrm{parf}/S}$. Assume that the pairs $(X_1,X_2)$, $(X_1,Y_2)$, $(Y_1,X_2)$, $(Y_1,Y_2)$ are tor-independent over $S$, and that $X_i$ and $Y_i$ have finite tor-dimension over $S$. Then one has a commutative square
\begin{equation}
\tag{6.7.1}\label{sga5:III:equation:6.7.1}
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=small,row sep=large]
f_*c'_*\RHom(c_1'{}^*L_1,c_2'{}^!L_2)\otimes^{\mathbf L} f_*c''_*\RHom(c_2''{}^*L_2,c_1''{}^!L_1) \arrow[r,"(1)"] \arrow[d,"(2)"']
& f_*\RHom(Rc'_*\cO_{C'}\otimes^{\mathbf L}Rc''_*\cO_{C''},K_X) \arrow[d,"(4)"] \\
d'_*\RHom(d_1'{}^*M_1,d_2'{}^!M_2)\otimes^{\mathbf L} d''_*\RHom(d_2''{}^*M_2,d_1''{}^!M_1) \arrow[r,"(3)"]
& \RHom(Rd'_*\cO_{D'}\otimes^{\mathbf L}Rd''_*\cO_{D''},K_Y),
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation}
where $M_i=f_{i*}L_i$, $(3)$ is given by \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.6.2]{6.6.2}, $(1)$ is deduced from \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.6.2]{6.6.2} by application of $f_*$ and composition with the canonical arrow $f_*c'_*\otimes^{\mathbf L} f_*c''_*\to f_*(c'_*\otimes^{\mathbf L} c''_*)$, $(2)$ is the tensor product of the arrows \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.5.4]{6.5.4} relative to the faces containing $f$, and $(4)$ is the composite of the duality isomorphism \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.1.1]{6.1.1} and of the arrow defined by the canonical arrow
\[
Rd'_*\cO_{D'}\otimes^{\mathbf L}Rd''_*\cO_{D''}\longrightarrow f_*(Rc'_*\cO_{C'}\otimes^{\mathbf L}Rc''_*\cO_{C''})
\]
which is adjoint to the tensor product of the base-change arrows $f^*Rd'_*\cO_{D'}\to Rc'_*\cO_{C'}$, $f^*Rd''_*\cO_{D''}\to Rc''_*\cO_{C''}$.
\end{statement}

The proof is essentially the same as that of \hyperref[sga5:III:theorem:4.4]{4.4}; we leave to the reader the small modifications to be made.

When $c'$ and $c''$ are tor-independent, and likewise $d'$ and $d''$, the arrow (4) of \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.7.1]{6.7.1} is rewritten in the form $f_*c_*K_C\to d_*K_D$; it is simply defined by the adjunction arrow $g_*K_C=g_*g^!K_D\to K_D$ (as is arrow (4) of \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.4.1]{4.4.1}). However, it would not be natural to consider only this case, since that would exclude correspondences with excess intersection.

From \hyperref[sga5:III:theorem:6.7]{6.7} one deduces formulas analogous to \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.5.1]{4.5.1} and \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.7.1]{4.7.1}: under the hypotheses of \hyperref[sga5:III:theorem:6.7]{6.7}, one has, for $u\in\Hom(c_1'{}^*L_1,c_2'{}^!L_2)$ and $v\in\Hom(c_2''{}^*L_2,c_1''{}^!L_1)$,
\begin{equation}
\tag{6.7.2}\label{sga5:III:equation:6.7.2}
\langle f_*u,f_*v\rangle=f_*\langle u,v\rangle,
\end{equation}
where $f_*u$ (respectively $f_*v$) denotes the direct image correspondence of $u$ (respectively $v$) in the sense of \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.5.5]{6.5.5}, and $f_*$ in the second member is the homomorphism
\begin{equation}
\tag{6.7.3}\label{sga5:III:equation:6.7.3}
\Hom(Rc'_*\cO_{C'}\otimes^{\mathbf L}Rc''_*\cO_{C''},K_X)
\longrightarrow
\Hom(Rd'_*\cO_{D'}\otimes^{\mathbf L}Rd''_*\cO_{D''},K_Y)
\end{equation}
which is the composite of the duality isomorphism \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.1.1]{6.1.1} and the arrow induced by the canonical arrow
\[
Rd'_*\cO_{D'}\otimes^{\mathbf L}Rd''_*\cO_{D''}\longrightarrow f_*(Rc'_*\cO_{C'}\otimes^{\mathbf L}Rc''_*\cO_{C''})
\]
cited in \hyperref[sga5:III:theorem:6.7]{6.7}. When $Y_1=Y_2=S=D'=D''$, \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.7.2]{6.7.2} is the \emph{Lefschetz-Verdier formula}.

As an illustration, we shall show that this formula implies the Woods Hole formula [1].

\subsection*{6.8}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:subsection:6.8}
For this purpose we shall need a few reminders on the ``Hodge'' cohomology class associated with a regularly embedded subscheme of a smooth scheme and on Grothendieck residues ([8] III 9). Let $f:X\to S$ be a smooth morphism purely of relative dimension $N$, and let $i:Y\hookrightarrow X$ be a regular immersion of codimension $d$, i.e. ((SGA~IV 16.9.2), (SGA~6 VII 1)) a closed subscheme defined locally by a regular sequence of $d$ equations, or, in equivalent terminology ((8 III 1), (SGA~6 VIII 1)), a closed immersion, locally a complete intersection, of codimension $d$. Put $g=fi$. Then one associates with $Y$ an element
\begin{equation}
\tag{6.8.1}\label{sga5:III:equation:6.8.1}
\cl^d_{X/S}(Y)\in\Ext^d_{\cO_X}(\cO_Y,\Omega^d_{X/S}),
\end{equation}
called the \emph{cohomology class associated with $Y$}, defined as follows. By the ``purity'' theorem ([8] III 7.2), one has
\begin{equation}
\tag{6.8.2}\label{sga5:III:equation:6.8.2}
\Ext^i_{\cO_X}(\cO_Y,\Omega^d_{X/S})=
\begin{cases}
0,& i\ne d,\\
\Hom(\bigwedge^d N_{Y/X},\Omega^d_{X/S}\otimes\cO_Y),& i=d,
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
where $N_{Y/X}=I/I^2$ is the conormal sheaf ($I=\Ker\,\cO_X\to\cO_Y$). Therefore
\begin{equation}
\tag{6.8.3}\label{sga5:III:equation:6.8.3}
\Ext^d_{\cO_X}(\cO_Y,\Omega^d_{X/S})=H^0(Y,\underline{\Hom}(\bigwedge^dN_{Y/X},\Omega^d_{X/S}\otimes\cO_Y)).
\end{equation}

The differential $d_{X/S}:\OO_X\to\Omega^1_{X/S}$ induces an $\OO_Y$-linear map
\[
d_{X/S}\otimes 1:N_{Y/X}\longrightarrow \Omega^1_{X/S}\otimes\OO_Y.
\]
The $d$-th exterior power of this map is a global section, on $Y$, of $\Lambda^d N_{Y/X}^{\vee}\otimes\Omega^d_{X/S}$; its image in $\Ext^d_{\OO_X}(\OO_Y,\Omega^d_{X/S})$ under the isomorphism \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.8.3]{6.8.3} is, by definition, the class \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.8.1]{6.8.1}.

Assume $g$ finite and flat (hence $d=N$). Let $\omega\in H^0(Y,\Lambda^d N_{Y/X}^{\vee}\otimes\Omega^d_{X/S})$. By \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.8.2]{6.8.2} and \hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:6.1]{6.1}, one may view $\omega$ as a homomorphism
\[
\OO_Y\longrightarrow g^!\OO_S(=\Lambda^d N_{Y/X}^{\vee}\otimes\Omega^d_{X/S}),
\]
and therefore, by adjunction, as a homomorphism $g_*\OO_Y\to\OO_S$. The residue (relative to $g$) of $\omega$ is defined as the section of $\OO_S$ which is the image of $1$ under this homomorphism, and is denoted $\operatorname{Res}_{Y/S}(\omega)$ (cf. ([8] III 9)). Let, on the other hand, $i_*\omega\in H^N(X,\Omega^N_{X/S})$ denote the image of $\omega$ by the composite of the isomorphism \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.8.3]{6.8.3} and the canonical arrow
\[
\Ext^N_{\OO_X}(\OO_Y,\Omega^N_{X/S})\longrightarrow H^N(X,\Omega^N_{X/S}).
\]
When $f$ is proper, it follows from transitivity for trace morphisms (or adjunction arrows) $i_*i^!\to 1$, $f_*f^!\to 1$, that
\begin{equation}
\tag{6.8.4}\label{sga5:III:equation:6.8.4}
\operatorname{Res}_{Y/S}(\omega)=\operatorname{Tr}_f(i_*\omega),
\end{equation}
where $\operatorname{Tr}_f:H^N(X,\Omega^N_{X/S})\to H^0(S,\OO_S)$ is defined by the adjunction arrow $f_*f^!\OO_S=Rf_*\Omega^N_{X/S}[N]\to\OO_S$ \hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:6.1]{(6.1)}. On the other hand, without any properness hypothesis, one has the important compatibility ([8] III 9 R6)
\begin{equation}
\tag{6.8.5}\label{sga5:III:equation:6.8.5}
\operatorname{Res}_{Y/S}(y\cl_{X/S}(Y))=\operatorname{Tr}_{Y/S}(y),
\end{equation}
for $y\in H^0(Y,\OO_Y)$, where $\operatorname{Tr}_{Y/S}:g_*\OO_Y\to\OO_S$ denotes the trace in the classical sense, i.e. $\operatorname{Tr}_{Y/S}(y)$ is the trace of the endomorphism of $g_*\OO_Y$ defined by multiplication by $y$ (this compatibility is not proved in loc. cit.; the case $N=1$ is treated in [Raynaud, Anneaux locaux henséliens, VII 1, Lecture Notes in Math. n\textsuperscript{o} 169, Springer Verlag, 1970]). In other words, $\operatorname{Tr}_{Y/S}$ corresponds, by adjunction, to the homomorphism $\OO_Y\to g^!\OO_S$ defined by multiplication by $\cl_{X/S}(Y)$. It follows that, for $F\in\ob D(S)_{\coh}$, the arrow
\begin{equation}
\tag{6.8.6}\label{sga5:III:equation:6.8.6}
g^*F\longrightarrow g^!F=g^*F\otimes^{\LL}g^!\OO_S
\end{equation}
given by multiplication by $\cl_{X/S}(Y)$ corresponds by adjunction to the arrow
\begin{equation}
\tag{6.8.7}\label{sga5:III:equation:6.8.7}
g_*g^*F=F\otimes^{\LL}g_*\OO_Y\longrightarrow F
\end{equation}
defined by $\operatorname{Tr}_{Y/S}:g_*\OO_Y\to\OO_S$.


\subsection*{6.9}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:subsection:6.9}

Let $X$ (respectively $Y$) be a proper smooth $S$-scheme of relative dimension $m$ (respectively $n$), $Z=X\times_S Y$, and let $a:A\hookrightarrow Z$, $b:B\hookrightarrow Z$ be regular immersions such that $a_2:A\to Y$, $b_1:B\to X$ are finite and flat (write $p_1:Z\to X$, $p_2:Z\to Y$ for the projections, and $a_i=p_i a$, $b_i=p_i b$). Let $c:C=A\times_Z B\to Z$, $L\in\ob D(X)_{\parf}$, $M\in\ob D(Y)_{\parf}$ (the subscript parf means ``perfect in the absolute sense'', which is also equivalent to being perfect rel. to $S$, since $X$ and $Y$ are smooth). Assume that $C$ is finite and flat over $S$. The hypotheses made therefore imply that $c$ is a regular immersion and that $a$ and $b$ are tor-independent.

One has $p_1^*\Omega^1_{X/S}=\Omega^1_{Z/Y}$, $p_2^*\Omega^1_{Y/S}=\Omega^1_{Z/X}$, and a decomposition
\[
\Omega^1_{Z/S}=\Omega^1_{Z/Y}\oplus\Omega^1_{Z/X},
\]
whence, for every integer $r$, a decomposition
\begin{equation}
\tag{6.9.0}\label{sga5:III:equation:6.9.0}
\Omega^r_{Z/S}=\bigoplus_{i+j=r}\Omega^i_{Z/Y}\otimes\Omega^j_{Z/X}.
\end{equation}
On the other hand, by \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.1.2]{6.1.2}, one has $p_2^!M=p_2^*M\otimes\Omega^m_{Z/Y}[m]$, hence
\[
H^0(Z,a_*a^!\RHom(p_1^*L,p_2^!M))=\Ext^m_{\OO_Z}(a_1^*L,a_2^*M\otimes\Omega^m_{Z/Y}),
\]
whence, by \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.4.4]{6.4.4} and \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.8.2]{6.8.2}, an isomorphism
\begin{equation}
\tag{6.9.1}\label{sga5:III:equation:6.9.1}
\Hom(a_1^*L,a_2^!M)=H^0(A,\RHom(a_1^*L,a_2^*M)\otimes\Lambda^m N_{A/Z}^{\vee}\otimes\Omega^m_{Z/Y}).
\end{equation}
Similarly one has an isomorphism
\begin{equation}
\tag{6.9.2}\label{sga5:III:equation:6.9.2}
\Hom(b_2^*M,b_1^!L)=H^0(B,\RHom(b_2^*M,b_1^*L)\otimes\Lambda^n N_{B/Z}^{\vee}\otimes\Omega^n_{Z/X}).
\end{equation}
Let $u\in\Hom(a_1^*L,a_2^*M)$, $v\in\Hom(b_2^*M,b_1^*L)$, and write
\begin{equation}
\tag{6.9.3}\label{sga5:III:equation:6.9.3}
\langle u,v\rangle\in H^0(C,\OO_C)
\end{equation}
for the section $\operatorname{Tr}(u_Cv_C)=\operatorname{Tr}(v_Cu_C)$, where $u_C\in\Hom(c_1^*L,c_2^*M)$ and $v_C\in\Hom(c_2^*M,c_1^*L)$ are induced by $u$ and $v$. Consider the cohomological correspondence $u^!\in\Hom(a_1^*L,a_2^!M)$ (respectively $v^!\in\Hom(b_2^*M,b_1^!L)$) deduced from $u$ (respectively $v$) by composition with the trace homomorphism \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.8.7]{6.8.7} $a_{2*}a_2^*M\to M$ (respectively $b_{1*}b_1^*L\to L$), or equivalently \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.8.6]{(6.8.6)} by product with $\cl_{Z/Y}(A)$ (respectively $\cl_{Z/X}(B)$). Note in passing that $\cl_{Z/Y}(A)$ (respectively $\cl_{Z/X}(B)$) is the component in $H^0(A,\Lambda^mN_{A/Z}^{\vee}\otimes\Omega^m_{Z/Y})$ (respectively $H^0(B,\Lambda^nN_{B/Z}^{\vee}\otimes\Omega^n_{Z/X})$) of $\cl_{Z/S}(A)$ (respectively $\cl_{Z/S}(B)$) defined by the projection $\Omega^m_{Z/S}\to\Omega^m_{Z/Y}$ (respectively $\Omega^n_{Z/S}\to\Omega^n_{Z/X}$) of \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.9.0]{6.9.0}. One checks easily that the cup-product
\[
\langle u^!,v^!\rangle\in\Ext^{m+n}_{\OO_Z}(\OO_C,\Omega^{m+n}_{Z/S})=H^0(C,\Lambda^{m+n}N_{C/Z}^{\vee}\otimes\Omega^{m+n}_{Z/S})
\]
defined in \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.6.3]{6.6.3} is given by
\begin{equation}
\tag{6.9.4}\label{sga5:III:equation:6.9.4}
\langle u^!,v^!\rangle=\langle u,v\rangle\cl_{Z/Y}(A)\cl_{Z/X}(B).
\end{equation}
On the other hand one checks that the homomorphism \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.7.3]{6.7.3}
\[
\Ext^{m+n}_{\OO_Z}(\OO_C,\Omega^{m+n}_{Z/S})\longrightarrow H^0(S,\OO_S)
\]
is the composite of the canonical arrow $\Ext^{m+n}_{\OO_Z}(\OO_C,\Omega^{m+n}_{Z/S})\to H^{m+n}(Z,\Omega^{m+n}_{Z/S})$ and the trace morphism $\operatorname{Tr}_{Z/S}:H^{m+n}(Z,\Omega^{m+n}_{Z/S})\to H^0(S,\OO_S)$. The Lefschetz-Verdier \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.7.2]{formula 6.7.2} therefore gives the relation
\begin{equation}
\tag{6.9.5}\label{sga5:III:equation:6.9.5}
\langle (u^!)_*,(v^!)_*\rangle=\operatorname{Tr}_{Z/S}(\langle u,v\rangle\cl_{Z/Y}(A)\cl_{Z/X}(B)).
\end{equation}
Thanks to \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.8.4]{6.8.4} one may rewrite the second member as a residue; thus, in summary, one has obtained:


\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:theorem:6.10}\begin{statement}{Theorem 6.10}
Under the hypotheses of the first paragraph of \hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:6.9]{6.9}, let $u\in\Hom(a_1^*L,a_2^*M)$, $v\in\Hom(b_2^*M,b_1^*L)$, and consider the cohomological correspondence $u^!$ (respectively $v^!$) from $L$ to $M$ (respectively from $M$ to $L$), with support in $A$ (respectively $B$), deduced from $u$ (respectively $v$) by composition with the trace $a_{2*}a_2^*M\to M$ (respectively $b_{1*}b_1^*L\to L$) \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.8.7]{(6.8.7)}, and the homomorphism $(u^!)_*:f_*L\to g_*M$ (respectively $(v^!)_*:g_*M\to f_*L$) deduced from $u^!$ (respectively $v^!$) by direct image \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.5.5]{(6.5.5)} ($f:X\to S$, $g:Y\to S$ denoting the projections). Then, with the notation of \hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:6.8]{6.8}, \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.9.3]{6.9.3}, one has
\begin{equation}
\tag{6.10.1}\label{sga5:III:equation:6.10.1}
\langle (u^!)_*,(v^!)_*\rangle=\operatorname{Res}_{C/S}(\langle u,v\rangle\cl_{Z/Y}(A)\cl_{Z/X}(B)).
\end{equation}
\end{statement}

\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:remark:6.11}\begin{statement}{Remarks 6.11}
a) As in \hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:3.6]{3.6}, using the description \hyperref[sga5:III:corollary:3.5]{3.5 b)} of the direct image of a correspondence, one checks that, if $h:Z\to S$ is the projection, $(u^!)_*$ (respectively $(v^!)_*$) is the composite of the arrows
\[
f_*L\xrightarrow{(1)}(ha)_*a_1^*L\xrightarrow{(ha)_*u}(ha)_*a_2^*M=g_*a_{2*}a_2^*M\xrightarrow{(2)}g_*M,
\]
\[
\text{(respectively }g_*M\xrightarrow{(1)}(hb)_*b_2^*M\xrightarrow{(hb)_*v}(hb)_*b_1^*L=f_*b_{1*}b_1^*L\xrightarrow{(2)}f_*L\text{)},
\]
where (1) is the usual functoriality arrow (defined by the adjunction arrow $1\to a_{1*}a_1^*$ (respectively $1\to b_{2*}b_2^*$)) and (2) is given by the trace $a_{2*}a_2^*M\to M$ (respectively $b_{1*}b_1^*L\to L$) \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.8.7]{(6.8.7)}.

b) Suppose that $S$ is the spectrum of a field. The intersection $C=A\times_{(X\times_S Y)}B$ is then formed of isolated points. At each point $z$ of $C$, choose, near the images of $z$, regular sequences $(s_1,\ldots,s_m)$, $(t_1,\ldots,t_n)$ of equations of $A,B$. Then \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.10.1]{6.10.1} is written, with the notation of the residue symbols of ([8] III 9), as
\begin{equation}
\tag{6.11.1}\label{sga5:III:equation:6.11.1}
\langle (u^!)_*,(v^!)_*\rangle=
\sum_{z\in C}\operatorname{Res}_z\left(\langle u,v\rangle
\prod_{1\le i\le m}\frac{d_{Z/Y}s_i}{s_i}
\prod_{1\le j\le n}\frac{d_{Z/X}t_j}{t_j}\right),
\end{equation}
where $d_{Z/Y}s_i$ (respectively $d_{Z/X}t_j$) denotes the component of type $(1,0)$ (respectively $(0,1)$) of $d_{Z/S}s_i$ (respectively $d_{Z/S}t_j$) in the decomposition \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.9.0]{6.9.0}.
\end{statement}

\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:corollary:6.12}\begin{statement}{Corollary 6.12}
(Woods-Hole formula [1]). Assume that $S$ is the spectrum of a field. Let $X$ be a proper smooth $S$-scheme, $F$ an $S$-endomorphism of $X$, $L$ an object of $D(X)_{\parf}$ (i.e. a perfect complex on $X$), and $u\in\Hom(F^*L,L)$. Assume that the scheme of fixed points $X^F$ is finite and consists of transverse points (i.e. points where the graph of $F$ meets the diagonal transversely). Then
\[
\sum_i(-1)^i\operatorname{Tr}((F,u),H^i(X,L))=
\sum_{x\in X^F}\frac{\operatorname{Tr}(u_x)}{\det(1-dF_x)},
\]
where $(F,u)$ is the endomorphism of $H^i(X,L)$ which is the composite of $H^i(X,L)\to H^i(X,F^*L)$ (inverse image) and $H^i(X,u)$, $\operatorname{Tr}(u_x)$ denotes the value at $x$ of the endomorphism induced by $u$ on the fibre of $L$ at $x$, and $\det(1-dF_x)$ is the value at $x$ of the determinant of the map $(1-\text{map induced by }F\text{ on }\Omega^1_{X,x})$.

\emph{Proof.} Apply \hyperref[sga5:III:theorem:6.10]{6.10}, taking account of \hyperref[sga5:III:remark:6.11]{6.11} a), b). Put $Z=X\times_S X$. Let $x\in X^F$. Near $x$, choose a system of local coordinates $(x_1,\ldots,x_n)$ (defining an étale morphism into $\mathbb A_S^n$), put $F_i=x_iF$, and write $(x_1,\ldots,x_n,y_1,\ldots,y_n)$ for the corresponding local coordinates on $Z$ near $z=(x,x)$ ($x_i=x_i\otimes1$, $y_i=1\otimes x_i$). Then, near $z$, $(x_1-y_1,\ldots,x_n-y_n)$ (respectively $(y_1-F_1,\ldots,y_n-F_n)$) is a regular system of equations for the diagonal $A$ (respectively for the graph $B$ of $F$). The contribution at $z$ of the second member of \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.11.1]{6.11.1} is
\[
\langle u,1\rangle_z=
\operatorname{Res}_z\left(\operatorname{Tr}(u)\frac{dx_1\cdots dx_n\,dy_1\cdots dy_n}{(x_1-y_1)\cdots(x_n-y_n)(y_1-F_1)\cdots(y_n-F_n)}\right).
\]
Observe that in the numerator one may replace $dy_i$ by
\[
dy_i-dF_i=dy_i-\sum_{1\le j\le n}(dF_i/dx_j)dx_j.
\]
Applying then formula ([8] III 9 R3) to the situation $(B\hookrightarrow Z\to S)$, $(s_1,\ldots,s_p)=(y_1-F_1,\ldots,y_n-F_n)$, $(t_1,\ldots,t_n)=(x_1-y_1,\ldots,x_n-y_n)$, $\omega=(\operatorname{Tr}(u)dx_1\cdots dx_n)$, one obtains
\begin{equation}
\tag{*}\label{sga5:III:equation:unnumbered:2}
\langle u,1\rangle_z=
\operatorname{Res}_x\left(\operatorname{Tr}(u)\frac{dx_1\cdots dx_n}{(x_1-F_1)\cdots(x_n-F_n)}\right).
\end{equation}
Since $x$ is transverse, the $x_i-F_i$ form a regular system of parameters of $\OO_{X,x}$, and hence one may write, near $x$,
\[
x_i=\sum_j(x_j-F_j)c_{ij},
\]
where $(c_{ij})$ is a matrix of sections of $\OO_X$ invertible at $x$, whose determinant has value at $x$ equal to $\det(1-dF_x)^{-1}$. Thanks to ([8] III 9 R1), one therefore deduces from (*) that
\[
\langle u,1\rangle_z=\operatorname{Res}_x\left(\operatorname{Tr}(u)\det(c_{ij})\frac{dx_1\cdots dx_n}{x_1\cdots x_n}\right),
\]
whence, by ([8] III 9 R6),
\[
\langle u,1\rangle_z=\operatorname{Tr}(u(x))\det(c_{ij}(x))=\operatorname{Tr}(u(x))\det(1-dF_x)^{-1}.
\]
The corollary therefore follows from \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.11.1]{6.11.1}.
\end{statement}

\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:remark:6.12.1}\begin{statement}{Remark 6.12.1}
If $X^F$ is assumed only finite, the preceding calculation shows that one has
\[
\sum_i(-1)^i\operatorname{Tr}((F,u),H^i(X,L))=
\sum_{x\in X^F}\operatorname{Res}_x\left(\operatorname{Tr}(u)\frac{dx_1\cdots dx_n}{(x_1-F_1)\cdots(x_n-F_n)}\right),
\]
where $(x_i)$ is a system of local coordinates at $x$, and the $F_i$ are the coordinates of $F$ (near $x$) in this system.

The reader will convince himself, moreover, that the preceding formula is still valid if $X$ is proper and is assumed only to be smooth at the points of $X^F$.
\end{statement}

\subsection*{6.13}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:subsection:6.13}
Assume that $S$ is the spectrum of an algebraic closure of the finite field $\mathbb F_q$ and that $X/S$ is obtained by extension of scalars from a proper smooth scheme $X_0/\mathbb F_q$. Take for $F$ the Frobenius endomorphism of $X/S$ ``defined by raising coordinates to the $q$-th power''. One has $X^F=X_0(\mathbb F_q)$, and $X^F$ consists of transverse points since $dF=0$. If $L$ is a locally free sheaf of finite type on $X$ and $u\in\Hom(F^*L,L)$, the Woods-Hole formula is therefore written as
\begin{equation}
\tag{6.13.1}\label{sga5:III:equation:6.13.1}
\sum_i(-1)^i\operatorname{Tr}((F,u),H^i(X,L))=
\sum_{x\in X_0(\mathbb F_q)}\operatorname{Tr}(u_x,L_x).
\end{equation}
In particular, for $L=\OO_X$, and $u$ the canonical isomorphism $F^*\OO_X\xrightarrow{\sim}\OO_X$, one finds:
\begin{equation}
\tag{6.13.2}\label{sga5:III:equation:6.13.2}
\sum_i(-1)^i\operatorname{Tr}(F,H^i(X,\OO_X))=\operatorname{Card}(X_0(\mathbb F_q))\quad(\operatorname{mod} p).
\end{equation}
Artin-Schreier theory makes it possible to rewrite the first member in the form $\sum_i(-1)^i\operatorname{Tr}(F,H^i(X,\mathbb F_p))$, where $H^*(X,\mathbb F_p)$ denotes the étale cohomology of $X$ with values in the constant sheaf $\mathbb F_p$. By a refinement of this argument, Deligne, in (SGA $4^{1/2}$ Fonctions L), deduced from \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.13.1]{6.13.1} the following generalization of \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.13.2]{6.13.2}:


\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:theorem:6.13.3}\begin{statement}{Theorem 6.13.3 (Deligne)}
Let $X_0$ be a separated scheme of finite type over $\mathbb F_q$, let $X$ be the scheme deduced from $X_0$ by extension of scalars to an algebraic closure $k$ of $\mathbb F_q$, and let $F$ be the Frobenius endomorphism of $X/k$. Then, for every $L_0\in\ob D_{\ctf}(X_0,\mathbb F_p)$, if $L$ denotes the inverse image of $L_0$ on $X$, one has
\[
\sum_i(-1)^i\operatorname{Tr}(F,H_c^i(X,L))=
\sum_{x\in X^F}\operatorname{Tr}(F,L_x).
\]
\end{statement}

Let us point out that the particular case of \hyperref[sga5:III:theorem:6.13.3]{6.13.3} corresponding to $L_0=\mathbb F_p$ was established by Katz in (SGA 7 XXII) by a completely different method, based on Dwork's calculation of the zeta function of a hypersurface. Moreover, \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.13.2]{6.13.2} in the proper smooth case is also an immediate consequence of the Lefschetz formula in crystalline cohomology ([5] VII 3.1.9). In fact, crystalline cohomology gives the more precise information that each factor $\det(1-Ft,H^i(X_0/W))\in W[t]$ of the zeta function is congruent mod $p$ to $\det(1-Ft,H^i(X_0,\OO_{X_0}))$ (at least if $H^*(X_0/W)$ is torsion-free). Katz showed [11] that, using estimates of B. Mazur [14], one could obtain higher congruences (involving the $H^i(X_0,\Omega^j_{X_0})$ and the Cartier operator) for smooth hypersurfaces (or complete intersections) for which the Hodge numbers (in the interesting dimension) are assumed to vanish up to a certain level.

On the other hand, if $X_0$ is a proper scheme over $\mathbb F_q$, geometrically integral, and such that $H^i(X_0,\OO_{X_0})=0$ for $i>0$, the Lefschetz-Verdier \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.7.2]{formula 6.7.2}, applied to $X=X_0\otimes k$ and the Frobenius endomorphism of $X$, immediately implies that $X_0$ has at least one rational point over $\mathbb F_q$ (since $\operatorname{Tr}(F,R\Gamma(X,\OO_X))=1$). Serre asks whether this conclusion is still true when one replaces $\mathbb F_q$ by a field satisfying $(C_1)$ ([17] II 3), for example $\mathbb C(t)$ (Tsen), or $\mathbb C((t))$ (Lang) (see loc. cit. for other examples); indeed, he observes that if $X_0$ is a complete intersection of multidegree $(m_1,\ldots,m_r)$ in $\mathbb P^n$, the condition of triviality of cohomology is equivalent ([16] no. 78) to the condition $m_1+\cdots+m_r<n+1$, which is also the condition that occurs when one writes condition $(C_1)$.

\subsection*{6.14}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:subsection:6.14}
The Woods Hole formula has many other applications, for which we refer to [2] and to Beauville's exposé [4].


\subsection*{6.15}\phantomsection\label{sga5:III:subsection:6.15}

The editor has not examined the case of components of fixed points of dimension $\geq1$, but it is not impossible that one could deduce from \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.7.2]{6.7.2} (probably conjugated with Riemann-Roch) Lefschetz-Riemann-Roch formulae in the style of those of Donovan [7], Iversen [10], Nielsen [15] (at least with values in the base field). Nor is it impossible, on the other hand, that the \hyperref[sga5:III:equation:6.7.2]{formula 6.7.2}, applied over the dual numbers, might provide residue formulae for vector fields analogous to those of Bott [6], [3].

\clearpage
\phantomsection\label{sga5:IIIB:expose:iiib}\section*{Exposé III B\\Calculations of Local Terms}
\begin{center}
by Luc Illusie
\end{center}

\section*{Introduction}

This exposé, written in January 1977, does not correspond to any oral exposé of the seminar. It comprises two independent parts. In the first, we compute the Lefschetz--Verdier local terms for cohomological correspondences between smooth curves over an algebraically closed field, supported on correspondences satisfying certain general-position hypotheses. The formula that we obtain gives as corollaries Verdier's formula~[6] for transversal endomorphisms (which applies in particular to the case of the Frobenius endomorphism), and the formula stated by Langlands in ([4]~7.12). The method of proof is parallel to that followed by Artin--Verdier in [6]. One reduces to proving the vanishing of the local term when the given complexes of sheaves have zero fibre at the images of the fixed point under consideration, and in fact one proves a stronger property, namely that a certain restriction map is zero \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:proposition:2.5]{(2.5)}. To establish this property, one first reduces to the tamely ramified case by covers of the same degree of the two given curves (so as not to destroy the general-position hypotheses), and then treats this case directly by means of Abhyankar's lemma and the relative purity theorem. The second part of this exposé, of a much more technical nature, is inspired by the method used by Grothendieck to establish the Lefschetz formula for certain cohomological correspondences on curves (see XII and (SGA~$4^{1/2}$ Rapport)). In no.~5, for complexes of modules on a topos, we develop a theory of non-commutative traces which generalizes the commutative theory of (SGA~6~I) and, in the case of a punctual topos, recovers the non-commutative theory developed by Grothendieck in the oral seminar (this was written up in Exposé XI, unfortunately lost). We apply the techniques of no.~5 to define, in no.~6, Lefschetz--Verdier local terms for cohomological correspondences between complexes of modules over rings that are not necessarily commutative. These local terms behave like non-commutative traces and make it possible, in particular when one takes as base ring the algebra of a group, to divide canonically the ordinary local terms attached to equivariant correspondences. As an application, we prove Grothendieck's divisibility conjecture of (\hyperref[sga5:XII:conjecture:4.5]{XII~4.5}), in a more general form. We also show that the local terms defined by Grothendieck in the Lefschetz formula of XII are indeed the Lefschetz--Verdier local terms, and we generalize the formula of (loc. cit.).

I thank P.~Deligne for the useful suggestions he made to me during many discussions; I am particularly grateful to him for having pointed out, and helped me correct, an error in an early proof of \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:theorem:1.2]{Theorem~1.2}.

\section*{Summary}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.84\textwidth}r@{}}
I. Correspondences in general position between curves & 3\\
1. Statement of the theorem and corollaries & 3\\
2. Reduction to a vanishing theorem & 9\\
3. Reduction to the tame case & 15\\
4. End of the proof of \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:theorem:1.2]{1.2} & 20\\
II. Equivariant correspondences & 25\\
5. Non-commutative traces & 25\\
6. Equivariant correspondences and divisibility of local terms & 46
\end{tabular}

\section*{I. Correspondences in General Position between Curves}

In this part, $S$ denotes the spectrum of an algebraically closed field $k$ of characteristic $p$, and $\Lambda$ a noetherian commutative ring killed by an integer prime to $p$. For every scheme $X$ over $S$ we denote by $D(X)$ the derived category of sheaves of $\Lambda$-modules on $X$, and by $D_{\ctf}(X)$ the full subcategory of $D(X)$ formed by complexes of finite tor-dimension and constructible cohomology.

\subsection*{1. Statement of the theorem and corollaries}\phantomsection\label{sga5:IIIB:subsection:1}

\subsubsection*{1.1}\phantomsection\label{sga5:IIIB:subsubsection:1.1}
Let $X$ (resp. $Y$) be the strict localization, at a closed point, of a smooth separated $S$-curve, let $A$ (resp. $B$) be a strictly local $S$-scheme, and let
\[
a:A\longrightarrow X\times_S Y,
\qquad
b:B\longrightarrow X\times_S Y
\]
be $S$-morphisms. We denote by $p_1:X\times_S Y\to X$, $p_2:X\times_S Y\to Y$ the projections, put $a_i=p_i a$, $b_i=p_i b$, and denote by $x$ (resp. $y$) the closed point of $X$ (resp. $Y$). We assume that $a_2$ and $b_1$ are finite and surjective, and that $A\times_{(X\times_S Y)}B$ is reduced to the closed point $z$ whose image in $X\times_S Y$ is $(x,y)$.

Let $L\in \ob D_{\ctf}(X)$, $M\in \ob D_{\ctf}(Y)$, $u\in\Hom(a_1^*L,a_2^!M)$, and $v\in\Hom(b_2^*M,b_1^!L)$. Denote by
\[
u_z\in\Hom(L_x,M_y)\qquad (\text{resp. }v_z\in\Hom(M_y,L_x))
\]
the composite homomorphism
\[
L_x=R\Gamma(X,L)\xrightarrow{a_1^*}R\Gamma(A,a_1^*L)
\xrightarrow{R\Gamma(A,u)}R\Gamma(A,a_2^!M)
\xrightarrow{a_{2*}}R\Gamma(Y,M)=M_y,
\]
where $a_{2*}$ is defined by the adjunction map $a_{2*}a_2^!M\to M$ (resp. the analogous composite homomorphism defined by means of $(b,v)$). Since $L_x$ and $M_y$ are perfect complexes of $\Lambda$-modules, one can form the cup-product (SGA~6~I~8.3)
\begin{equation}\tag{1.1.1}\label{sga5:IIIB:equation:1.1.1}
\langle u_z,v_z\rangle=\Tr(u_zv_z)=\Tr(v_zu_z)\in\Lambda.
\end{equation}
Put $\Lambda_X(1)[2]=K_X$, $\Lambda_Y(1)[2]=K_Y$. By (I~5) (or (SGA~$4^{1/2}$ Duality~1, or Finitude~4.1)) the functor $D_X=\RHom(-,K_X)$ (resp. $D_Y=\RHom(-,K_Y)$) on $D_{\ctf}(X)$ (resp. $D_{\ctf}(Y)$) is dualizing, i.e. for every $E\in\ob D_{\ctf}(X)$ (resp. $E\in\ob D_{\ctf}(Y)$), one has $E\xrightarrow{\sim}D_XD_XE$ (resp. $E\xrightarrow{\sim}D_YD_YE$). We shall sometimes write $D$ instead of $D_X$, $D_Y$.

On the other hand, denote by $p_i^!$ the functor $p_i^*(-)(1)[2]$. We have trivially
\[
K_X\otimes_S^{\LL}M\xrightarrow{\sim}p_2^!M,
\qquad
L\otimes_S^{\LL}K_Y\xrightarrow{\sim}p_1^!L,
\]
and it follows from (\hyperref[sga5:III:proposition:2.3]{III~2.3}), by passage to the limit, that the Künneth maps analogous to (\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.1.1]{III~3.1.1})
\[
DL\otimes_S^{\LL}M\longrightarrow \RHom(p_1^*L,p_2^!M),
\qquad
L\otimes_S^{\LL}DM\longrightarrow \RHom(p_2^*M,p_1^!L)
\]
are isomorphisms. Since $a_2$ is finite, $a$ is finite, and hence the functor $a^!$ is defined; one verifies easily that one has the transitivity isomorphism $a^!p_2^!=a_2^!$. By the induction formula for $a$ (SGA~4~XVIII~3.1.12.2), one therefore has a canonical isomorphism analogous to (\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.2.1]{III~3.2.1})
\[
a^!\RHom(p_1^*L,p_2^!M)\xrightarrow{\sim}\RHom(a_1^*L,a_2^!M),
\]
whence an isomorphism
\begin{equation}\tag{*}\label{sga5:IIIB:equation:unnumbered}
\Hom(a_1^*L,a_2^!M)\xrightarrow{\sim}H^0(X\times_S Y,a_*a^!(DL\otimes_S^{\LL}M)).
\end{equation}
Likewise one has an isomorphism
\begin{equation}\tag{**}\label{sga5:IIIB:equation:unnumbered:2}
\Hom(b_2^*M,b_1^!L)\xrightarrow{\sim}H^0(X\times_S Y,b_*b^!(L\otimes_S^{\LL}DM)).
\end{equation}
As in (\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.1.2]{III~4.1.2}), one defines a natural pairing
\[
(DL\otimes_S^{\LL}M)\otimes^{\LL}(L\otimes_S^{\LL}DM)
\longrightarrow K_X\otimes_S^{\LL}K_Y=\Lambda_{X\times_S Y}(2)[4]\stackrel{\mathrm{dfn}}{=}K_{X\times_S Y}.
\]
Composing with the pairing analogous to (\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.2.3]{III~4.2.3})
\[
a_*a^!P\otimes^{\LL} b_*b^!Q\longrightarrow c_*c^!(P\otimes^{\LL}Q),
\]
where $P=DL\otimes_S^{\LL}M$, $Q=L\otimes_S^{\LL}DM$, and $c:Z=A\times_{(X\times_S Y)}B\to X\times_S Y$ is the canonical projection, and taking into account the isomorphisms (*), (**), one deduces a pairing analogous to (\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.2.5]{III~4.2.5})
\[
\langle\ ,\ \rangle:\Hom(a_1^*L,a_2^!M)\otimes\Hom(b_2^*M,b_1^!L)
\longrightarrow H^0(X\times_S Y,c_*c^!K_{X\times_S Y}).
\]
One verifies easily, by passage to the limit, that $c^!K_{X\times_S Y}=\Lambda_z$, so that $H^0(X\times_S Y,c_*c^!K_{X\times_S Y})=\Lambda$. Thus one has a ``cup-product''
\begin{equation}\tag{1.1.2}\label{sga5:IIIB:equation:1.1.2}
\langle u,v\rangle\in\Lambda.
\end{equation}

\subsubsection*{1.1.3}\phantomsection\label{sga5:IIIB:subsubsection:1.1.3}
Let $X'$ (resp. $Y'$) be a smooth $S$-curve, $A'$ (resp. $B'$) a separated $S$-curve,
\[
a':A'\longrightarrow X'\times_S Y',
\qquad
b':B'\longrightarrow X'\times_S Y'
\]
finite $S$-morphisms, $x'$ (resp. $y'$) a closed point of $X'$ (resp. $Y'$), $z'$ an isolated closed point of $A'\times_{(X'\times_S Y')}B'$, with image $s'$ (resp. $t'$) in $A'$ (resp. $B'$), and such that $a'_2(=p_2a')$ (resp. $b'_1(=p_1b')$) is quasi-finite at $s'$ (resp. $t'$),
\[
L'\in\ob D_{\ctf}(X'),\quad M'\in\ob D_{\ctf}(Y'),\quad
u'\in\Hom(a_1'^*L',a_2'^!M'),\quad v'\in\Hom(b_2'^*M',b_1'^!L').
\]
One can then consider the local term (\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.2.7]{III~4.2.7}) $\langle u',v'\rangle_{z'}\in\Lambda$. On the other hand, if $X,Y,A,B$ are the strict localizations of $X',Y',A',B'$ at $x',y',s',t'$, if $a:A\to X\times_S Y$, $b:B\to X\times_SY$ are the morphisms deduced from $a'$, $b'$, if $L=L'|X$, $M=M'|Y$, and if $u\in\Hom(a_1^*L,a_2^!M)$, $v\in\Hom(b_2^*M,b_1^!L)$ are the maps deduced from $u'$, $v'$, then the data $(X,Y,a,b,u,v)$ satisfy the hypotheses at the beginning of \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:subsubsection:1.1]{1.1}, and hence a cup-product \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:equation:1.1.2]{1.1.2} $\langle u,v\rangle\in\Lambda$ is defined. It follows from the compatibility of the formation of local terms (\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.2.7]{III~4.2.7}) with localization (\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.2.6]{III~4.2.6}) that $\langle u',v'\rangle_{z'}=\langle u,v\rangle$. It is also easy to see that any data $(X,Y,a,b,u,v)$ satisfying the hypotheses at the beginning of \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:subsubsection:1.1]{1.1} are induced by data $(X',Y',a',b',x',y',z',s',t',u',v')$ as above, where one may also assume, if desired, that $A'\times_{(X'\times_S Y')}B'$ is reduced to $z'$.

In general the cup-products \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:equation:1.1.1]{1.1.1} and \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:equation:1.1.2]{1.1.2} are unequal. Nevertheless one has the following theorem, which is the main result of this first part:

\phantomsection\label{sga5:IIIB:theorem:1.2}\begin{statement}{Theorem 1.2}
Under the hypotheses at the beginning of \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:subsubsection:1.1]{1.1}, assume moreover that $A$ and $B$ are in general position, i.e. that the intersection of the tangent cones at $(x,y)$ to the images of $A$ and $B$ in $X\times_S Y$ is reduced to $(x,y)$, and that likewise $A$ and $X\times\{y\}$ are in general position, as are $\{x\}\times Y$ and $B$. Then
\begin{equation}\tag{1.2.1}\label{sga5:IIIB:equation:1.2.1}
\langle u_z,v_z\rangle=\langle u,v\rangle.
\end{equation}
\end{statement}

By the Lefschetz--Verdier formula (\hyperref[sga5:III:corollary:4.7]{III~4.7}), taking \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:subsubsection:1.1.3]{1.1.3} into account, one deduces:

\phantomsection\label{sga5:IIIB:corollary:1.3}\begin{statement}{Corollary 1.3}
Let $F$ be an endomorphism of a proper smooth curve $X/S$ such that the fixed-point scheme $X^F$ is finite and formed of points where the graph of $F$ is transverse to the diagonal. Let $L\in\ob D_{\ctf}(X)$, and $u\in\Hom(F^*L,L)$. Then
\[
\Tr((F,u),R\Gamma(X,L))=\sum_{x\in X^F}\Tr(u_x,L_x),
\]
where the trace on both sides is taken in the sense of (SGA~6~I~8).
\end{statement}

For $L$ concentrated in degree $0$, \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:corollary:1.3]{1.3} is the result of Artin--Verdier ([6]~4.1). By a passage to the limit explained in XV, one deduces from \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:corollary:1.3]{1.3} an analogous result in $\ell$-adic cohomology ([6]~1.1), (XV~\S2 no.~3 prop.~2):

\phantomsection\label{sga5:IIIB:corollary:1.3.1}\begin{statement}{Corollary 1.3.1}
With $X$ and $F$ as in \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:corollary:1.3]{1.3}, let $L$ be a constructible $\mathbb Q_\ell$-sheaf on $X$ ($\ell$ prime to $p$), and let $u\in\Hom(F^*L,L)$. Then:
\[
\sum_i(-1)^i\Tr((F,u),H^i(X,L))=\sum_{x\in X^F}\Tr(u_x,L_x).
\]
\end{statement}

The hypotheses of \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:corollary:1.3]{1.3} and \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:corollary:1.3.1]{1.3.1} apply in particular to the case where $(F,u)$ is a Frobenius correspondence (XV~2), whence Grothendieck's trace formula ([2]~5.1), (SGA~$4^{1/2}$ Rapport~3.2).

\subsubsection*{1.4}\phantomsection\label{sga5:IIIB:subsubsection:1.4}
With the notation of \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:subsubsection:1.1]{1.1}, assume that $b_2$ and $a_1$ are finite and surjective (instead of $a_2$ and $b_1$), and consider the dual correspondences (\hyperref[sga5:III:subsection:5.1]{III~5.1})
\[
Du\in\Hom(a_2^*DM,a_1^!DL),\qquad Dv\in\Hom(b_1^*DL,b_2^!DM),
\]
and the homomorphisms $(Du)_z\in\Hom((DM)_y,(DM)_x)$, $(Dv)_z\in\Hom((DL)_x,(DM)_y)$. Assume that $(A,\{x\}\times Y)$ is in general position, as are $(X\times\{y\},B)$ and $(A,B)$. Applying \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:theorem:1.2]{1.2} to $Du,Dv$, one obtains
\[
\langle Du,Dv\rangle=\langle (Du)_z,(Dv)_z\rangle,
\]
whence, by (\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:5.1.6]{III~5.1.6}) and (\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.2.6]{III~4.2.6}),
\begin{equation}\tag{1.4.1}\label{sga5:IIIB:equation:1.4.1}
\langle u,v\rangle=\langle (Du)_z,(Dv)_z\rangle.
\end{equation}

From this remark and from \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:theorem:1.2]{1.2} one deduces, by the Lefschetz--Verdier formula (\hyperref[sga5:III:corollary:4.7]{III~4.7}):

\phantomsection\label{sga5:IIIB:corollary:1.5}\begin{statement}{Corollary 1.5}
Let $X,Y$ be proper smooth curves over $S$,
\[
a:A\longrightarrow X\times_S Y,
\qquad
b:B\longrightarrow X\times_S Y
\]
correspondences where $A$ and $B$ are proper curves over $S$, let $L\in\ob D_{\ctf}(X)$, $M\in\ob D_{\ctf}(Y)$, $u\in\Hom(a_1^*L,a_2^!M)$, and $v\in\Hom(b_2^*M,b_1^!L)$. Assume that $C=A\times_{(X\times_S Y)}B$ is finite over $S$ and of the form $C=C_1\amalg C_2$, with the following properties: for $z\in C_1$, the morphisms deduced from $a_2,b_1$ by strict localization at the images of $z$ are finite, and, if $(x,y)$ denotes the image of $z$ in $X\times_S Y$, each of the pairs $(A,B)$, $(A,X\times\{y\})$, $(\{y\}\times Y,B)$ is in general position at $(x,y)$; for $z\in C_2$, the morphisms deduced from $a_1,b_2$ by strict localization at the images of $z$ are finite, and each of the pairs $(A,B)$, $(A,\{x\}\times Y)$, $(B,X\times\{y\})$ is in general position at $(x,y)$ (``general position'' meaning that the intersection of the tangent cones at $(x,y)$ to the images of the curves of the pair under consideration is reduced to $(x,y)$). Then, if $u_*\in\Hom(R\Gamma(X,L),R\Gamma(Y,M))$, $v_*\in\Hom(R\Gamma(Y,M),R\Gamma(X,L))$ are the morphisms defined by $u,v$ (\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.7.6]{III~3.7.6}), one has, with the notation of \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:equation:1.1.1]{1.1.1}, (\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:5.1.5]{III~5.1.5}),
\begin{equation}\tag{1.5.1}\label{sga5:IIIB:equation:1.5.1}
\langle u_*,v_*\rangle=\sum_{z\in C_1}\langle u_z,v_z\rangle+\sum_{z\in C_2}\langle (Du)_z,(Dv)_z\rangle.
\end{equation}
\end{statement}

\subsubsection*{1.6}\phantomsection\label{sga5:IIIB:subsubsection:1.6}
Assuming the formalism of $\ell$-adic cohomology, one deduces from \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:corollary:1.5]{1.5}, by the passage-to-the-limit technique of XIV, that, under the hypotheses of \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:corollary:1.5]{1.5} on $X,Y,a,b$, for $L\in\ob D^b(X,\mathbb Q_\ell)$ ($\ell$ prime $\ne p$), $M\in\ob D^b(Y,\mathbb Q_\ell)$, $u\in\Hom(a_1^*L,a_2^!M)$, $v\in\Hom(b_2^*M,b_1^!L)$, one has the analogue of \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:equation:1.5.1]{1.5.1} in $\mathbb Q_\ell$. This formula contains, as a special case, Proposition 7.12 of Langlands [4]: apply the formula with $X=Y$, $M$ the sheaf $F$ considered by Langlands; $L=\alpha M$ ($\alpha\in\mathbb Q_\ell^*$), $A$ the diagonal of $X\times X$, $b$ the correspondence $\phi$ of (loc. cit.), $u$ the correspondence supported on $A$ given by $\alpha^{-1}:L\to M$, $v$ the correspondence $\Phi$ of (loc. cit.); with the notation of (loc. cit.), $C_1$ (resp. $C_2$) is the set of fixed points where $a<d$ (resp. $a>d$).

\subsubsection*{1.7}\phantomsection\label{sga5:IIIB:subsubsection:1.7}
Under the hypotheses of \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:theorem:1.2]{1.2}, take for $u$ (resp. $v$) the correspondence $\cl(A)$ (resp. $\cl(B)$) defined by $\Tr_{a_2}:a_{2*}\Lambda\to\Lambda$ (resp. $\Tr_{b_1}:b_{1*}\Lambda\to\Lambda$). \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:equation:1.2.1]{Formula 1.2.1} becomes
\begin{equation}\tag{1.7.1}\label{sga5:IIIB:equation:1.7.1}
\deg(a_2)\deg(b_1)=\langle \cl(A),\cl(B)\rangle,
\end{equation}
where $\deg(a_2)$ (resp. $\deg(b_1)$) denotes the generic degree of $a_2$ (resp. $b_1$). Taking into account the hypotheses of general position, the left hand side of \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:equation:1.7.1]{1.7.1} is none other than the intersection multiplicity at $(x,y)$ of the cycles $a_*A$, $b_*B$, the direct images of $A$ and $B$. It is not difficult to deduce \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:equation:1.7.1]{1.7.1} directly from the compatibility of the formation of the cohomology class associated with a cycle with intersections (SGA~$4^{1/2}$ Cycle~2.3.8).


\subsection*{2. Reduction to a vanishing theorem}\phantomsection\label{sga5:IIIB:subsection:2}

\subsubsection*{2.1}\phantomsection\label{sga5:IIIB:subsubsection:2.1}
Under the hypotheses of \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:subsubsection:1.1]{1.1}, let $s$ (resp. $t$) denote the image of $z$ in $A$ (resp. $B$), and let $i:U=X-\{x\}\hookrightarrow X$ and $j:V=Y-\{y\}\hookrightarrow Y$ be the canonical inclusions. Since $a_2$ and $b_1$ are finite, one has $a_2^{-1}(y)=s$, $b_1^{-1}(x)=t$, hence
\[
a_1^{-1}(U)\subset a_2^{-1}(V),\qquad b_2^{-1}(V)\subset b_1^{-1}(U),
\]
and there are commutative diagrams
\[
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
U \arrow[d,"i"] \arrow[rd,phantom,"(1)"{description}] & a_1^{-1}(U) \arrow[l,"a'_1"'] \arrow[r,"a'_2"] \arrow[d] \arrow[rd,phantom,"(2)"{description}] & V \arrow[d,"j"]\\
X & A \arrow[l,"a_1"] \arrow[r,"a_2"'] & Y
\end{tikzcd}
\qquad
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
U \arrow[d,"i"] \arrow[rd,phantom,"(3)"{description}] & b_2^{-1}(V) \arrow[l,"b'_1"'] \arrow[r,"b'_2"] \arrow[d] \arrow[rd,phantom,"(4)"{description}] & V \arrow[d,"j"]\\
X & B \arrow[l,"b_1"] \arrow[r,"b_2"'] & Y ,
\end{tikzcd}
\]
where squares (1) and (4) are cartesian.

\phantomsection\label{sga5:IIIB:lemma:2.2}\begin{statement}{Lemma 2.2}
To prove \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:theorem:1.2]{1.2}, it is enough to consider the following two cases:
\[
\text{a) } L_x=0,\quad M_y=0;\qquad
\text{b) } L|U=0,\quad M|V=0 .
\]
\end{statement}

Consider the decreasing filtrations on $L$ and $M$ defined by the subcomplexes
\[
F^nL=
\begin{cases}
L,& n\le 0,\\
i_!i^*L,& n=1,\\
0,& n>1,
\end{cases}
\qquad
F^nM=
\begin{cases}
M,& n\le 0,\\
j_!j^*M,& n=1,\\
0,& n>1.
\end{cases}
\]
The complex $a_{2*}a_1^*L$ (resp. $b_{1*}b_2^*M$) is then filtered by
\[
F^n a_{2*}a_1^*L=a_{2*}a_1^*F^nL.
\]
We have $F^n a_{2*}a_1^*L=a_{2*}a_1^*L$ for $n\le0$, and $0$ for $n>1$; moreover, since $a_2$ is finite and square (1) is cartesian,
\[
F^1a_{2*}a_1^*L=j_!(a'_2)_!(a'_1)^*i^*L.
\]
Similarly,
\[
F^n b_{1*}b_2^*M=
\begin{cases}
b_{1*}b_2^*M,& n\le 0,\\
i_!(b'_1)_!(b'_2)^*j^*M,& n=1,\\
0,& n>1.
\end{cases}
\]

We may suppose that $u$ (resp. $v$) is defined by a homomorphism of complexes
\[
u:a_{2*}a_1^*L\longrightarrow M
\quad
(\text{resp. }v:b_{1*}b_2^*M\longrightarrow L).
\]
The preceding formulas show that the composite
\[
F^1a_{2*}a_1^*L\longrightarrow a_{2*}a_1^*L\xrightarrow{u}M
\]
(resp. $F^1b_{1*}b_2^*M\to b_{1*}b_2^*M\xrightarrow{v}L$) factors uniquely through $F^1M$ (resp. $F^1L$); in other words, $u$ (resp. $v$) is a homomorphism of filtered complexes. Thus $u$ (resp. $v$) underlies a filtered correspondence, in the sense of (\hyperref[sga5:III:statement:4.13]{III 4.13}), which we again denote by
\[
u\in\Hom_{DF}(a_1^*L,a_2^!M),\qquad
v\in\Hom_{DF}(b_2^*M,b_1^!L).
\]
By additivity of cup-products ([3] V 3.8.7), (\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.13.1]{III 4.13.1}), one has
\[
\langle u_z,v_z\rangle
 =\langle \operatorname{gr}^0u_z,\operatorname{gr}^0v_z\rangle
 +\langle \operatorname{gr}^1u_z,\operatorname{gr}^1v_z\rangle,
\]
and
\[
\langle u,v\rangle
 =\langle \operatorname{gr}^0u,\operatorname{gr}^0v\rangle
 +\langle \operatorname{gr}^1u,\operatorname{gr}^1v\rangle.
\]
The lemma follows, since $\operatorname{gr}^1L=i_!i^*L$ (resp. $\operatorname{gr}^1M=j_!j^*M$) has zero fibre at $x$ (resp. $y$), whereas $\operatorname{gr}^0L$ (resp. $\operatorname{gr}^0M$) is concentrated at $x$ (resp. $y$).

\phantomsection\label{sga5:IIIB:lemma:2.3}\begin{statement}{Lemma 2.3}
Under the hypotheses of \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:subsubsection:1.1]{1.1}, suppose that $L|U=0$ and $M|V=0$. Then
\[
\langle u_z,v_z\rangle=\langle u,v\rangle .
\]
\end{statement}

The idea of the proof is that then $u$ and $v$ are direct images of correspondences between complexes concentrated at the closed points, and that the formula to be proved merely expresses the Lefschetz formula for the projections $x\to S$, $y\to S$. In view of \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:subsubsection:1.1.3]{1.1.3}, the data of \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:subsubsection:1.1]{1.1} may be replaced by the following data: $X$ (resp. $Y$) is a smooth $S$-curve endowed with a closed point $x$ (resp. $y$), $A$ (resp. $B$) is a separated $S$-curve, $a:A\to X\times_S Y$, $b:B\to X\times_S Y$ are finite $S$-morphisms such that $A\times_{X\times_SY}B$ is reduced to a closed point $z$ above $(x,y)$,
\[
u\in\Hom(a_1^*L,a_2^!M),\qquad v\in\Hom(b_2^*M,b_1^!L),
\]
with
\[
L=h_{1*}L',\quad M=h_{2*}M',\quad
L'\in\Ob D_{\mathrm{ctf}}(\{x\}),\quad M'\in\Ob D_{\mathrm{ctf}}(\{y\}),
\]
where $h_1:\{x\}\to X$ and $h_2:\{y\}\to Y$ are the canonical inclusions. We must show that
\[
\langle u_z,v_z\rangle=\langle u,v\rangle_z,
\]
where
\[
u_z\in\Hom(\mathrm R\Gamma(X,L),\mathrm R\Gamma(Y,M)),\qquad
v_z\in\Hom(\mathrm R\Gamma(Y,M),\mathrm R\Gamma(X,L))
\]
are defined as in \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:subsubsection:1.1]{1.1}, and $\langle u,v\rangle_z$ is the value at $z$ of the cup-product (\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.2.7]{III 4.2.7}). We have $\mathrm R\Gamma(X,L)=\mathrm R\Gamma_c(X,L)=L_x$, $\mathrm R\Gamma(Y,M)=\mathrm R\Gamma_c(Y,M)=M_y$, and $u_z$ (resp. $v_z$) is nothing other than the direct image $u_*$ (resp. $v_*$) of $u$ (resp. $v$) on $S$ in the sense of (\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.7.6]{III 3.7.6}), as is shown by the calculation procedure (\hyperref[sga5:III:corollary:3.5]{III 3.5 b)}).

On the other hand, if $h=h_1\times_Sh_2:(x,y)\to X\times_SY$ is the inclusion, and if $a':A'\to(x,y)$, $b':B'\to(x,y)$ denote the fibres of $a$, $b$ at $(x,y)$, each reduced to a point, then there are canonical isomorphisms
\[
h_*a'_*a'{}^!(DL_x\lten_{\Lambda}M_y)
 \xrightarrow{\sim} a_*a^!(DL\lten_{\Lambda}M),
\]
\[
h_*b'_*b'{}^!(L_x\lten_{\Lambda}DM_y)
 \xrightarrow{\sim} b_*b^!(L\lten_{\Lambda}DM),
\]
and the arrows
\[
h_*:\Hom(a_1'{}^*L_x,a_2'{}^!M_y)\to \Hom(a_1^*L,a_2^!M),
\]
\[
h_*:\Hom(b_2'{}^*M_y,b_1'{}^!L_x)\to \Hom(b_2^*M,b_1^!L)
\]
(\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:3.7.6]{III 3.7.6}) are isomorphisms. The correspondences $u$ and $v$ are therefore written uniquely as
\begin{equation}\tag{1}\label{sga5:IIIB:equation:1}
u=h_*u',\qquad v=h_*v',
\end{equation}
with
\[
u'\in\Hom(a_1'{}^*L_x,a_2'{}^!M_y),\qquad
v'\in\Hom(b_2'{}^*M_y,b_1'{}^!L_x).
\]
By the Lefschetz formula (\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.5.1]{III 4.5.1}) for $(h_1,h_2,A'\to A,B'\to B)$, one has
\begin{equation}\tag{2}\label{sga5:IIIB:equation:2}
\langle u,v\rangle_z=\langle u',v'\rangle_z .
\end{equation}
On the other hand, by (1), $u_*=u'_*$ and $v_*=v'_*$; hence, by the Lefschetz formula for $\{x\}\to S$, $\{y\}\to S$, one has
\begin{equation}\tag{3}\label{sga5:IIIB:equation:3}
\langle u_*,v_*\rangle=\langle u',v'\rangle_z .
\end{equation}
In view of the preceding remark, the conclusion follows by comparing (2) and (3).

\phantomsection\label{sga5:IIIB:lemma:2.4}\begin{statement}{Lemma 2.4}
To prove \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:theorem:1.2]{1.2}, one may suppose that $a$ and $b$ are closed immersions, and it is enough to prove that, if $L_x=0$ and $M_y=0$, the restriction map
\begin{equation}
H_A^0(X\times_SY,DL\lten_SM)
 \longrightarrow H_z^0(B,b^*(DL\lten_SM))
\tag{2.4.1}\label{sga5:IIIB:equation:2.4.1}
\end{equation}
is zero.
\end{statement}

Put $X\times_SY=Z$. The closed subschemes $a':A'\to Z$, $b':B'\to Z$, images of $a$ and $b$, satisfy the same hypotheses as $a$ and $b$. Let $u'$ (resp. $v'$) be the correspondence supported on $A'$ (resp. $B'$) which is the direct image of $u$ (resp. $v$) by the arrow deduced from the adjunction arrow $f_*f^!\to1$ (resp. $g_*g^!\to1$), where $f:A\to A'$ (resp. $g:B\to B'$) is the projection. It is immediate that $u_z=u'_z$ and $v_z=v'_z$. On the other hand, it follows easily from the definition of the cup-product \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:equation:1.1.2]{1.1.2} that
\[
\langle u,v\rangle=\langle u',v'\rangle,
\]
hence the first assertion.

By \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:lemma:2.2]{2.2} and \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:lemma:2.3]{2.3}, one may suppose that $L_x=0$ and $M_y=0$, and the conclusion of \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:theorem:1.2]{1.2} then becomes $\langle u,v\rangle=0$. Let $P,Q\in\Ob D_{\mathrm{ctf}}(Z)$. The cup-product
\[
H_A^0(Z,P)\otimes H_B^0(Z,Q)\longrightarrow H_z^0(Z,P\lten Q)
\]
decomposes as
\[
H_A^0(Z,P)\otimes H_B^0(Z,Q)
 \xrightarrow{\,b^*\otimes 1\,}
H_z^0(B,b^*P)\otimes H_B^0(Z,Q)
 \longrightarrow H_z^0(Z,P\lten Q),
\]
where $b^*$ is the restriction and the second arrow, a variant of the product considered in (SGA $4^{1/2}$ Cycle 1.2.1), is derived from the evident product at the level of sections of sheaves. This assertion is easily checked, for example by means of the flasque resolutions of (SGA 4 XVII 4.2). If $a':\{z\}\to B$, $b':\{z\}\to A$, $c:\{z\}\to Z$ are the inclusions, one may also invoke the standard decomposition of the Künneth arrow (\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.2.1]{III 4.2.1})
\[
a'{}^!P\lten_Z b'{}^!Q\longrightarrow c^!(P\lten Q)
\]
into a ``base-change arrow''
\[
a'{}^!P\lten_Z b'{}^!Q\longrightarrow a'{}^!b^*P\lten a^*b'{}^!Q,
\]
followed by ``projection arrows''
\[
a'{}^!b^*P\lten a^*b'{}^!Q
 \longrightarrow a'{}^!(b^*P\lten b'{}^!Q)
 \longrightarrow a'{}^!b'{}^!(P\lten Q)).
\]
Taking $P=DL\lten_SM$, $Q=L\lten_SDM$, and returning to the definition of $\langle u,v\rangle$ (\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:4.2.5]{III 4.2.5}), one sees that it is enough to prove that $b^*u=0$, whence the conclusion.

In view of \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:lemma:2.4]{2.4}, \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:theorem:1.2]{1.2} will therefore follow from the following more precise result.

\phantomsection\label{sga5:IIIB:proposition:2.5}\begin{statement}{Proposition 2.5}
Under the hypotheses of \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:theorem:1.2]{1.2}, suppose that $a$ and $b$ are closed immersions and that $L_x=0$, $M_y=0$. Let $T$ be the strict localization of $X\times_SY$ at $z=(x,y)$, and identify $A$, $B$ with closed subschemes of $T$. Then the restriction arrows
\[
b^*:\mathrm R\Gamma_A(T,(DL\lten_SM)|T)
 \longrightarrow \mathrm R\Gamma_z(B,b^*(DL\lten_SM)),
\]
\[
a^*:\mathrm R\Gamma_B(T,(L\lten_SDM)|T)
 \longrightarrow \mathrm R\Gamma_z(A,a^*(L\lten_SDM))
\]
are zero.
\end{statement}

\subsubsection*{2.6}\phantomsection\label{sga5:IIIB:subsubsection:2.6}
The proof of \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:proposition:2.5]{2.5} will occupy the rest of number 2 and numbers 3 and 4. By symmetry of the statement, it is enough to prove the assertion concerning $b^*$. We shall rewrite it in a form which will be more convenient. First note that
\[
\mathrm R\Gamma(T,(DL\lten_SM)|T)
 =(DL\lten_SM)_z
 =(DL)_x\lten M_y=0,
\]
because $M_y=0$, and similarly
\[
\mathrm R\Gamma(B,b^*(DL\lten_SM))
 =(DL\lten_SM)_z=0.
\]
Consequently, the relative cohomology triangles give isomorphisms
\[
\mathrm R\Gamma(T-A,(DL\lten_SM)|T-A)
 \xrightarrow{\sim}
\mathrm R\Gamma_A(T,(DL\lten_SM)|T)[1],
\]
\[
\mathrm R\Gamma(B-z,b^*(DL\lten_{\Lambda}M)|B-z)
 \xrightarrow{\sim}
\mathrm R\Gamma_z(B,b^*(DL\lten_{\Lambda}M))[1],
\]
under which the arrow $b^*$ of \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:proposition:2.5]{2.5}, shifted by $1$, is identified with the restriction
\begin{equation}
b^*:\mathrm R\Gamma(T-A,(DL\lten_SM)|T-A)
 \longrightarrow \mathrm R\Gamma(B-z,(DL\lten_SM)|B-z).
\tag{2.6.1}\label{sga5:IIIB:equation:2.6.1}
\end{equation}
Since $L_x=0$, $M_y=0$, one may write
\begin{equation}
L=i_!DP,\qquad M=j_!Q,
\tag{2.6.2}\label{sga5:IIIB:equation:2.6.2}
\end{equation}
where $i:X-x\to X$, $j:Y-y\to Y$ are the inclusions, and $P\in\Ob D_{\mathrm{ctf}}(X-x)$, $Q\in\Ob D_{\mathrm{ctf}}(Y-y)$. By the duality isomorphism (SGA 4 XVIII 3.19.6), together with passage to the limit, one has
\[
DL=Ri_*DDP=Ri_*P,
\]
hence
\[
DL\lten_SM=Ri_*P\lten_Sj_!Q.
\]
Put $X\times_SY=Z$, and identify $X\times\{y\}$ and $\{x\}\times Y$ with closed subschemes of $Z$ (resp. $T$), which we shall simply denote by $X$ and $Y$. Let
\[
f':Z-Y\hookrightarrow Z,\qquad
g':Z-(X\cup Y)\hookrightarrow Z-Y,
\]
\[
f:T-Z\hookrightarrow T,\qquad
g:T-(X\cup Y)\hookrightarrow T-Y
\]
be the inclusions. The Künneth formula (\hyperref[sga5:III:equation:1.6.4]{III 1.6.4}) gives, by passage to the limit,
\[
DL\lten_SM=Rf'_*g'_!(P\lten_SQ),
\]
and hence
\begin{equation}
(DL\lten_SM)|T=Rf_*g_!((P\lten_SQ)|T-(X\cup Y)).
\tag{2.6.3}\label{sga5:IIIB:equation:2.6.3}
\end{equation}
With this identification, \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:equation:2.6.1]{2.6.1} becomes
\begin{equation}
b^*:\mathrm R\Gamma(T-(Y\cup A),g_!(E)|T-(Y\cup A))
 \longrightarrow \mathrm R\Gamma(B-z,g_!(E)|B-z),
\tag{2.6.4}\label{sga5:IIIB:equation:2.6.4}
\end{equation}
where
\[
E=(P\lten_SQ)|T-(X\cup Y).
\]
In the next two numbers, we shall show that the arrow \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:equation:2.6.4]{2.6.4} is zero: first we shall reduce to the case where the cohomology sheaves of $E$ have tame ramification, more precisely where the inertia action occurs through an $\ell$-group, for a prime number $\ell\ne p$, and then we shall treat that case directly by means of a purity theorem.

\subsection*{3. Reduction to the tame case}\phantomsection\label{sga5:IIIB:subsection:3}

\subsubsection*{3.1}\phantomsection\label{sga5:IIIB:subsubsection:3.1}
Recall that the base ring $\Lambda$ is annihilated by an integer prime to $p$, hence is a direct product of noetherian rings $\Lambda_i$, each $\Lambda_i$ being annihilated by a power of a prime number $\ell_i\ne p$. For every scheme $S'$ over $S$, the derived category $D(S',\Lambda)$ is the product of the categories $D(S',\Lambda_i)$, so that, in order to prove the vanishing of \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:equation:2.6.4]{2.6.4}, we may suppose that $\Lambda$ is annihilated by a power of a prime number $\ell\ne p$. We may also suppose that $P$ (resp. $Q$) is bounded and has locally free components of finite type.

\subsubsection*{3.2}\phantomsection\label{sga5:IIIB:subsubsection:3.2}
Put $X-x=U$, $Y-y=V$. Let $U''/U$ be a finite Galois covering with group $G$ which trivializes the components of $P$, let $H$ be an $\ell$-Sylow subgroup of $G$, let $U'=U''/H$ be the quotient covering, and let $X'$ be the normalized strictly local trait of $X$ in $U'$. Then $X'/X$ is a finite connected covering of degree $d_1$ prime to $\ell$, and the representation of $\pi_1(U')$ defined by the components of $P'=P|U'$ factors through a finite $\ell$-group. Proceeding similarly with $Q$, one finds a finite connected covering $Y'/Y$ of degree $d_2$ prime to $\ell$, inducing on $V$ an étale covering $V'$ such that the representation of $\pi_1(V')$ defined by the components of $Q'=Q|V'$ factors through a finite $\ell$-group. Let
\[
d=d_1e_1=d_2e_2=\operatorname{lcm}(d_1,d_2).
\]
After adjoining an $e_1$-th root (resp. an $e_2$-th root) of a uniformizer of $X'$ (resp. $Y'$), we may suppose that $X'$ (resp. $Y'$) has the same properties as above, and moreover that $d_1=d_2=d$.

Let $x'$ (resp. $y'$) be the closed point of $X'$ (resp. $Y'$), $T'$ the strict localization of $X'\times_S Y'$ at $(x',y')$, $z'$ the closed point of $T'$, $A'$ (resp. $B'$) the inverse image of $A$ (resp. $B$) in $T'$, and
\[
E'=(P\lten_{\Lambda}Q)|T'-(X'\cup Y')
   =(P'\lten_{\Lambda}Q')|T'-(X'\cup Y').
\]
We then have a commutative square of restriction arrows
\[
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=huge,row sep=large]
\mathrm R\Gamma(T-(Y\cup A),g_!(E)|T-(Y\cup A))
 \arrow[r,"b^*"] \arrow[d]
&
\mathrm R\Gamma(B-z,g_!(E)|B-z) \arrow[d,"(1)"]\\
\mathrm R\Gamma(T'-(Y'\cup A'),g'_!(E')|T'-(Y'\cup A'))
 \arrow[r,"b'{}^*"]
&
\mathrm R\Gamma(B'-z',g'_!(E')|B'-z') ,
\end{tikzcd}
\]
where $b':B'\to T'$, $g':T'-(X'\cup Y')\to T'-Y'$ are the inclusions. The covering $T'/T$ is finite, of degree $d^2$, and étale outside $X\cup Y$, hence induces an étale covering of degree $d^2$ from $B'-z'$ onto $B-z$. The composite of (1) and the trace morphism
\[
\mathrm R\Gamma(B'-z',g'_!(E')|B'-z')
 \longrightarrow
\mathrm R\Gamma(B-z,g_!(E)|B-z)
\]
is therefore multiplication by $d^2$, which is an isomorphism, since $\ell$ does not divide $d$. To prove that the arrow \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:equation:2.6.4]{2.6.4} is zero, it is therefore enough to prove that $b'{}^*=0$. We are thus reduced to the case where the representation of $\pi_1(U)$ (resp. $\pi_1(V)$) defined by the components of $P$ (resp. $Q$) factors through a finite $\ell$-group, provided one ensures that $A'$, $B'$ satisfy the general-position hypotheses of \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:theorem:1.2]{1.2}. This will follow from the following lemma.

\phantomsection\label{sga5:IIIB:lemma:3.3}\begin{statement}{Lemma 3.3}
Under the hypotheses of \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:theorem:1.2]{1.2}, $a$ and $b$ being closed immersions, let $X'/X$, $Y'/Y$ be finite coverings of strictly local $S$-traits, of the same degree $d$. Let $A'$ (resp. $B'$) denote the inverse image of $A$ (resp. $B$) in $X'\times_S Y'$. Then the pairs $(A',B')$, $(A',X')$, $(Y',B')$ are each in general position in the sense of \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:theorem:1.2]{1.2}.\footnote{With the usual abuse of notation $X'=X'\times\{y'\}$, etc. (where $x'$ (resp.\ $y'$) is the closed point of $X'$ (resp.\ $Y'$).}
\end{statement}

The proof of \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:lemma:3.3]{3.3} will be given after a few preliminaries.

\phantomsection\label{sga5:IIIB:lemma:3.4}\begin{statement}{Lemma 3.4}
Let $X\xleftarrow{s_1}W\xrightarrow{s_2}Y$ be finite morphisms of $S$-traits, let $s=(s_1,s_2):W\to X\times_SY$, and let $u,v,w$ be uniformizers of $X,Y,W$ respectively, so that
\[
s_1^*u=aw^m\pmod {w^{m+1}},\qquad
s_2^*v=bw^n\pmod {w^{n+1}},
\]
with $a,b\in k^*$. Then: (i) if $m<n$, $s(W)$ is tangent to $X$ at $z=(x,y)$; (ii) if $m=n$, $s(W)$ is tangent to the subscheme of $X\times_SY$ with equation
\[
ap_2^*(v)-bp_1^*(u)=0.
\]
\end{statement}

Let $Z=X\times_SY$, and let $W'$ be the reduced closed subscheme which is the image of $s$. In the tangent space to $Z$ at $z$, the slope, with respect to the tangent to $X$, of the tangent to $W'$ is the value at the origin of the function induced on $W'$ by $p_2^*(v)/p_1^*(u)$; this is also the value at the origin of the function induced by $p_2^*(v)/p_1^*(u)$ on $W$, and is therefore $0$ (resp. $b/a$) in case (i) (resp. (ii)).

In the situation of \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:lemma:3.4]{3.4}, we shall define the slope of $W$ with respect to $((X,u),(Y,v))$ as the element of $k\cup\{\infty\}$ equal to $0$ if $m<n$, to $\infty$ if $m>n$, and to $b/a$ if $m=n$. This element is independent of the choice of $w$.

\phantomsection\label{sga5:IIIB:lemma:3.5}\begin{statement}{Lemma 3.5}
Let $f:X'\to X$, $g:Y'\to Y$ be finite morphisms of $S$-traits with the same ramification index $d$, and let $u,v,u',v'$ be uniformizers of $X,Y,X',Y'$ respectively. Then there exists $C\in k^*$ such that, for every commutative diagram of finite morphisms of $S$-traits
\[
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
X' & W' \arrow[l,"s'_1"'] \arrow[r,"s'_2"] \arrow[d,"h"] & Y' \arrow[d,"g"]\\
X & W \arrow[l,"s_1"] \arrow[r,"s_2"'] & Y ,
\end{tikzcd}
\]
one has $\lambda=C{\lambda'}^d$, where $\lambda$ (resp. $\lambda'$) is the slope of $W$ (resp. $W'$) with respect to $((X,u),(Y,v))$ (resp. $((X',u'),(Y',v'))$).
\end{statement}

By hypothesis,
\[
f^*u=\alpha u'{}^d\pmod {u'{}^{d+1}},\qquad
g^*v=\beta v'{}^d\pmod {v'{}^{d+1}},
\]
with $\alpha,\beta\in k^*$. We show that $C=\beta/\alpha$ has the required properties. Let $w$ (resp. $w'$) be a uniformizer of $W$ (resp. $W'$). We have
\[
s_1^*u=aw^m\pmod {w^{m+1}},\qquad
s_2^*v=bw^n\pmod {w^{n+1}},
\]
\[
s'_1{}^*u'=a'w'{}^{m'}\pmod {w'{}^{m'+1}},\qquad
s'_2{}^*v'=b'w'{}^{n'}\pmod {w'{}^{n'+1}},
\]
\[
h^*w=\gamma w'{}^r\pmod {w'{}^{r+1}},
\]
with $a,b,a',b',\gamma\in k^*$. Commutativity of the squares in the diagram implies
\[
m'd=rm,\qquad n'd=rn,
\]
hence $m'/n'=m/n$. Suppose first that $m=n$. Then $\lambda=b/a$, $\lambda'=b'/a'$. If $N=m'd=rm=n'd=rn$, the preceding congruences give
\[
(fs'_1)^*u=\alpha a'{}^d w'{}^N\pmod {w'{}^{N+1}},\qquad
(s_1h)^*u=a\gamma^m w'{}^N\pmod {w'{}^{N+1}},
\]
whence $\alpha a'{}^d=a\gamma^m$. Similarly $\beta b'{}^d=b\gamma^m$, whence $\lambda=C{\lambda'}^d$, for $\lambda\ne0,\infty$. But, since $m'/n'=m/n$, $\lambda=0$ (resp. $\infty$) is equivalent to $\lambda'=0$ (resp. $\infty$), which completes the proof.

We now prove \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:lemma:3.3]{3.3}. Let $Z=X\times_SY$, $Z'=X'\times_SY'$, let $\widetilde A$ (resp. $\widetilde B$) be the normalization of $A$ (resp. $B$), and let $\widetilde A'$ (resp. $\widetilde B'$) be the normalization of $A'$ (resp. $B'$). Since $\widetilde A'$ (resp. $\widetilde B'$) is also the normalization of $\widetilde A\times_ZZ'$ (resp. $\widetilde B\times_ZZ'$), we have commutative squares
\begin{equation}\tag{*}\label{sga5:IIIB:equation:unnumbered:3}
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
\widetilde A' \arrow[r] \arrow[d] & Z' \arrow[d] & \widetilde B' \arrow[l] \arrow[d]\\
\widetilde A \arrow[r] & Z & \widetilde B . \arrow[l]
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation}
Let $(x_i)_{1\le i\le m}$ (resp. $(y_j)_{1\le j\le n}$) be the points of $\widetilde A$ (resp. $\widetilde B$) above $x$ (resp. $y$), and let $(x'_i)_{1\le i\le m'}$ (resp. $(y'_j)_{1\le j\le n'}$) be the points of $\widetilde A'$ (resp. $\widetilde B'$) above $x'$ (resp. $y'$). Since $A$ and $B$ are strictly local, we have decompositions
\[
\widetilde A=\coprod_{1\le i\le m}A_i,\qquad
\widetilde B=\coprod_{1\le j\le n}B_j,\qquad
\widetilde A'=\coprod_{1\le i\le m'}A'_i,\qquad
\widetilde B'=\coprod_{1\le j\le n'}B'_j,
\]
where $A_i,B_j,A'_i,B'_j$ are strictly local traits with respective closed points $x_i,y_j,x'_i,y'_j$. Choose uniformizers $u,v,u',v'$ of $X,Y,X',Y'$ respectively; let $\lambda_i$ (resp. $\mu_j$) be the slope of $A_i$ (resp. $B_j$) with respect to $((X,u),(Y,v))$, and let $\lambda'_i$ (resp. $\mu'_j$) be that of $A'_i$ (resp. $B'_j$) with respect to $((X',u'),(Y',v'))$. The general-position hypotheses on $A$ and $B$ are expressed by
\begin{equation}
\lambda_i\ne0\text{ for all }i,\qquad
\mu_j\ne\infty\text{ for all }j,\qquad
\lambda_i\ne\mu_j\text{ for all }i,j,
\tag{1}\label{sga5:IIIB:equation:1:2}
\end{equation}
and we have to show that
\begin{equation}
\lambda'_i\ne0\text{ for all }i,\qquad
\mu'_j\ne\infty\text{ for all }j,\qquad
\lambda'_i\ne\mu'_j\text{ for all }i,j.
\tag{2}\label{sga5:IIIB:equation:2:2}
\end{equation}
Let $i'\in[1,m']$, $j'\in[1,n']$, and let $x_i$ (resp. $y_j$) be the image of $x'_{i'}$ (resp. $y'_{j'}$). The preceding diagram provides commutative squares of finite morphisms of $S$-traits
\[
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
X' & A'_{i'} \arrow[l] \arrow[r] \arrow[d] & Y' \arrow[d]\\
X & A_i \arrow[l] \arrow[r] & Y
\end{tikzcd}
\qquad
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
X' & B'_{j'} \arrow[l] \arrow[r] \arrow[d] & Y' \arrow[d]\\
X & B_j \arrow[l] \arrow[r] & Y .
\end{tikzcd}
\]
By \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:lemma:3.5]{3.5}, there exists $C\in k^*$ such that
\[
\lambda_i=C{\lambda'_{i'}}^d,\qquad
\mu_j=C{\mu'_{j'}}^d.
\]
Thus (1) implies (2), which completes the proof of \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:lemma:3.3]{3.3}.

\subsection*{4. End of the proof of \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:theorem:1.2]{1.2}}\phantomsection\label{sga5:IIIB:subsection:4}

\subsubsection*{4.1}\phantomsection\label{sga5:IIIB:subsubsection:4.1}
To complete the proof of \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:proposition:2.5]{2.5}, hence of \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:theorem:1.2]{1.2}, it remains to establish the vanishing of \hyperref[sga5:IIIB:equation:2.6.4]{2.6.4} under the following additional hypotheses: $\Lambda$ is annihilated by a power of a prime number $\ell\ne p$, and $P$ and $Q$ are bounded, with locally free components of finite type, such that the corresponding representations of the fundamental groups factor through finite $\ell$-groups. We have
\[
g_!(E)|T-(Y\cup A)=g_{1!}(E_1),
\]
where $g_1:T-(X\cup Y\cup A)\hookrightarrow T-(Y\cup A)$ is the inclusion, and
\[
E_1=(P\lten_{\Lambda}Q)|T-(X\cup Y\cup A).
\]
Now $E_1$ is a complex with bounded, constructible, locally constant cohomology such that the corresponding representation of $\pi_1(T-(X\cup Y\cup A))$ factors through an $\ell$-group. The assertion to be proved will therefore follow from the following more general statement.

\phantomsection\label{sga5:IIIB:proposition:4.2}\begin{statement}{Proposition 4.2}
Let $T$ be the strict localization at a closed point of a smooth $S$-scheme of dimension $2$, let $t$ be the closed point of $T$, and let $A$, $B$, $X$ be closed subschemes of dimension $1$ of $T$. Let $i:T-(A\cup X)\hookrightarrow T-A$ be the inclusion. Suppose that $X$ is regular, and that $A$ is in general position with respect to $B$ and $X$; that is, the intersection of the tangent cones at $t$ to $A$ and $B\cup X$ is reduced to $t$. Let $E\in\Ob D^b(T-(A\cup X))$ be such that $H^*(E)$ is constructible and locally constant, and that the corresponding representation of $\pi_1(T-(A\cup X))$ factors through an $\ell$-group. Then the restriction arrow
\[
\mathrm R\Gamma(T-A,i_!(E))
\longrightarrow
\mathrm R\Gamma(B-t,i_!(E)|B-t)
\]
is zero.
\end{statement}

The proof will rely on the following lemma.

\phantomsection\label{sga5:IIIB:lemma:4.3}\begin{statement}{Lemma 4.3}
Let $Z$ be a connected regular noetherian scheme of dimension $2$, with residual characteristics prime to $\ell$, and let $X$, $Y$ be regular divisors meeting transversely at a point $z$, giving a diagram of inclusions
\[
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large,row sep=large]
Z-(X\cup Y) \arrow[r,"i'"] \arrow[d,"j"'] & Z-Y \arrow[d,"j"]\\
Z-X \arrow[r,"i"'] \arrow[d,"m"'] & Z \arrow[d,"m"]\\
Y-z \arrow[r,"i_1"'] & Y .
\end{tikzcd}
\]
Assume the purity hypothesis
\[
(P)\qquad R^qj_*(\mathbb Z/\ell)=0\quad\text{for }q>1.
\]
Let $L\in\Ob D^b(Z-(X\cup Y),\Lambda)$ have constructible locally constant cohomology, with the corresponding representation of $\pi_1(Z-(X\cup Y))$ factoring through an $\ell$-group. Then:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\alph*)]
\item There is a canonical isomorphism
\[
i_{1!}m'^*Rj_*i'_!L\xrightarrow{\sim} m^*Rj_*(i'_!L),
\]
and the complex $m'^*Rj_*i'_!L$ has constructible, locally constant cohomology.
\item Suppose that $Y$ is connected, and let $\bar s$ be a geometric point of $Y$ localized at a closed point $s\ne z$. Then the restriction arrow
\begin{equation}
\mathrm R\Gamma(Y,m^*Rj_*(i'_!L))\longrightarrow (Rj_*(i'_!L))_{\bar s}
\tag{4.3.1}\label{sga5:IIIB:equation:4.3.1}
\end{equation}
is zero.
